Peer Review File


Evolutionary dynamics in the years preceding acute myeloid
leukaemia
Corresponding Author: Professor Jamie Blundell


Any redactions in this file are there to maintain patient confidentiality, the confidentiality of unpublished data, or to remove
third-party material.

This file contains all reviewer reports in order by version, followed by all author rebuttals in order by version.



Version 0:

Reviewer comments:

Referee #1

(Remarks to the Author)
The acquisition of somatic mutations in hematopoietic stem cells and their clonal expansion during ageing is known as
clonal hematopoiesis and associated with a higher risk of developing acute myeloid leukemia (AML), myelodysplastic
syndrome (MDS) or myeloproliferative neoplasms (MPN). In the manuscript entitled ‘Evolutionary dynamics in the decades
preceding acute myeloid leukemia’ the authors use a novel, error-corrected duplex targeted sequencing approach to
describe the evolution of clonal mosaicism (single nucleotide variants SNVs, mosaic chromosomal aberrations and
translocations recurrent in AML) in serum samples from 50 individuals of the UKCTOCS cohort in the years preceding AML
diagnosis, and 50 age-matched controls. In line with previous publications, AML cases had showed higher clonal complexity
and accelerated clonal evolution, with different patterns, here termed linear evolution, evolution with clonal interference, and
late evolution. The authors provide a mathematical framework to quantitatively model the clonal fitness of multiple competing
clones and clones with multiple mutations, extending a simple ‘single-locus’ evolutionary model of the HSC population from
previous work. Fitting clonal trajectories to the data according to this model seems to accurately recapitulate clonal
dynamics with the proposed model and provides estimate for each clone. On average clonal fitness increased by a factor of
three per additional acquired mutation. Finally, the authors conduct simulations based on the proposed theoretical model
which are in broad agreement with the empirical data. Overall this study seems well conducted and provides unprecedented
detail into the dynamics of clones in the pre-AML phase as well as a new theoretical framework for HSC clonal evolution that
accounts for clonal interference. The manuscript is well written and the data is very well presented. However, several
important aspects need to be addressed:

- All theoretical and experimental data point to hematopoietic stem and progenitor cells being the unit of selection for pre-
malignant clonal hematopoiesis. It is established that VAFs from whole blood DNA reasonably reflect clone size in the stem
cell compartment, though this this is imperfect, as the mutations are often absent in some lymphoid cells. Here, the authors
use cell-free DNA from serum as the source for sequencing, not “peripheral blood DNA’ as the Methods misleadingly state.
To my knowledge, it is unclear how good the correlation of VAFs from serum samples with VAFs from whole blood samples
is, and previous investigations (mainly in cancer patients, e.g. PMID: 32269342, PMID: 34587721, PMID: 38637689) report
varying degrees of correlation, which is presumably – among other factors – dependent on how serum samples were
processed and the size of the clones. For example, in PMID 38637689, mutations with a VAF less than 0.02-0.03 had poor
correlation between cell free DNA and whole blood DNA. The majority of the mutations in the study by Watson et al. fall
below this threshold. As nearly all the inferences (clone birth date, fitness, mutation order, clonal architecture, etc.) rely on
the serum VAF measurement to be an accurate reflection of what is happening in HSCs, it is imperative that the authors
assess the concordance between cfDNA VAF and HSC VAF using their panel. I suggest the authors compare clone size
quantification in ~30-50 paired serum and bone marrow HSPC or granulocyte DNA (at least with the SNV panel) to convince
the reader that VAF in serum samples is a reliable measure of HSC clone size in nearly every case.

- It is standard with a new sequencing panel or technology to perform several types of assay validation. For example, by
using a standard cell line or cell mixing experiments to determine sequencing error rate and assay sensitivity. Sequencing
the same sample in replicates to determine the reliability of VAF measurements would also be critical. It does not appear
that rigorous assay validation was done in this case. I refer the authors to PMID 35610705 for further examples of assay
validation.

- It is likely that in some of the presented cases, AML was preceded by CCUS/MDS (e.g. in those with spliceosome and
ASXL1 mutations), others might have had an MPN prephase (e.g. those with JAK2 or MPL mutations) and these could
potentially correlate with certain evolutionary patterns. It would be informative if the authors could provide information on
blood counts, i.e. hemoglobin, thrombocytes, white blood cell and ideally neutrophil counts at the sampling timepoints, and,
if available, the months before AML diagnosis, to see at what stage in the clonal evolution homeostasis of hematopoiesis
breaks down. If many of these cases with selective sweeps preceding the AML actually had MDS or MPN, the conclusion
would simply be that MDS and MPN are predictors for AML risk—which is not surprising.

- Related to the previous point, could the authors also provide information about the classification of the AML, in particular if
it was classified as de-novo or secondary (therapy-associated, MDS-related) as well as the profile of genetic aberrations in
the diagnostic workup of the AML? In the mutations reported in Extended Data Figure 2, only one FLT3 mutation and 3
NPM1 mutations were detected, in contrast to the expected frequency of ~30%, respectively, in AML, possibly reflecting the
immediate disease initiating role of these mutations.

- Given that there is inevitable uncertainty of clone size estimation, either from inaccuracy of the VAF for technical reasons,
or imperfect reflection of clone size in the VAF if measured in serum samples, I would appreciate the quantification of
uncertainties of the derived quantities. I.e., it would be informative if the authors could provide a measure of uncertainty for
the estimated birth dates of clones and the resulting fitness estimates. While for clones with strong dynamics during
sampling time, it is credible that these estimations are relatively accurate, they are potentially not very accurate for mutations
that have completed a sweep, e.g. like those in Extended Data Fig. 6 a-c or the ASXL1 mutation in Extended Data Fig. 8b.

- While the classification into early, branching and late evolution in the main manuscript looks convincing, it is not
immediately clear to me what exactly led to the classification for some of the cases in the Supplements. For example, it is not
clear to me, why Suppl Fig. 16a, e or g would be categorized as linear, while 17e or i would be called ‘clonal interference’ as
they look very similar. The same for some of the controls, why is Suppl Fig 21a called ‘clonal interference’ when there is only
one clone, and the variation in clone size is likely due to incorrect clone size estimation for indels or variations in the
composition of DNA obtained from serum samples. Is this classification meaningful at all in controls? Why would the
occurrence of a KRAS mutation at age of 71 in Suppl Fig 21d be called late evolution, while the occurrence of a TET2
mutation at age 72 in Suppl Fig 20d is called linear? Should there be a category ‘unclassified’ for samples without sufficient
information / long delay between last sampling and AML diagnosis?

- For the inference of clonal structure, the authors use faster growth rates as an argument that mutations are more likely not
single mutants. On the other hand, the authors conclude that with each additional mutation, clonal fitness increases by a
factor of ~3. Does the former assumption not bias the latter conclusion?

- It seems the authors would be in a good position to determine whether certain mutation combinations are additive or
synergistic for fitness effects. For example, if TET2 and SRSF2 have particular fitness effects when occurring as initiating
mutations, does the combination yield a fitness estimate that is greater than just adding the two?

- The occurrence of ‘sweeps’, where a mutation fixes and a clone takes over the complete production of hematopoietic cells
is interesting. These clones could indeed be missed, if variants were filtered on VAF to exclude potential germline variants.
However, for some of the non-hotspot variants presented here that have already completed the sweep before the first
sampling timepoint, how do the authors know that these are not rare germline mutations? ASXL1 A709T is almost certainly a
germline variant; only truncating mutations are recurrently seen in ASXL1 clonal expansions. There may be other examples
like this.


(Remarks on code availability)


Referee #2

(Remarks to the Author)
Watson et al. conducted sequencing of a target panel of variants for 50 pre-AML women (2-10 time points each) and a
matched sample of healthy women. All samples were drawn from blood between the ages of 50 and 74 years. They
identified several expected patterns, including a lower proportion of clonal hematopoiesis (CH) in healthy women (78%)
compared to those with AML (90%). Additionally, the AML patients exhibited larger variant allele frequencies (VAFs) than
the healthy individuals and typically had multiple clonal hematopoiesis (CH) events. These findings provide valuable
confirmations, and it is noteworthy that they observed the first driver hits often occur decades before CH, with progression to
AML requiring sequential driver hits, all derived from the assembly of a large longitudinal dataset.

The area where the authors present a novel claim is in their conclusion that the observed CH dynamics can be fully
explained by classic ecological competition, excluding the roles of extrinsic factors like spatial dynamics in the bone marrow,
which have been previously considered. They arrive at this conclusion by generating a model that tracks SNV frequencies
(VAFs) over time as a proxy for the prevalence of phylogenetic lineages, analogous to competition between species.
However, I am not convinced that such a conclusion can be confidently made without considering the absolute counts of
hematopoietic stem cells (HSCs) present in an individual over time. If the number of HSCs fluctuates due to spatial or
environmental constraints, then some of the changes in allele frequency could be attributed to the depletion of existing stem
cell populations, which may or may not be compensated by the increase in novel HSC lineages..

(Remarks on code availability)


Referee #3

(Remarks to the Author)
This study from Caroline Watson, Jamie Blundell and colleagues reports an analysis of serial blood samples in the years
preceding eventual development of AML in 50 patients and controls. Using error corrected sequencing the authors quantify
disease latency and fitness advantage of clones and infer phylogenetic trees. The key findings they report are (1) in most
patients the initiating driver mutation is likely acquired in early life, (2) serial acquisition of mutations accelerates clonal
fitness and (3) strong clonal competition occurs in some patients in the pre-AML phase. The authors use these data to
develop a model of evolutionary dynamics in blood which recapitulates many of the experimental observations and known
AML epidemiology, concluding that they find clonal fitness to be predictable and find little evidence of extrinsic factors
playing a major role in this process.

I found the manuscript to be very well written and data clearly presented. The findings are interesting and the sample set
analysed is powerful. The questions outlined in the introduction are important and their ultimate model does help to address
these questions.

However, many of the conclusions and parameters used for their model are already known from the current literature. For
example, it is well known from a number of previous studies that initiating driver mutations for myeloid malignancies are
acquired early in life. It is also known from ‘snapshot’ CH risk models that pre-AML samples typically have “more mutations
per sample, higher variant allele frequencies, indicating greater clonal expansion, and showed enrichment of mutations in
specific genes” (DOI: 10.1038/s41586-018-0317-6). Previous studies have shown increasing fitness advantage conferred by
multiple mutations within a clone e.g. DOI: 10.1038/s41586-021-04312-6. The observed clonal competition is interesting
(although this is also a well-recognised phenomenon) but mechanistic insights into this process are lacking in the current
study. It would be useful if the authors could more clearly articulate what it is about their ‘unique’ sample set that has
provided the crucial information on which their model is based that would otherwise not be known based on current
literature.

Specific comments

The abstract implies samples collected annually for up to 15 years, but in reality sample availability was much more patchy
with the large majority of samples collected in the 8 years prior to AML diagnosis over a period of 5-6 years. This should be
more clearly described with median number of samples over what period of time and median time before AML provided.

The study does not provide detailed information with regards to clinical, laboratory and genetic features of the ultimate AML
that developed. This is absolutely crucial in order to validate that the identified clones/variants drive the development of AML
and to explore the possibility of additional clonal evolution and ‘unknown drivers’, important as a substantial number of
patients (12%) lacked any known driver mutations using their panel (this is unusual in AML). Relating to this, the authors
describe their method for identifying fusion transcripts but is it really correct that they find no patients with chromosomal
rearrangements? This again would be unusual for AML and raises technical concerns.

Many patients already had high clonality across all samples studied and multiple mutations already present at the first
timepoint. Did these patients have normal blood parameters? It is quite possible some of these patients had established
MDS, MPN or overlap syndromes and that, at least in some patients, they are actually studying secondary AML. This is
crucial as the argument for early detection is very different in MDS versus persons with normal blood parameters. Many of
the identified mutations are common in MDS and would define AML with MDS related genetic abnormalities.

The reported VAF detection limit of 0.1%, impressive but almost all the validation is in silico. For SNVs, rearrangements and
mCA this proposed 0.1% sensitivity, and precision of quantification, needs experimental validation using different levels of
spike-in samples from cell lines or samples with known genetic changes; particularly important for chromosome level
changes and to validate the quantification of clonal burden upon which many of the analyses depend e.g. sFig12 looks very
marginal for gains and losses even with 10% level. Without this validation, I would question whether they are really
achieving the sensitivity for detection and precision for quantification that is claimed.

There is extensive experimental data supporting that extrinsic factors can influence clonal fitness. I find the claim that there is
limited evidence of extrinsic factors influencing clonal fitness highly speculative and I would recommend this is toned down
and perhaps they could expand on how their model might be adjusted to incorporate environmental factors as suggested in
the discussion. The stalling/reversal of clones shown in Fig 1c would be consistent with extrinsic effects. How do clones
interfere with each others growth without doing this cell extrinsically noting that this occurs in some cases before high level
clonality is established (excluding competition for space within the niche as a factor)?

The ’late evolution’ cases are puzzling. This is a good example of why details of the AML cases is crucial. It seems unlikely
that 20% of AML will have ‘uncommon driver mutations’ when genomic landscape of AML is so well characterised. Why not
carry out WGS of AMLs to identify all of the drivers? This is crucial for the purposes of this study to distinguish between very
short pre-leukaemic phase vs uncommon driver mutations.

What was the overall clonal advantage observed for clones in controls? Is this consistent with the literature? It appears that
very few are showing clonal expansion (Fig 1c).

When quantifying dynamics, the assumption is that the population frequencies of all clones sum to one. Could the authors
more clearly explain how this works when only low level clone(s) are identified e.g. in controls. How are population
frequencies of ‘all clones’ measured?

I have a number of questions relating to the final model of evolutionary dynamics in blood:
- Why does their model not use the actual inferred median fitness advantage of single mutant clones (20%), double (38%)
etc?
- How does this fit with known changes in HSC number and rate of division with time
- How does HSC number change with time in their model?
- Why does the model assume symmetrical cell division? A model where HSCs divide symmetrically is not compatible with
hematopoiesis
- How does the model and assumptions with regards to driver mutation accumulation fit the known mutation rate in HSC?
- How does the model fit the previously reported variation in clonal advantages that vary throughout life and for different
mutations? E.g. DNMT3A-R882 does not confer a stronger clonal advantage but does increase the risk of AML substantially
more than other DNMT3A mutations. TET2 and DNMT3A mutations are thought to exert different clonal fitness advantage
with age. Spliceosome mutations exert a much stronger clonal advantage than other CH mutations.
- All controls who do not develop AML should nevertheless develop oligoclonal haematopoiesis with age – how does this fit
their model


(Remarks on code availability)
I do not have expertise to review the code or mathematical models; hopefully other reviewers will have this expertise.

Version 1:

Reviewer comments:

Referee #1

(Remarks to the Author)
I will here focus on my major two technical concerns, which relate to the accuracy of using cell-free DNA for quantifying
hematopoietic cell VAF, and the use of a novel sequencing assay. My other concerns have been satisfactorily addressed.

I previously recommended that the authors compare VAF from serum and granulocyte (or HSC) DNA in the same samples.
This remains my suggestion and would be the best way to remove all doubts about the results. It should be fairly trivial to
obtain fresh blood samples from a number of people with CH/AML and split the sample into sorted granulocytes and serum
from blood that has been stored for ~22 hours as in UKCTOCS and show the concordance in VAF.

The authors have elected not to do this but instead present an analysis of cell-type deconvolution of serum based on DNA
methylation patters in the cell-free DNA of 88 samples. Since this is an unconventional approach and does not directly
address the concern, there must be no doubt about the methods used here. The results of this analysis show ~90%
granulocyte and ~10% monocyte origin of this DNA, with negligible contribution from other lineages. I have several
questions about the analysis as presented. First, the authors have used a method (EpiDISH) designed to be used for
genomic DNA analysis, not cell-free DNA. This might be problematic because the fragment sizes of cell-free/leaked DNA
are smaller and more irregular due to nucleases in blood that digest non-nucleosomal DNA, which could confound cell type
inferences. Please provide a size trace of the DNA obtained from UKCTOCS serum samples prior to library preparation and
compare to genomic DNA. Are there differences between the AML cases and controls? How was DNA was prepared for
WGBS? Second, the authors approach used a reference dataset generated from methylation array data and applied it to
WGBS data; this might be problematic for known or unknown reasons. For both these reasons, I recommend the authors use
a reference panel and method designed for cell-free DNA and derived from WGBS data (Celfie, PMID 33976150). Third, the
authors perform relatively shallow (~7X) sequencing, which could influence the reliability of the results. Please show the
coverage at each informative site across the samples and the number of sites with adequate coverage for each cell type.

My second concern related to the validation of TETRIS-seq. They have now performed such validation and report a high
sensitivity for detecting variants (~90% at 0.1% VAF) and a low mean absolute error in VAF replicates (0.97%). I am
confused why 0.1% is still used as the lower limit of detection in the paper given this statement: “The density of variants we
detect starts to decline below 0.3% VAF, suggesting that 0.3% VAF is our reliable limit of detection for the longitudinal final
timepoint samples.” Please explain.

Another, perhaps more important, issue is the reproducibility of the VAF measurements, especially for low VAF clones. This
is important because the growth rates for small clones are generally a large relative change in VAF calculated from very
small absolute changes, so the VAFs need to be very accurate and precise to have reliable inferences for growth rate.
Based on the data in Supplementary Figure 8A, it appears that the analytical precision of VAF measurements may not very
good for clones with VAF below ~0.5-1%. I believe the MAE value the authors report is misleading, as this metric will be
heavily weighted by clone size. Please show the MAE and also the mean relative error based on different windows of VAF.
If the reproducibility of VAF measurements for smaller clones is poor, then I think these would have to be removed from the
analysis.


(Remarks on code availability)


Referee #2

(Remarks to the Author)
My previous reservation about this manuscript was that the population dynamics could be better modeled if we knew the
actual population size of HSCs in patients, as the manuscript assumed they remained constant across ages and individuals.
The authors have acknowledged that "It is conceivable that some observed VAF changes could partly reflect depletion of
wild-type or less fit clones rather than solely the expansion of fitter ones into a constant pool" but they suggest that the HSC
pool may be roughly the same size throughout a persons (adult) life, and cite the Mitchell paper to support this (though the
Osorio and Lee-Six 2018 papers make such suggestions as well). This does not necessarily answer the questions; no one
seems to have adequately addressed the issue of population size constancy. However, to address the comment I made,
authors further emphasize the relative nature of their inferred fitness scores for AML-associated lineages in comparison to
unaffected/healthy lineages in the respective figure legend: "...(where 'fitness' is the growth rate relative to wild-type HSCs)."

In my opinion, this is likely the best possible answer given the available constraints, as a recapture-style experiment to
quantify population size per lineage is unlikely to be feasible at this stage. I suggest that they mention this fact in the text to
ensure complete transparency.

I do, however, have another comment. The authors have not made available (or at least I could not find them) alignments or
results from variant calling, which others could use for reproducing the finding and for downstream analyses. Previous
articles published by Nature (Mitchell and Williams) had made FastA files available as an R data file (.rda) on their GitHub
linked in the Data Availability section. Since the current authors did not build FastA files, the VCFs could be sufficient for
reproducibility studies.

(Remarks on code availability)


Referee #3

(Remarks to the Author)
The authors have done a very thorough job responding to my comments. It is a vast amount of work with huge amounts of
supplemental material with 49 supplemental figures and a 50 page rebuttal. The authors should be commended for
presenting it all so clearly.

I appreciate the arguments made about novelty which are reasonable and are essentially a matter of opinion. My opinion
remains that the current study is very interesting, and the sample set is unique, but the findings support what is already
known, albeit using a powerful new set of samples. Pre-AML samples have previously described analysis of serial samples
and early high VAF mutations e.g. Desai et al Nat Med 2018 but these did not have as many cases with serial samples. The
most striking finding, that extrinsic factors are unlikely to play a role in AML development, is in my view highly speculative
and unsubstantiated. I also have doubts about the early clonal sweep given the number of cases now uncovered with
antecedent myeloid malignancy.

Specific points that in my view do require additional actions

1. My key original concern is somewhat amplified after reading the new material. This relates to the lack of samples and
clinical information relating to AML diagnosis. This is a major problem exemplified in extended Table 10 which outlines
some very unusual clinical scenarios. As I thought was likely, the authors have now uncovered multiple cases with a pre-
diagnosis of a myeloid malignancy. Some of these are quite atypical presentations. For example MDS diagnosed 10 weeks
after AML (048), CML diagnosed 4 years after AML (063), MDS diagnosed 1 week before AML (047), a case of MF
diagnosed 3 months before AML (022) and others. All of these would be very unusual clinical scenarios. This seems to rely
on information from national cancer, death and administrative registries – there is a very real chance that diagnoses will be
incorrectly coded or missed altogether. The absence of blood counts mean other patients may have had myeloid malignancy
either not diagnosed or not recorded in the registry information, a frequent situation in UKBB for example. A number of cases
had AML and MDS diagnosis made simultaneously – again this is not a clinical scenario I recognise e.g. case 043 with AML
and MDS diagnosed at the same time with no mutations a year before diagnosis. A number of patients received
chemotherapy with no samples between chemotherapy and AML diagnosis – an entirely different clone could have been
selected. Some cases here again seem very odd e.g. 3 cases with chemotherapy given within weeks of AML diagnosis. This
is again a very unlikely clinical scenario. I have major doubt with regards to the veracity of clinical information here. Can the
authors explain why it is not possible to go back to medical records for these patients; was consent for this not provided? On
a brief look, the patients on this study did consent to this but I do not have access to the full details so an explanation would
be helpful here. It remains a distinct possibility that poorly coded diagnoses might have led to incorrect diagnosis of AML
being entered, or MDS/MPN missed. It is crucial to independently back up the diagnosis, and timing of this, by accessing
medical records and genetic information available at diagnosis to ascertain which mutations and/or cytogenetic
abnormalities were missed (see also below). If information is from death registries, timing of death should be included. Does
the death registry include information on timing of diagnosis?

2. A final point here is that ext Table 10 also identifies two cases of CML, one simultaneous with AML diagnosis (045) yet in
this case no BCR::ABL was detected and there was a missing driver. The other case (063) had CML diagnosed 4 years after
AML (very unusual) and no BCR::ABL or any other mutation detected. This raises major doubts with regards to their pipeline
for detecting chromosomal rearrangements. Again, medical record adjudication or some other explanation for absence of
BCR::ABL is required

Other points:

The Horizon Myeloid Reference Standard is helpful for mutation detection sensitivity. The validation does show that VAF
quantification is quite noisy and inaccurate at this level. Does this not affect the model? This does not address the issue
relating to mCA calling and I do not find the simulations convincing, particularly in light of the point above that apparently the
BCR-ABL fusion gene was not detected in two patients with CML in the study. This is not fully validated and forms an
important part of their modelling. Please provide empirical evidence or in-silico mixing (or both) for CN-LOH, gains, losses at
0.1–0.5% with sensitivity/precision and VAF accuracy.

The authors have toned down the comment with regards to lack of a role for extrinsic factors, I do not this can be concluded
from their data and a large body of experimental data supports that extrinsic factors can influence clonal selection. Why is
median fitness in single hit clones in controls (12%) different to behaviour of single hit clones in pre-AML (20%) the authors
argue that single hit clones destined for later AML have a greater clonal advantage. Is this context dependent selection not
evidence of a possible role for extrinsic factors influencing clonal behaviour? Just a comment required here.

There is no evidence that HSCs numbers remain constant in pre-AML pts with multiple clones. This seems like a major
assumption without evidence to back it up. The number of cells with stem cell properties might well increase many fold in the
pre-AML phase. An additional added complication that differentiation block might occur with multiple mutations, affecting
VAF in mature neutrophils. Again, this required additional comment rather than experimentation.

New data:

With regards to the new data includes, for the cell type deconvolution analysis I am unclear how many samples were
analysed 23 pre-AML and 23 control? Elsewhere in the methods it states 96 and 88. Are these technical duplicates? Please
clarify.

Minor point:

I do think that the abstract is misleading at present and would suggest it more accurately reflects actual sample availability.
No patients had annual samples for up to 15 years, not even close, median of 5 samples collected over a much shorter
timeframe. This should be clarified in the abstract.



(Remarks on code availability)

Reviewer comments:

(Remarks to the Author)
All of my concerns have been satisfactorily addressed.

Referee #3

(Remarks to the Author)
Apologies once again for the delay with my review.

Thanks to the authors for presenting their thoughts and revisions so clearly.

The revisions substantially improve the validation of the pipeline for CNA and SV noting the significant changes made to the
claimed sensitivity, which seems to have no impact on their model. I think technically the paper is improved.
My fundamental concerns remains the same:

1. The lack of clinical ground truth at AML diagnosis is a major concern. I understand the explanation that it is not possible to
access the clinical data and/or samples at AML diagnosis directly - but this is a problem and, in addition, the revision history
raises significant concerns. At the outset, cases with a prior diagnosis of haem malignancy were missed. There are 17/47
cases in the cohort with concurrent haem malignancy. This has not impacted the model. New cases of ALL or misclassified
CML have been identified - again, no impact on the model. There are many AML cases with no identified driver and/or one
mutation e.g. in ASXL1 (ext fig 5e) or DNMT3A (ext data fig 4e and 6d) which one does not see in clinical practice. Cases
remain with concurrent DLBCL and NHL (pretty unusual), MDS diagnosis very close to AML diagnosis and other odd clinical
scenarios. What about other myeloid malignancies in the cohort without AML? One would expect at least equal numbers of
MDS and MPN. No targeted rearrangements were detected in the assayed pre-diagnostic serum samples. The study cannot
conclude that fusion-defined AML was absent in the eventual leukemias, nor can it confidently infer the timing of such
lesions without paired diagnostic material. In my view, there is a well justified major concern about the veracity of the clinical
data and therefore any conclusions about population dynamics of AML. This should be very clearly highlighted in the main
text.
2. The degree to which the modelling conclusions are actually constrained by the (revised) data is unclear. Very significant
revisions have not had any material impact on the model. A number of examples show fitness is inferred from extremely
limited data e.g. ext Fig 9d (with many other examples). The abstract still implies annual sampling as early as 15 years but
the reality is the sampling is quite sparse and usually over a much shorter period. I have carefully read the arguments made
by the authors but I still do not think the paper justifies the stronger conclusion that extrinsic factors are unimportant or only
second-order. At most, they have shown that a simple intrinsic-fitness model can fit many pre-AML trajectories reasonably
well. That is not a surpise and could have been modelled without these data. It is not the same as demonstrating that models
incorporating strong extrinsic selection factors might also be consistent with equally well-fitting models. I believe this aspect
of the discussion needs to be toned down much more as could be quite misleading for the field. The different fitness of single
hit clones in pre-AML versus controls speaks to this point. The authors argue that "statistical conditioning on the outcome" is
the explanation for increased fitness of single-hit pre-AML clones but it seems entirely plausible, or even likely, that extrinsic
pressures in certain individuals alter the clonal fitness of single hit clones, making progression to AML more likely due to
increase population size

Overall, the paper’s real novelty is narrower than the manuscript sometimes suggests. It is already well established that
somatic mutations can precede AML by years, that CH clones show gene- and combination-specific longitudinal fitness
differences, and that aging hematopoiesis becomes progressively oligoclonal under positive selection. What this paper adds
is powerful serial pre-diagnostic sampling and the attempt to fit explicit clone trajectories over time. That is meaningful, but
many of the conclusions drawn already had substantial support before this work. The conclusion that extrinsic factors are
second order cannot be reliably concluded from these data.

I believe there is not a great deal more experimentation the authors can do, they have done a lot of work to address my
comments as best they can.

Response to reviewers’ comments                                                                          Watson et al



Evolutionary dynamics in the decades preceding acute myeloid leukaemia:
Response to reviewers’ comments
We thank the editor and all 3 reviewers for their constructive comments and questions. We address each of the
reviewers’ comments below.


Reviewer 1
“The acquisition of somatic mutations in hematopoietic stem cells and their clonal expansion during ageing is
known as clonal hematopoiesis and associated with a higher risk of developing acute myeloid leukemia (AML),
myelodysplastic syndrome (MDS) or myeloproliferative neoplasms (MPN). In the manuscript entitled ‘Evolution-
ary dynamics in the decades preceding acute myeloid leukemia' the authors use a novel, error-corrected duplex
targeted sequencing approach to describe the evolution of clonal mosaicism (single nucleotide variants SNVs,
mosaic chromosomal aberrations and translocations recurrent in AML) in serum samples from 50 individuals of
the UKCTOCS cohort in the years preceding AML diagnosis, and 50 age-matched controls. In line with previous
publications, AML cases had showed higher clonal complexity and accelerated clonal evolution, with different pat-
terns, here termed linear evolution, evolution with clonal interference, and late evolution. The authors provide a
mathematical framework to quantitatively model the clonal fitness of multiple competing clones and clones with
multiple mutations, extending a simple ‘single-locus' evolutionary model of the HSC population from previous
work. Fitting clonal trajectories to the data according to this model seems to accurately recapitulate clonal dy-
namics with the proposed model and provides estimate for each clone. On average clonal fitness increased by a
factor of three per additional acquired mutation. Finally, the authors conduct simulations based on the proposed
theoretical model which are in broad agreement with the empirical data. Overall this study seems well conducted
and provides unprecedented detail into the dynamics of clones in the pre-AML phase as well as a new theoretical
framework for HSC clonal evolution that accounts for clonal interference. The manuscript is well written and the
data is very well presented. However, several important aspects need to be addressed:”

    response 1
    We appreciate these constructive comments from the reviewer and outline detailed responses below.

“All theoretical and experimental data point to hematopoietic stem and progenitor cells being the unit of selection
for pre-malignant clonal hematopoiesis. It is established that VAFs from whole blood DNA reasonably reflect clone
size in the stem cell compartment, though this this is imperfect, as the mutations are often absent in some lym-
phoid cells. Here, the authors use cell-free DNA from serum as the source for sequencing, not “peripheral blood
DNA' as the Methods misleadingly state. To my knowledge, it is unclear how good the correlation of VAFs from
serum samples with VAFs from whole blood samples is, and previous investigations (mainly in cancer patients,
e.g. PMID: 32269342, PMID: 34587721, PMID: 38637689) report varying degrees of correlation, which is presum-
ably – among other factors – dependent on how serum samples were processed and the size of the clones. For
example, in PMID 38637689, mutations with a VAF less than 0.02-0.03 had poor correlation between cell free DNA
and whole blood DNA. The majority of the mutations in the study by Watson et al. fall below this threshold. As
nearly all the inferences (clone birth date, fitness, mutation order, clonal architecture, etc.) rely on the serum VAF
measurement to be an accurate reflection of what is happening in HSCs, it is imperative that the authors assess
the concordance between cfDNA VAF and HSC VAF using their panel. I suggest the authors compare clone size
quantification in 30-50 paired serum and bone marrow HSPC or granulocyte DNA (at least with the SNV panel) to
convince the reader that VAF in serum samples is a reliable measure of HSC clone size in nearly every case.”

    response 2
    We thank the reviewer for raising this important point regarding the relationship between our genomic
    readout and haematopoietic stem cell (HSC) clone size.

    Firstly, concerning the composition of the UKCTOCS serum samples, we wish to clarify that the DNA
    analysed is predominantly cellular DNA. This originates from the lysis of white blood cells, primarily
    neutrophils and monocytes, during sample processing, rather than being classical cell-free DNA. To sub-
    stantiate this, we performed whole-genome bisulphite sequencing on 46 UKCTOCS samples (23 pre-AML
    cases and 23 matched controls). Using cell-specific methylation signatures for deconvolution (detailed in
    the revised Methods section, shown below), we estimate that approximately 90% of the DNA derives from
    neutrophils and 10% from monocytes, with a negligible contribution from lymphocytes. We show this
    composition in the new Supplementary Figure 1 (shown below).



August 2024                                                                                                         1
Response to reviewers’ comments                                                                                                                                                        Watson et al




    Secondly, regarding whether VAFs from this neutrophil-enriched DNA accurately reflect HSC clone size,
    we agree that a direct comparison with bone marrow-derived VAFs from the same UKCTOCS participants
    would be ideal. Regrettably, bone marrow samples are not available for this cohort, and replicating the
    original sample processing conditions after 15-20 years of storage is not feasible. However, to address
    this, we have compared our findings with data from a recent study by Spencer-Chapman et al., Nature
    2024 which reports high-resolution phylogenies from matched HSC and monocyte/granulocyte DNA. As
    shown in their Extended Data Figure 7a and their Figure 3d (both reproduced below for the reviewers’
    convenience), these studies demonstrate a strong correlation between clone fractions in HSCs and mono-
    cytes, and concordance between monocyte and granulocyte fractions. This supports the premise that our
    myeloid-derived DNA readout likely provides a faithful reflection of HSC clone size. We have added this
    discussion to the new Supplementary Note 1 (shown below).

    Figure 1: a. Extended Data Figure 7a from Spencer-Chapman et al., Nature 2024. A strong correlation can be seen between clonal fractions
    in HSCs and monocytes from allogeneic haematopoietic cell transplantation (HCT) donor-recipient pairs 9-31 years after HLA-matched sibling
    HCT. HSC clonal fractions were inferred from phylogenies constructed using whole-genome sequencing (WGS) of single-cell derived colonies
    from peripheral blood CD34+ haemaetopoietic stem and progenitor cells (HSPCs). Monocyte clonal fractions were estimated from targeted

    inferred clonal fractions. b. Figure 3d from Spencer-Chapman et al., Nature 2024. Clonal fractions in monocytes and granulocytes are

    Finally, our own UKCTOCS data further suggest that neutrophil-derived VAFs are a reliable proxy for HSC
    clone dynamics. In cases exhibiting clonal expansion without significant clonal interference e.g., C92_005
    (Fig 3d, shown below) and C92_007 (Fig 3e, shown below), where exponential growth at a fixed rate is ex-
    pected, the measured VAF trajectories indeed follow consistent straight lines on a logarithmic scale across
    multiple timepoints. This remarkable agreement suggests that the measured VAFs accurately capture the
    underlying HSC kinetics. A recent study by Fabre et al., Nature 2022 also provides similar evidence, where
    growth rates inferred from HSC phylogenies agree with growth rates inferred from sampling of peripheral
    blood.

        addition to main text: Methods

        Cell type deconvolution. Whole genome bisulphite (WGBS) libraries were prepared for 96 UKCTOCS sam-
        ples (from 23 pre-AML cases and 23 controls). To estimate the proportions of different blood cell types present
        in the samples (B-cells, NK-cells, CD4 T-cells, CD8 T-cells, monocytes, neutrophils and eosinophils), the R
        package EpiDISH was used, using the reference dataset “centDHSbloodDMC.m” within the package61 . As this
        approach leverages methylation frequency across 333 CpGs, all samples for which at least 286 CpGs were cov-
        ered were considered (88 samples, average depth ∼7X). See Supplementary Note 1 and Supplementary Fig.
        1.




        addition to supplement: Supplementary Note 1 and Supplementary Fig. 1

        1. UKCTOCS cell type deconvolution
        Although the UKCTOCS samples were processed as ‘serum samples’, the DNA analysed is predominantly cel-
        lular in origin, arising from the lysis of white blood cells during sample processing, rather than classical cell-
        free DNA. To determine the predominant cellular origin of this DNA, we performed whole-genome bisulphite
        sequencing on 46 UKCTOCS samples (23 pre-AML cases and 23 matched controls). Using cell-specific methy-
        lation signatures for deconvolution (see Methods), we estimate that approximately 90% of the DNA derives
        from neutrophils and 10% from monocytes, with a negligible contribution from lymphocytes (Supplementary
        Figure 1). Although the DNA is not derived directly from haematopoietic stem cells (HSCs), recent work by
        Spencer-Chapman et al.79 supports the validity of using such myeloid-derived DNA for clonal analysis. Their
        data (shown in their Extended Data Figure 7a and their Figure 3d), demonstrate a strong correlation between
        clone fractions in HSCs and monocytes, and concordance between monocyte and granulocyte fractions. This
        supports the premise that the DNA derived from the UKCTOCS serum samples likely provides a faithful reflec-
        tion of HSC clone size.

“It is standard with a new sequencing panel or technology to perform several types of assay validation. For ex-
ample, by using a standard cell line or cell mixing experiments to determine sequencing error rate and assay sen-
sitivity. Sequencing the same sample in replicates to determine the reliability of VAF measurements would also
be critical. It does not appear that rigorous assay validation was done in this case. I refer the authors to PMID
35610705 for further examples of assay validation.”

Response to reviewers’ comments                                                                                     Watson et al



    response 3
    We agree with the reviewer that rigorous assay validation is crucial, a point also raised by Reviewer 3. To
    address this, we have performed extensive validation of our TETRIS-seq platform. For validation of SNV/
    indel detection, we used Horizon Myeloid Reference Standard (containing known mutations at defined
    variant allele frequencies (VAFs)) run in serial dilutions (100%, 25%, 1%, 0.1%) and analysed 2 replicates
    per dilution. We assessed concordance of inferred VAFs and determined assay sensitivity, specificity and
    precision. With typical duplex depth of ∼2000, our practical limit of detection (LoD) for SNVs and indels
    was ∼0.1% VAF. Across variants with expected VAF ≥ 0.1%, strict sensitivity was 88.8%, rising to 95% if
    considering the ability to ‘rescue’ variants (and recall them as ‘real’) if they were reliably called as ‘real’ at
    another longitudinal timepoint. Quantitative agreement between expected and observed VAFs was strong
    (Pearson r 0.975, with mean absolute error (MAE) of 0.97%). Specificity calculated at the position-specific
    substitution level (REF → ALT) was 0.9999992 (false positive rate of 7.69 ×10−7 ). We have added details of
    this validation work to Supplementary Note 2E and 2H (shown below).

    We have also significantly improved and validated our chromosomal rearrangement calling method. In
    earlier versions of the pipeline, we used Manta, but were concerned about its performance, particularly
    its sensitivity at low VAF and its ability to call intra-chromosomal rearrangements (see Gong et al., 2020).
    We therefore developed a custom rearrangement caller, written in Python, and validated it using both in
    silico chromosomal rearrangement data, as well as serial dilutions of DNA samples from individuals with
    known chromosomal rearrangements. We have added a summary of the approach to the Methods sec-
    tion (see below) and full details of the caller’s development, logic and validation are provided in the new
    Supplementary Note 2J (shown below).

       addition to supplement: Supplementary Note 2E

        E. The need for an in silico noise correction approach
        To assess the true positive rate (i.e. what proportion of true variants could be detected) and false positive rate
        (i.e. how many false variants are detected) of our duplex sequencing approach, a Myeloid DNA Reference
        Standard (Horizon Discovery Ltd.) was used, which contains 14 SNVs and 5 insertions/ deletions in gene
        regions targeted by our SNV/ indel panel at validated VAFs ranging from 5 - 70%. Serial dilutions were
        created (25%, 1% and 0.1%), by diluting the Myeloid DNA Reference Standard with a sample of peripheral
        blood-derived DNA from a 65 year old individual (hDNA). These serial dilutions, as well as 100% Myeloid DNA
        Reference Standard and 100% hDNA were sequenced in replicate on the same sequencing lane as the pre-AML
        and control samples (i.e. NovaSeq S4 XP workflow, with ∼ 68 million reads per sample). The sequencing files
        were then processed using the computational workflow described above. Variants were filtered out if the total
        depth at the position was <500, the mean variant position in the read was <8 or the variant’s MAF in ExAC93
        was >1%.

        There was good concordance between replicate samples (Supplementary Fig. 8a) and 88% of the vali-
        dated variants with expected VAFs >0.1% could be detected in the DCS. Only 41% of validated variants with
        VAFs of 0.01-0.1% could be detected, which reduced to 15% for those with VAFs of 0.001-0.1%. Our reliable
        VAF limit of detection, is therefore likely close to ∼ 0.1%, which is consistent with the mean DCS depth of
        ∼1800. We found however, that we were calling far more variants at frequencies <0.5% than we expected
        (white data points Supplementary Fig. 8a), in both the Myeloid Reference Standard DNA and the hDNA,
        the majority of which were detectable in only one of the replicate samples. Analysis of the serial dilutions
        revealed many of these ‘variants’ at <0.5% VAF to be errors, as they were found at the same frequencies in the
        100% Myeloid Reference Standard as the dilutions (cluster of variants lying at frequencies <1% around the
        grey dotted line, (Supplementary Fig. 8b). Further analysis of specific base changes revealed C>T errors were
        particularly common (Supplementary Fig. 8c) in both the Myeloid Reference Standard as well as the hDNA
        sample.

        Supplementary Fig. 8: DCS variant calling using serial dilutions of Myeloid Reference Standard DNA. a. Concordance
        between replicates for serial dilutions of Myeloid Reference Standard DNA. Validated variants in the reference standard are shown
        in colour, according to their expected VAF. b. Correlation between variant VAFs in the undiluted Myeloid Reference Standard and

        hDNA sample (top) and the undiluted Myeloid Reference Standard sample (bottom).


        How many variants do we expect to see?
        In previous work we showed that the distribution of clone sizes is consistent with a simple branching process
        model of haematopoietic stem cell (HSC) divisions38 , such that the probability density as a function of VAF ( f )
        is given by:

                                                         2N τµ − φ(1−2f
                                                                                               e st − 1
                                            ρ( f ) =                e   f)      where     φ=                                            (5)
                                                       f (1 − 2 f )                            2N τs
                                                           N = total number of HSCs
                                 τ = time in years between successive symmetric cell differentiation divisions
                              µ = mutation rate per year, t = age in years, s = fitness effect of variant (% per year)

        Single-cell derived sequencing work37 provides us with HSC mutation rate estimates of ∼ 2.7 × 10−9 /bp
        /year, which, multiplied by the size of our SNV/ indel panel (∼ 58 kb), gives a mutation rate (µ) estimate of
        ∼ 1.6 × 10−4 per year. Our previous work has provided estimates for N τ of ∼ 100, 000 as well as the distribution
        of fitness effects (s) of variants across 10 of the most commonly mutated clonal haematopoiesis genes38 .
        Using these parameters and integrating the expected density of clones (eq. 5) over the distribution of fitness
        effects and then over a range of VAFs (e.g. from 0.1% to 50%), allows us to estimate the number of variants we
        should expect to observe above a certain VAF in an individual of a particular age (Supplementary Fig. 9a). We
        can estimate that we should expect to see ∼ 8 variants between 0.1% and 50% VAF in a 65 year old individual,
        of which ∼ 5 will be at VAFs < 0.5%. This is much less than the 252 putative variants between 0.1% and 50% VAF
        (240 at VAFs <0.5%) that we observed in our test DNA sample from a 65 year old individual (Supplementary
        Fig. 9b). The theoretical error rate of duplex sequencing is often quoted as < 10−9 error per bp, which is simply
        the probability of two complementary errors occurring at the same nucleotide position on both DNA strands,
        either spontaneously or during the 1st PCR cycle43, 94 . In reality, however, library preparation artefacts e.g.
        due to sonication, end-repair and mapping errors44 have all been shown to slip through the duplex error
        correction and likely explains why we see far more ‘variants’ than we expect. Therefore, in order to reliably call
        ‘true variants' <0.5% VAF we developed an in silico noise correction method for SNV calling, which involved
        developing a null model for the errors, so that inconsistencies from the model would be identified as ‘true
        variants'.

        addition to supplement: Supplementary Note 2H

         H. Testing the in silico noise correction model
         Myeloid reference standard samples. To evaluate the sensitivity and specificity of our variant calling, after the
         in silico noise correction model and post-processing steps, we used serial dilutions of Horizon Myeloid DNA
         Reference Standards which contains common myeloid malignancy-associated variants at validated VAFs (see
         Supplementary Note E). All dilutions (and their replicates) were sequenced on the same NovaSeq 6000 S4 lane
         (40 samples per lane). The noise correction model is typically applied across 40 samples at a time (from a
         single sequencing lane), in order to estimate a per-position background error rate and compute per-sample
         p-values. However, if all the Myeloid DNA Reference Standard dilutions/ replicates were grouped together
         in the same 40-sample set, the per-position background error rate would be inflated by concurrent true
         positives. Therefore, to mirror routine calling conditions, in which we expect genuine variants at any given
         position to be uncommon across a batch, we evaluated each reference standard dilution using an independent
         39+1 configuration: for each dilution sample, we assembled a 40-sample set comprising 39 non-reference
         standard samples (from the same sequencing flow-cell) plus a single reference standard sample, ran the noise
         correction model once on that set, and reported calls for the reference standard sample.

         After running the noise correction model on all the reference standard dilutions and replicates, post-
         processing filters (as described above) were applied. Unlike our UKCTOCS samples, the reference standard
         samples only had a single timepoint, but to mirror longitudinal UKCTOCS sample variant calling, we marked
         the reference standard variants as ‘eligible for longitudinal rescue’ if their noise correction model p-value was
         <0.1 with ≥2 variant reads (DCS reads), because it could be recalled as ‘real’ if the same position had a p-value
         < 10−10 at any other timepoint. After the noise correction and post-processing filters had been applied, we
         categorise the variants as follows:
                                              • Detected (i.e. called as ‘real’ by noise correction model): p-value < 10−6 )
                                              • Borderline, eligible for longitudinal rescue: p-value < 0.1 with ≥2 variant reads
                                              • Borderline, single read: p-value < 0.1 with 1 variant reads (or <4 for indels); ‘filtered out’ by post-
                                                processing filters
                                              • Not above position error-rate (not called as ‘real’ by noise correction model): p-value ≥0.1
                                              • Not detected: 0 variant reads


         The results of the variant calling, noise correction model and post-processing steps are shown in Supplemen-
         tary Fig. 14 and Supplementary Table 1.

        Supplementary Fig. 14: Observed vs expected VAF across Horizon Myeloid Reference Standard dilutions with duplex
        error correction.. Datapoints are coloured according to decision category: blue - detected (p -value < 10−6 ); green - borderline,
        eligible for longitudinal rescue (p -value < 0.1 with ≥2 variant reads); orange - borderline, single variant read (p -value <0.1, 1 variant
        read (or <4 for indels), removed by post-processing filters); red - not above position error-rate (p -value≥0.1); grey - not detected (0
        variant reads). Shaded area shows the 95% confidence interval for VAF expected from binomial counting noise at sequencing depth
        ∼2000X.


        Analytical sensitivity and specificity. Because single-read events are removed by post-processing, we defined
        a practical read-count limit of detection (LoD) as two variant reads. For our mean duplex depth of ∼2000, this
        corresponds to a LoD of ∼0.1% VAF. Sensitivity was therefore calculated only over instances whose expected
        VAF was ≥0.1%, so variants genuinely below what can be measured at that depth were not counted as false
        negatives (principally the 0.1% myeloid reference standard dilutions and the variants at expected VAFs of
        0.05% in the 1% dilution).

        Across all expected-positive instances (variants with expected VAF ≥0.1%, 71/80 (88.8%) were detected under
        the strict definition (‘Detected: noise correction model-positive and passing post-processing filters). Apply-
        ing the longitudinal rescue rule increased recovery to 76/80 (95%). Within the reference standard dilutions,
        all expected-positive variants in the 100% dilution were detected (36/36 strict). In the 25% dilution, 35/36
        were detected strictly (97.2%), with the remaining single variant (SF3B1 p.G740E, replicate A) ‘eligible for lon-
        gitudinal rescue’, thus yielding 100% augmented sensitivity at 25% dilution. In the 1% dilution there were 8
        expected-positive instances (≥0.1% VAF). None of these were error-model positive, but 4/8 would be ‘eligible
        for longitudinal rescue’, yielding 50% augmented sensitivity at 1% dilution.

      addition to supplement: Supplementary Note 2H cont...

      Our deliberately conservative background error threshold together with the single variant read filter trades
      some low-VAF sensitivity near the ∼0.1% practical LoD for high specificity. In our intended longitudinal
      use setting, the ability to ‘longitudinally rescue’ variants recovers most borderline true positives, yielding
      near-complete sensitivity overall.

      To calculate specificity we first calculated how many possible SNVs could be called across our panel, by
      annotating all the substitutions possible with ANNOVAR (refGene + ExAC)77 and then removing sites that
      were intronic, synonymous or whose ExAC frequency was >0.001. Assuming all sites were callable in every
      sample, this yielded 130,111 possible SNVs across 47,217 genomic positions. With 10 samples across our
      reference standard dilutions (including replicates of undiluted donor DNA used as a diluent), the specificity
      denominator (K ) was 1,301,110 for substitutions and 472,170 for genomic positions. To decide whether
      non-validated variants were false positives, we required replicate support in the undiluted Myeloid Reference
      Standard: variants present in both 100% Myeloid Reference Standard replicates at similar VAF were treated as
      genuine variants and excluded from the false positive count. Variants lacking such support (and not seen in
      other dilutions or in the undiluted donor DNA) were counted as false positives. Under this rule we observed
      1 false positive (a single 100% Myeloid Reference Standard replicate at ∼0.4% VAF). At the substitution-level,
      this gave a false positive rate of 7.69 per 10 million (7.69 ×10−7 ) and specificity (1 − (F /K )) of 0.9999992.
      For comparison, at the genomic position level, the false positive rate was 2.12 per million (2.12 ×10−6 ) with
      specificity of 0.999997.

      Quantitative accuracy. To reflect how the pipeline is used in practice, quantitative accuracy was assessed
      using both ‘detected’ variants and ‘borderline, eligible for rescue’ variants. On a log-log scale of observed
      vs expected VAF (Supplementary Fig. 14 and restricting to variants with expected VAF ≥0.1% VAF, observed
      VAFs correlated strongly with expected values (Pearson r = 0.975), with a mean absolute error (MAE) of 0.97%,
      meaning that, on average, observed VAFs were typically within approximately +/- 1% of the expected value.
      The regression slope was 1.014, indicating only a slight over-estimation in VAF and minimal systematic bias in
      VAF estimation.


      UKCTOCS samples. Before the in silico noise correction method was applied to our initial 20 pre-AML and 20
      control UKCTOCS final timepoint samples, the number of variants we detected at <1% VAF was ∼100-1000-fold
      higher than we expected (Supplementary Fig. 15a). After applying the noise correction method, however, the
      number of real variants we called was in close agreement with that observed in other studies (Supplementary
      Fig. 15b). The density of variants we detect starts to decline below 0.3% VAF, suggesting that 0.3% VAF is our
      reliable limit of detection for the longitudinal final timepoint samples. Whilst our limit of detection is not as
      low as the Young 201636 and 201983 studies, these studies used 250 ng input DNA for their SSCS error-corrected
      sequencing approach, which is ∼ 5 times more than we used. Also, when calling variants from earlier timepoint
      samples, we can use the information from these final timepoint samples to increase our prior for lower VAF
      variants being real. Our limit of detection across all the UKCTOCS samples will therefore actually be much
      lower and at some positions we should be able to call variants down to near single molecule resolution.

        addition to Methods:

        TETRIS-seq indel, FLT3-ITD, chromosomal rearrangements.
        ... A custom chromosomal rearrangement caller was written in Python and validated using serial dilutions
        of samples with known chromosomal rearrangements (see Supplementary Note J). The caller used a stepwise
        algorithm to identify four classes of chromosomal rearrangement. Calling was restricted to the common break-
        point regions of AML-associated rearrangements that were specifically targeted by our sequencing panel (Sup-
        plementary Fig. 6). Discordant read pairs and their supplementary alignments were first extracted from these
        targeted regions to identify candidate breakpoints. Depending on the read configuration, one or both break-
        point partners could be inferred. For discordant reads where only one partner was identified, soft-clipped
        regions ≥10bp were ‘remapped’ by comparing them to breakpoint partner sequences already identified from
        ≥3 read pairs; a match was assigned if there was >95% sequence match. Concordant soft-clipped reads with-
        out discordant alignments were similarly ‘remapped’, by comparing them to breakpoint partner sequences
        that had been identified by ≥5 read pairs. VAFs were calculated for each breakpoint as the ratio of supporting
        reads to local read depth, and the higher of the two breakpoint VAFs was reported. Breakpoint coordinates
        were annotated using ANNOVAR77 and all calls were subsequently post-processed to refine breakpoint co-
        ordinates, remove artifacts, and collapse multiple read-based events corresponding to the same underlying
        rearrangement (see Supplementary Note J). Chromosomal rearrangement calling was initially performed on
        the pre-consensus BAM files, but any rearrangements that remained after post-processing were recalled on
        the SSCS BAM file to enable more accurate VAF quantification.




        addition to supplement: Supplementary Note 2J:

        J. Detecting chromosomal rearrangements.
        Detection of chromosomal rearrangements from short-read NGS data typically utilise one of three different
        methods:
              • The ‘discordant read pair’ method involves the identification of read pairs that are mapped in an ab-
                normal orientation or whose mapped interval is significantly different to the expected library insert size
                (Supplementary Fig. 19a). A key limitation of this method is its poor breakpoint resolution as well as
                low sensitivity for low VAF events.
              • The ‘split reads’ method looks for regions where there are increased numbers of ‘soft-clipped’ incom-
                pletely mapped reads, which may be suggestive of a chimeric read spanning a rearrangement break-
                point (Supplementary Fig. 19b). The soft-clipped reads can then be assembled in to a contig span-
                ning the translocation breakpoint, which can then be aligned back to the reference genome (‘local re-
                assembly’) to determine the breakpoint positions. The benefit of the ‘split reads’ method is its ability
                to infer breakpoint positions at single-nucleotide resolution and it has higher sensitivity than the ‘dis-
                cordant reads’ method, although high sequencing depth is typically needed to obtain sufficient reads
                overlapping the breakpoint.
              • Combination methods typically involve the ‘discordant read pair’ method first, to predict the presence
                of chromosomal rearrangements, and then the ‘split read’ method next, with or without local reassem-
                bly, to refine the breakpoint positions. The advantage of local reassembly is its ability to reconstruct
                novel rearrangements, although low VAF events can be difficult to detect as there may not be sufficient
                variant reads present for contig assembly100 . Callers that utilise a combination of methods as well as
                local realignment generally have higher sensitivity and lower false discovery rates than callers that use
                a single method, although existing packages generally struggle with detection of low VAF chromosomal
                rearrangements and intra-chromosomal translocations100 .


        Given the limitations of currently available software packages, and our desire for a caller that could reliably
        detect chromosomal rearrangements at low VAF, as well as intra-chromosomal translocations, we developed
        our own custom caller.

      Supplementary Fig. 19: Common methods utilised for detection of chromosomal rearrangements. a. ‘Dis-
      cordant reads’ method identifies read pairs that are mapped in an abnormal orientation or whose mapped interval is
      significantly different to the expected library insert size. b. ‘Split reads’ method looks for regions where there are in-
      creased number of ‘soft-clipped’ incompletely mapped reads. Many translocation caller packages use a combination
      of these methods.


      Categories of chromosomal rearrangement. Chromosomal rearrangements can occur between non-
      homologous chromosomes (e.g. t(8;21)), between homologous chromosomes (e.g. t(16;16)) or within a single
      chromosome (e.g. inv(16)). Depending on whether the rearrangement involves two p-arms, two q-arms or
      a p- and a q-arm, the relocated region may retain its original orientation or become inverted. Chromosomal
      rearrangements can therefore be categorised into four different classes (Supplementary Fig. 20): i) rearrange-
      ment between non-homologous chromosomes with the relocated regions remaining in their original orien-
      tation, ii) rearrangement between homologous chromosomes (or within a chromosome) with the relocated
      regions remaining in their original orientation, iii) rearrangement between non-homologous chromosomes
      with inversion of the relocated regions, and iv) rearrangement between homologous chromosomes (or within
      a chromosome) with inversion of the relocated regions. The AML-associated chromosomal rearrangements
      targeted by our custom panel include rearrangements in three out of four of these categories, but we designed
      our caller so that it could detect all four categories.

        addition to supplement: Supplementary Note 2J: cont...
        Characteristics of paired end reads spanning chromosomal rearrangements. To understand the features
        we would expect to see in paired end sequencing data for each of the four categories of chromosomal rear-
        rangement, a custom Python script was written to generate simulated ‘samples’, each containing a different
        chromosomal rearrangement from each of the four categories, at a defined VAF (Box 3). The mapped SSCS
        BAM files from these samples were aligned using BWA72 , to understand the features that characterise reads
        that span breakpoint regions. Read features in the BAM file were systematically explored using pysam78 and
        Integrative Genomics Viewer (IGV)101 .

        Primary alignments and supplementary alignments. If BWA72 encounters a read that maps to two different
        chromosomes (or different regions of the same chromosome), it will soft-clip the shorter aligned sequence
        and report the longer aligned sequence as the ‘primary alignment’ for that read (Supplementary Fig. 21). If
        the shorter aligned sequence is longer than 30 bp, however, BWA72 also outputs an alternatively mapped
        read in which the longer aligned sequence is soft-clipped and the shorter aligned sequence is reported as
        the ‘supplementary alignment’. This means that as long as at least one of the reads in the pair overlaps the
        rearrangement breakpoint by >30bp, there will always be a primary alignment and a supplementary alignment
        present for the pair of reads. The minimum sequence length of 30bp can be adjusted, although setting this too
        short will increase the chance of mis-mapping information in the supplementary alignment. Supplementary
        reads can be identified in the BAM file by their SAM FLAG which is usually four digits starting with a ‘2’ (e.g.
        2145 and 2193 in Supplementary Fig. 21).

              Primary alignment                                                                 Supplementary alignment
                                                                                         mate                                                                           mate
                                                                                   chr    chr                                                                   chr      chr
                             read 1 forward                                                              read 1 forward
                             97                                                    22     9              2145                                                     9       9
              DNA fragment
                                                                       145          9    22                                                   2193               22      22
