        Supplementary Fig. 21: Primary and supplementary alignments of a pair of reads spanning a t(9;22)
        BCR::ABL breakpoint region. If a read maps to two different chromosomes, or chromosomal regions, BWA72
        assigns the mapping of the longer aligned sequence as the read’s ‘primary alignment’ and ‘soft-clips’ the shorter
        sequence that maps to another region. It also outputs a ‘supplementary alignment’ of the mapping of the shorter
        aligned sequence with ‘soft-clipping’ of the longer sequence that maps to another region. The alignment information
        of a read’s ‘mate’ is that of the ‘mate’ in the primary alignment (e.g. the ‘mate’ of the supplementary read 1 forward
        shown is the primary read 2 reverse read). The number shown within the read is the read’s SAM flag.

        Discordant and concordant reads. Read pairs can be defined as ‘discordant’ or ‘concordant’. ‘Discordant
        reads’ are those whose read pair ‘mate’ maps to a different chromosome or chromosomal region (e.g. read 1
        is mapped to chr22 and read 2 is mapped to chr9 in the primary alignment reads in Supplementary Fig. 21).
        ‘Concordant reads’ are those whose read pair ‘mate’ maps to the same chromosomal region (e.g. both read 1
        and read 2 map to chr9). It is important to be aware that, for supplementary alignments, the ‘mate’ referred
        to in the BAM alignment information is the primary alignment mate, not the supplementary alignment
        mate (i.e. the ‘mate’ of a read 1 supplementary alignment is the read 2 primary alignment). For example, in
        Supplementary Fig. 21, although the supplementary read pairs are mapping to different chromosomes, they
        are mapping to the same chromosome as their primary alignment mate and so these supplementary reads
        would be classified as ‘concordant’.

        The method for identifying discordant read pairs differs slightly depending on the category of chromosomal
        rearrangement. For rearrangements involving non-homologous chromosomes, discordant reads can simply
        be identified as those which map to different chromosomes. For rearrangements involving homologous chro-
        mosome(s), discordant read pairs map to the same chromosome, but the ‘template length’ (reported in the
        BAM alignment information) is significantly larger than the expected library insert size. For non-homologous
        or homologous rearrangements that involve inversion of the relocated segment, the discordant reads have the
        additional feature of being mapped in the same direction (e.g. read 1 forward and read 2 forward).

        Identifying breakpoint partners. Depending on the length of the DNA fragment and where the rearrangement
        breakpoint lies along the length of the fragment, several different read pair scenarios can arise (Supplementary
        Figs. 22, 23 and 24). The scenario to which a read pair belongs can be determined by the reads’ SAM FLAGS
        and BAM alignment information as shown in Supplementary Fig. 22 (chromosomal rearrangements without
        inversion), Supplementary Fig. 23 (chromosomal rearrangements with inverted segment on the ‘left’) and
        Supplementary Fig. 24 (chromosomal rearrangements with inverted segment on the ‘right’).




August 2024                                                                                                                                                                               11
Response to reviewers’ comments                                                                                                     Watson et al




      addition to supplement: Supplementary Note 2J: cont...
      In scenario 1, where the DNA fragment is approximately the same length as the read, the pair of reads both
      have their predominant mapping on the same chromosomal region. The primary alignments will therefore be
      ‘concordant’ and the supplementary alignments will be ‘discordant’ (‘scenario 1 read pairs’ in Supplementary
      Figs. 22, 23 and 24). In this scenario, one of the breakpoint partners can be inferred from the mapped location
      of the primary alignment and the other breakpoint partner can be inferred from the mapped location of the
      supplementary alignment.

      In scenario 2, where the DNA fragment is longer than the read length but the read pairs still overlap, the pair
      of reads have their predominant mapping on different chromosomes. The primary alignments will therefore
      be ‘discordant’ and the secondary alignments will be ‘concordant’ (‘scenario 2 read pairs’ in Figures 22, 23 and
      24). In this scenario, if both reads overlap the breakpoint region then both breakpoint partners can be inferred
      from either the primary or supplementary alignment (Supplementary Fig. 22, read pair d, Supplementary
      Figs. 23 and 24, read pair e). If only one of the reads overlaps the breakpoint then one breakpoint partner can
      be inferred from the primary alignment and the other breakpoint partner from the supplementary alignment
      (Supplementary Fig. 22, read pair e, Supplementary Figs. 23 and 24, read pairs f-g).

      In scenario 3 only one, or none, of the breakpoint partners can be inferred from the reads, either because
      the DNA fragment is longer than both reads, or because the breakpoint region lies towards the end of the
      DNA fragment (‘scenario 3 read pairs’, Supplementary Figs. 22, 23 and 24). When neither of the reads
      span the breakpoint (e.g. because the DNA fragment is very long) it is only possible to infer bounds on the
      breakpoint regions (Supplementary Fig. 22, read pairs g, Supplementary Figs. 23 and 24, read pair j). When
      only one of the breakpoints can be inferred (e.g. because the soft-clipped region is too short to have formed
      a supplementary alignment) the only way to infer the other breakpoint region would be to remap the short
      soft-clipped region (Supplementary Fig. 22, read pairs f, h, i, Supplementary Figs. 23 and 24, read pairs h, i, k, l).

      Identifying breakpoint coordinates. For reads that span a rearrangement breakpoint, it should be possible
      to infer the breakpoint coordinates at single nucleotide resolution for at least one side of the breakpoint from
      the BAM alignment information. The way in which this can be done depends on whether the ‘unmapped’ soft
      clipped sequence is at the beginning or end of the read and whether the relocated region is inverted compared
      to its original orientation. The interpretation of the location of the soft-clipped sequence becomes more com-
      plicated for rearrangements involving inversion, as it is also affected by which of the breakpoint partners is
      inverted. For example: considering the forward strand of DNA, if the breakpoint partner on the left side of
      the rearrangement is inverted, the soft-clipped ‘unmapped’ region will be at the start of the read, whereas if
      the right-side breakpoint partner is inverted, the soft-clipped ‘unmapped’ region will be at the end of the read.
      Which breakpoint partner is inverted also needs to be taken into account when considering the position of any
      soft-clipping due to Illumina adapter sequences. The way in which breakpoint coordinates are inferred and
      soft-clipping interpreted, for chromosomal rearrangements involving an inversion, is summarised in Table S3.

        Supplementary Fig. 22: Characteristics of read pairs that span chromosomal rearrangement breakpoint
        regions, for rearrangements that do not involve inversion of the relocated segments (either non-homologous
        or homologous). In scenario 1 read pairs, one of the breakpoint partners can be inferred from the primary alignment
        and the other breakpoint partner can be inferred from the supplementary alignment. In scenario 2 read pairs, one
        or both breakpoint partners can be inferred from either the primary alignment or the supplementary alignment. In
        scenario 3 read pairs, only one, or none of the breakpoint partners can be inferred from the reads.

      Supplementary Fig. 23: Characteristics of read pairs that span chromosomal rearrangement breakpoint
      regions, for rearrangements that involve inversion of the relocated segment on the ‘LEFT’ of the breakpoint
      fusion (either non-homologous or homologous). In scenario 1 read pairs, one of the breakpoint partners can
      be inferred from the primary alignment and the other breakpoint partner can be inferred from the supplementary
      alignment. In scenario 2 read pairs, one or both breakpoint partners can be inferred from either the primary alignment
      or the supplementary alignment. In scenario 3 read pairs only one, or none of the breakpoint partners can be inferred
      from the reads.

        Supplementary Fig. 24: Characteristics of read pairs that span chromosomal rearrangement breakpoint
        regions, for rearrangements that involve inversion of the relocated segment on the ‘RIGHT’ of the breakpoint
        fusion (either non-homologous or homologous). In scenario 1 read pairs, one of the breakpoint partners can
        be inferred from the primary alignment and the other breakpoint partner can be inferred from the supplementary
        alignment. In scenario 2 read pairs, one or both breakpoint partners can be inferred from either the primary alignment
        or the supplementary alignment. In scenario 3 read pairs, only one, or none of the breakpoint partners can be inferred
        from the reads.

      addition to supplement: Supplementary Note 2J: cont...
      TETRIS-seq chromosomal rearrangement caller. Armed with an understanding of the characteristic features
      of paired end reads that span chromosomal rearrangements, a stepwise method was developed to allow all
      four possible categories of chromosomal rearrangement to be called. This stepwise process involves identifi-
      cation of all possible read pair scenarios in Supplementary Figs. 22, 23 and 24, as well as the identification of
      both breakpoint partners from all of these reads. Calling was restricted to the common breakpoint regions of
      AML-associated rearrangements specifically targeted by our sequencing panel (Supplementary Fig. 6).

      Step 1: Discordant read pair identification. The discordant reads (with mapping quality ≥20) are identified
      within the targeted regions, as well as their concordant alternate alignments (if present). This will retrieve all
      read pairs in Supplementary Fig. 20 except h and i.

      Step 2: Breakpoint partner identification. Either one or both breakpoints partners are called, depending on
      which scenario the read pair belongs to. In Supplementary Fig. 22, both breakpoint partners are identified
      from scenario 1 and scenario 2 read pairs (read pairs a-e) and one breakpoint partner is identified from read
      pair g. Breakpoint bounds are called from read pairs f.

      Step 3: Discordant soft-clip remapping. Soft-clipped regions are remapped for the discordant reads in which
      only one breakpoint partner could be called (Supplementary Fig. 22, read pair g). The soft-clipped regions
      of these reads will be <30 bp, or otherwise a concordant supplementary alignment would be present, and so
      they are at high risk of being mis-mapped if an aligner, e.g. BWA, is used for the remapping. To try to avoid
      this, information from reads that had already allowed identification of both breakpoint partners in step 2
      were used. If one of these breakpoint partners had the same coordinates as the discordant soft-clipped read,
      then the sequence of the other breakpoint partner was compared to the sequence of the soft-clipped region.
      If there was > 95% sequence match, then the unknown breakpoint partner in the discordant soft-clipped
      read could be identified. To try to further avoid mis-mapping, this process was only applied to soft-clipped
      sequences if they were ≥ 10 bp and breakpoint partners from step 2 had to have been identified from a min-
      imum of 3 sets of paired reads in order to be compared to the sequences of the discordant soft-clipped regions.

      Step 4: Concordant read pair identification and concordant soft-clip remapping. Soft-clipped concordant
      reads (with mapping quality ≥20) that do not have a discordant alternate alignment, and whose start coordi-
      nate matches a breakpoint position already found, are identified (Supplementary Fig. 22, read pairs h and i).
      The soft-clipped regions of these concordant reads are then remapped, using the same process used in step 3,
      except breakpoint partners had to have already been identified from a minimum of 5 sets of paired reads in
      order to be compared to the sequences of the concordant soft-clipped regions.

      Step 5: Chromosomal rearrangement VAF calculation and annotation. VAFs for both sides of the chromoso-
      mal rearrangement are calculated:

                                number of read pairs supporting the chromosomal rearrangement
        VAF =
                read depth at the breakpoint coordinate for reads with no evidence of chromosomal rearrangement



      VAFs for both sides of the rearrangement are calculated in case one of the breakpoint partners has poor or
      no panel coverage. The highest of the two VAFs is taken as the final VAF of the chromosomal rearrangement.
      Breakpoint coordinates were annotated with ANNOVAR77 . An output file is produced which details the type
      of chromosomal rearrangement, the identification of both breakpoint partners, the breakpoint coordinates,
      the VAF and the number of reads supporting the rearrangement from each of the different read categories in
      Supplementary Fig. 22.

              Box 3: Generating simulated samples containing chromosomal rearrangements.
              Simulated ‘samples’, each containing a different chromosomal rearrangement from each of the four chromosomal
              rearrangement categories (Supplementary Fig. 20), were generated using custom python scripts.

              t(9;22) BCR::ABL: Non-homologous chromosomes with relocated region in original orientation. To generate
              simulated samples containing t(9;22) BCR-ABL translocations, the full BCR and ABL1 sequences were downloaded
              from Ensembl (GRCh37 Release 104)102 . A random breakpoint position was chosen from the most commonly
              affected BCR and ABL1 breakpoint regions62 . intron 1-2 of ABL1 and intron 14-15 of BCR and BCR::ABL1 and
              ABL1:BCR fusion sequences were created by simply joining the ends of the sequences at the breakpoints together.
              The appropriate number of copies of BCR, ABL1, BCR::ABL1 fusion sequence and ABL1::BCR fusion sequence
              were created, according to the chosen number of simulated cells and VAF.

              t(3;3): Homologous chromosomes with relocated region in original orientation. To generate simulated samples
              containing t(3;3), a random 3’ breakpoint position was chosen between chr3 128294928-128295400 and a random
              5’ breakpoint position was chosen between chr3 128324400-128324929). Fusion sequences were created by simply
              joining the ends of the sequences at the breakpoints together. The appropriate number of copies were chosen
              according to the chosen number of simulated cells and VAF.

              t(9;11) KMT2A::MLLT3: Non-homologous chromosomes with relocated region inverted. To generate simulated
              samples containing t(9;11) KMT2A::MLLT3 translocations, the full KMT2A and MLLT3 sequences were downloaded
              from Ensembl (GRCh37 Release 104)102 . A random breakpoint position was chosen from the most commonly
              affected KMT2A and MLLT3 breakpoint regions62 : intron 7-8 of KMT2A and intron 5-6 of MLLT3. The t(9;11)
              KMT2A::MLLT3 translocation involves relocation of the end part of the MLLT3 gene to join next to the end of
              the first part of the KMT2A gene, with inversion of the relocated MLLT3 segment in the process. Similarly, the
              reciprocal translocation involves relocation of the end part of the KMT2A gene to join next to the end of the
              first part of the MLLT3 gene, with inversion of the relocated KMT2A segment in the process. If viewing from the
              perspective of the forward strand, the inverted segment is located on the 3’ side of the KMT2A::MLLT3 fusion gene
              on the q arm of chromosome 11 and is located on the 5’ side of the MLLT3::KMT2A fusion gene on the p arm
              chromosome 9. KMT2A::MLLT3 and MLLT3::KMT2A fusion sequences were created to reflect this.

              t(16;16) and inv(16) CBFB::MYH11: Homologous chromosomes with relocated region inverted. To generate
              simulated samples containing t(16;16) CBFB::MYH11 and samples containing inv(16) CBFB::MYH11, the full
              CBFB and MYH11 sequences were downloaded from Ensembl (GRCh37 Release 104)102 .. A random breakpoint
              position was chosen from the most commonly affected CBFB and MYH11 breakpoint regions64 : intron 5-6 of
              CBFB and intron 33-34 of MYH11. In both t(16;16) and inv(16), the relocated gene regions are inverted compared
              to their original orientation. In t(16;16) it is because segments from the p and q arms interchange with each other,
              whereas in inv(16) it is because an inversion within the chromosome occurs. Both t(16;16) and inv(16) result in a
              CBFB::MYH11 and MYH11:CBFB fusion genes. In t(16;16), if viewing from the perspective of the forward strand,
              the inverted segment is located on the 3’ side of the CBFB::MYH11 fusion gene on the q arm and the inverted
              segment is located on the 5’ side of the MYH11::CBFB fusion gene on the p arm. In contrast, in inv(16), the
              inverted segment is located on the 3’ side of the CBFB::MYH11 fusion gene on the p arm and on the 5’ side of the
              MYH11::CBFB fusion gene on the q arm. CBFB::MYH11 and MYH11::CBFB fusion sequences, for both t(16;16) and
              inv(16) were created to reflect this.

              Simulated sequencing data was then generated from these samples, using a custom Python workflow that matched
              the steps of our custom panel workflow as much as possible. Briefly, the sequences were ‘fragmented’, producing
              DNA fragment sizes normally distributed about 200 bp. The sequences of our actual custom panel probes were
              then used to ‘capture’ the simulated DNA fragments if at least 30 consecutive nucleotides matched the sequence of
              the probe. An interleaved fastq file was then produced, to simulate paired end 150 bp ‘sequencing’ of the fragments,
              containing read1 and read2 sequences from both the forward and reverse reads, with 3bp UMI (duplex) at the start
              of each read. If the DNA fragment was <150 bp in length, then Illumina adapter sequences were appended to the
              end of the read, so that the total length of the read was 150 bp. The fastq files were then processed using the first
              part of our computational workflow to produce a mapped SSCS BAM file. Illumina adapter sequences were hard-
              clipped and their position information stored in the ‘XT’ tag of the BAM file. UMI information was stored in the
              ‘RX’ tag of the BAM file. The mapped SSCS BAM files were used for exploring the types of reads present in each of
              the different four classes of chromosomal rearrangement and for in silico testing of our custom caller.

Response to reviewers’ comments                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                        Watson et al

      Testing the TETRIS-seq chromosomal rearrangement caller using in silico data. To test the sensitivity of the
      chromosomal rearrangement caller, simulated ‘samples’ were initially used, each containing a different chro-
      mosomal rearrangement from each of the four categories of rearrangement. Samples with a range of different
      VAFs were created (from 5 to 40%), with each sample containing a different breakpoint position chosen ran-
      domly from within the known common breakpoint regions (Box 3). The samples were processed using the
      custom rearrangement caller as described above, with and without steps 3 and 4, to assess how the sensitivity
      was improved by the addition of the discordant soft-clip remapping and concordant soft-clip remapping steps
      (steps 3 and 4). If these steps were not included (Supplementary Fig. 25a, b), the caller systematically under-
      estimated the VAF of the rearrangement. Addition of these steps, however, resulted in very good concordance
      between the known and calculated VAF for each of the samples (Supplementary Figs. 25c), although there
      was some underestimation of the VAF for intrachromosomal rearrangements at VAFs > 10%. The caller was
      successfully able to detect rearrangements in which only one of the breakpoint partners was covered by our
      custom panel probes (e.g. because the other breakpoint partner was in a highly repetitive region), although
      the calculated VAF was slightly underestimated (Supplementary Fig. 25, highlighted by ‘*’).

        Testing the TETRIS-seq chromosomal rearrangement caller using patient samples with known chromosomal
        rearrangements. To test the sensitivity, as well as specificity, of the chromosomal rearrangement caller using
        real samples, we used genomic DNA samples from patients who had known AML-associated chromosomal
        rearrangements (samples kindly provided by Dr Peter Valk, Erasmus University Medical Centre) (see Table S4).
        Serial dilutions were prepared by mixing these patient-derived samples with genomic DNA from a healthy
        human donor (from the LEGACY study, REC 21/YH/0222), to create defined VAFs for benchmarking the caller
        performance. The diluted samples (50ng input), along with undiluted healthy donor DNA (16 samples in total)
        underwent library preparation, as described in the Methods (TETRIS-seq error-corrected library preparation/
        sequencing), but using only the mCA/ chromosomal rearrangement panel probes. Libraries were sequenced
        on the Illumina NovaSeq X 1.5B platform (CRUK Cambridge Institute Genomics Core Facility) using 150bp
        paired-end reads, with 8bp reads for index 1 and index 2. All 16 samples were sequenced on a single lane
        yielding ∼ 60 million reads per sample. The sequencing data was processed as described in Supplementary
        Note C through to the mapped BAM stage (pre-consensus calling). The pre-consensus called BAM was used
        for initial calling of chromosomal rearrangements.

        Table S 4: DNA samples used to validate the TETRIS-seq chromosomal rearrangement caller. Genomic DNA
        from patients with confirmed chromosomal rearrangements (as indicated by karyogram) was used to generate a
        series of dilutions into genomic DNA from a healthy human donor. These dilutions were prepared to create defined
        VAFs for benchmarking caller performance. The chromosomal rearrangements that should be detectable using our
        targeted panel are highlighted in bold.

        Post-processing of chromosomal rearrangement calls. Chromosomal rearrangement calling from genomic
        DNA samples is inherently noisier and more error-prone than calling from simulated in silico data as real sam-
        ples are subject to biochemical artifacts introduced during fragmentation, end-repair, ligation, hybrid capture,
        and PCR amplification. These processes can all generate chimeric molecules that can mimic true chromoso-
        mal rearrangements. Therefore, to reduce false-positive calls, we implemented a series of post-processing
        steps and filters as shown below. These post-processing steps were applied to the chromosomal rearrange-
        ment calls from both the serial dilutions of patient samples with known rearrangements and the UKCTOCS
        samples:
              • Intra-gene rearrangements (i.e. where both breakpoints map within the same gene) were excluded, as
                these are common artefacts in hybrid capture-based panels.
              • Rearrangements were removed if neither breakpoint overlapped one of the AML-associated genes of
                interest: BCR, ABL1, PML, RARA, MYH11, CBFB, RUNX1, RUNX1T1, DEK, NUP214, KMT2A, MLLT3.
              • Rearrangements were grouped together if they shared the same left and right gene partners, rearrange-
                ment type, and had breakpoints within 500 bp of each other. This helped merge fragmented calls rep-
                resenting the same underlying event.
              • Rearrangements were required to match the expected inversion status of the gene pair. For gene pairs
                where an inversion is biologically expected (e.g. CBFB::MYH11 in inv(16), KMT2A::MLLT3 in t(9;11),
                DEK ::NUP214 in t(6;9)), one partner must be annotated as inverted. For gene pairs where no inversion is
                expected (e.g. BCR::ABL1 in t(9;22), PML::RARA in t(15;17), RUNX1::RUNX1T1 in t(8;21)), both partners
                must be non-inverted. Rearrangements not meeting these criteria were excluded.
              • Read evidence filters were applied to all rearrangements, based on the diversity of read-level evidence
                types (as shown in Supplementary Figs. 22, 23 and 24) and number of supporting reads. By default,
                rearrangements were required to have evidence from ≥5 distinct read-level evidence types and ≥5 dis-
                cordant reads. An exception was made for the AML-associated rearrangements specifically targeted by
                our panel for which only ≥3 distinct read-level evidence types and ≥3 discordant reads were required.
                Reciprocal fusions of the targeted AML-associated rearrangements (e.g. MLLT3::KMT2A as the recipro-
                cal of KMT2A::MLLT3) were exempt from all of the above read-based filters, and were retained regardless
                of read support, provided the corresponding canonical fusion was detected and passed all filtering cri-
                teria. For MYH11::CBFB and CBFB::MYH11, both were treated as canonical fusions because they can
                arise from an inversion on the same chromosome; therefore the presence of only one fusion product,
                but not the other, was not grounds for exclusion.




August 2024                                                                                                                     19
Response to reviewers’ comments                                                                                                                                                                                                                              Watson et al




      addition to supplement: Supplementary Note 2J: cont...
      If, after the above filtering steps, any rearrangements were classified as ‘real’, the chromosomal rearrangement
      caller was re-run on the SSCS BAM file (without read ‘flag’ filtering, see Supplementary Note Cii), restricted to
      the breakpoint regions of that rearrangement, in order to obtain a more accurate VAF estimate. Where both
      canonical and reciprocal rearrangements were detected, the higher of the two VAFs was taken as the ‘event
      VAF’.

      TETRIS-seq chromosomal rearrangement caller sensitivity and specificity. After applying the post-
      processing steps described above to the calls from the serially diluted samples with known rearrangements,
      rearrangements were detected as detailed in Table S5 and shown in Supplementary Fig. 26. The TETRIS-seq
      chromosomal rearrangement caller demonstrated high sensitivity and specificity: all targeted AML-associated
      rearrangements at VAFs ≥2.5% were detected (100% sensitivity for VAFs ≥2.5%). At 0.5% VAF, t(9:11) and t(6;9)
      were still detected, but inv(16) and t(8;21) fell below the detection threshold. No chromosomal rearrange-
      ments were detected in the undiluted healthy donor DNA (i.e. no false positives), nor were any unexpected
      chromosomal rearrangements detected in the patient-derived samples, indicating 100% specificity.

      Observed VAFs, pooled across all samples and dilutions (excluding non-detected events) correlated strongly
      with expected values (Pearson r = 0.79), with a mean absolute error (MAE) of 4.89%, meaning that measured
      VAFs were typically within approximately +/-5% of the true value. The regression slope was 0.94, indicating
      only a slight underestimation in VAF and minimal systematic bias in VAF estimation.

      In the t(8;21) RUNX1::RUNX1T1-positive sample (karyotype: 46,XX,t(8;21)(q22;q22)[10]/46,XX,t(8;21)(q22;q22),
      inc[7]/47,XX,+4,t(8;21)(q22;q22)[3]), the caller identified two distinct rearrangement events involving the
      same gene pair but with different breakpoint locations. Both events were reproducibly detected in the 50%,
      25% and 5% dilutions, suggesting they represent genuine rearrangements. While the karyogram could be
      interpreted as a single ancestral t(8;21) event, followed by clonal divergence through acquisition of additional
      chromosomal changes, the detection of two independent RUNX1::RUNX1T1 fusions suggests that the translo-
      cation was acquired on two separate occasions, likely as a late event in clonal evolution. Because the total
      rearrangement burden in this sample was split between these two independent events, the effective VAF for
      each event fell below 0.5% in the 1% dilution, which may have contributed to its lack of detection at this level.

      The TETRIS-seq chromosomal rearrangement caller was then applied, as described above, to assess the pres-
      ence of chromosomal rearrangements in the UKCTOCS final timepoint samples from both the pre-AML cases
      and controls. No rearrangements were detected in any final timepoint samples, and so no earlier timepoints
      were examined for rearrangements.

      TETRIS-seq rearrangement caller. Observed VAFs from the TETRIS-seq chromosomal rearrangement caller for
      DNA samples with known AML-associated rearrangements, tested across serial dilutions. For each rearrangement,
      the plotted observed VAF corresponds to the maximum VAF between the canonical and reciprocal fusion calls. MAE
      = Mean Absolute Error (average absolute deviation of observed VAF from expected VAF in VAF units (%)). For the
      sample with t(8;21) RUNX1::RUNX1T1, two distinct rearrangement events with different breakpoints were detected in
      the same sample (likely reflecting convergent evolution in separate subclones): for this sample, the plotted observed
      VAF is the sum of the two events to allow better comparison with the expected VAF of the sample. See Table S5 for
      the VAFs of individual events.

Response to reviewers’ comments                                                                                  Watson et al



“ It is likely that in some of the presented cases, AML was preceded by CCUS/MDS (e.g. in those with spliceosome
and ASXL1 mutations), others might have had an MPN prephase (e.g. those with JAK2 or MPL mutations) and these
could potentially correlate with certain evolutionary patterns. It would be informative if the authors could provide
information on blood counts, i.e. hemoglobin, thrombocytes, white blood cell and ideally neutrophil counts at
the sampling timepoints, and, if available, the months before AML diagnosis, to see at what stage in the clonal
evolution homeostasis of hematopoiesis breaks down. If many of these cases with selective sweeps preceding
the AML actually had MDS or MPN, the conclusion would simply be that MDS and MPN are predictors for AML
risk—which is not surprising.”



     response 4
     We thank the reviewer for this question regarding the clinical context. While detailed blood count data were
     not routinely collected as part of the UKCTOCS protocol, we have obtained and incorporated information
     on any diagnoses of myelodysplastic syndrome (MDS), chronic myelomonocytic leukaemia (CMML) or
     myeloproliferative neoplasm (MPN) for all participants. We have added details to the Methods section on
     how these data were obtained (see below). This information, including the timing of such diagnoses rel-
     ative to AML, is now presented in Supplementary Table 10, summarised as an addition to Extended Data
     Fig. 2 (see below) and annotated on the relevant individual case figures (Fig. 2 (Müller plots), Fig. 3 (clonal
     trajectories), Extended Data Figs. 3-9 (clonal trajectories) and Supplementary Figs. 27-30 (Müller plots)).
     We have also added Supplementary Note 5 (shown below) where we have grouped the clonal evolutionary
     histories (Müller plots) for the relevant pre-AML cases, according to diagnosis of MDS, CMML or MPN, or
     receipt of prior chemotherapy (see below). Our analysis did not reveal a consistent association between
     specific evolutionary patterns (linear, clonal interference, late) and a preceding MDS/CMML/MPN diag-
     nosis within our cohort, though we acknowledge our study may not be fully powered to detect all such
     associations. Interestingly, we observed instances of individuals developing MDS, CMML or MPN without
     a detectable antecedent clonal sweep by our methods, and conversely, individuals with sweeps who did
     not have a recorded MDS, CMML or MPN diagnosis prior to AML.


        addition to main text: Methods

        Information on any diagnosis of myelodysplastic syndrome (MDS; ICD-10 code D46 or C94.6), chronic
        myelomonocytic leukaemia (CMML; ICD-10 code C93.1) or myeloproliferative neoplasm (MPN; ICD-10 codes
        D45, D47.1, D47.3, D47.4, C92.1, C92.2, C94.4) was obtained for all pre-AML cases and controls via record link-
        age to the National Cancer Registry (England/ Wales), Hospital Episodes Statistics (HES) databases or Patient
        Episode Database for Wales (PEDW), the Office for National Statistics (ONS) mortality records and, for par-
        ticipants in Northern Ireland, the Central Services Agency and Northern Ireland Cancer Registry. Where the
        diagnosis appeared in more than one of these sources, the earliest recorded entry was taken as the date of di-
        agnosis. Information on receipt of chemotherapy (ICD-10 codes Z51.1, Z51.2, Z92.6) or radiotherapy (ICD-10
        codes Z51.0, Z92.3) prior to AML diagnosis (or, for controls, prior to the matched case’s age at AML diagnosis)
        was obtained from the HES databases and PEDW. No pre-AML case or control was recorded as having received
        prior radiotherapy. Smoking information, where available, was obtained from UKCTOCS participant ques-
        tionnaires and HES databases (ICD-10 code Z72.0). Supplementary Table 10 summarises the timing of any
        recorded diagnosis of MDS, CMML or MPN (including MPN subtype), as well as prior chemotherapy expo-
        sure and smoking history, where available. The last updates from all linkage sources were received in 2020. As
        HES and PEDW only cover NHS-funded care in England and Wales it is possible that diagnoses or treatments
        delivered privately, abroad, or elsewhere in the UK may not have been captured.

      addition to supplement: Supplementary Note 5

      5. pre-AML cases also diagnosed with MDS, CMML or MPN, or received prior chemotherapy
      Clonal evolutionary histories for pre-AML cases who were also diagnosed with MDS, CMML, or MPN, as well
      as those with prior chemotherapy exposure, are grouped accordingly and shown in Supplementary Figs. 46, 47,
      48, and 49, respectively. Our analysis did not identify a consistent association between specific evolutionary
      patterns and a preceding MDS, CMML or MPN diagnosis. Although selective sweeps might be anticipated
      in many such cases, we observed individuals who developed MDS, CMML, or MPN without evidence of a
      detectable antecedent sweep.

“Related to the previous point, could the authors also provide information about the classification of the AML,
in particular if it was classified as de-novo or secondary (therapy-associated, MDS-related) as well as the profile
of genetic aberrations in the diagnostic workup of the AML? In the mutations reported in Extended Data Figure
2, only one FLT3 mutation and 3 NPM1 mutations were detected, in contrast to the expected frequency of 30%,
respectively, in AML, possibly reflecting the immediate disease initiating role of these mutations.”

     response 5
     We appreciate these thoughtful comments. Regarding the classification of AML, as described above, we
     have obtained additional clinical information regarding prior diagnoses of MDS, CMML or MPN as well
     as any prior chemotherapy or radiotherapy received by participants. Based on these data, out of the 50
     pre-AML cases, 11 had a diagnosis of MDS, 2 had CMML and 4 had MPN. For all 8 individuals who received
     chemotherapy prior to their diagnosis of AML, the chemotherapy occurred after our final sampling point
     (see Supplementary Fig. 49 shown above in response 4). In 3 of these cases, chemotherapy was only
     received within 3 months of AML diagnosis. Thus, while it is possible these 8 cases may be clinically
     classified as therapy-related AML, the pre-leukaemic dynamics that we observed were not influenced by
     chemotherapy exposure. No pre-AML case or control had received prior radiotherapy. The timing of MDS,
     CMML and MPN diagnoses relative to AML, is now presented in Supplementary Table 10, summarised
     as an addition to Extended Data Fig. 2 and annotated on the relevant individual case figures (Fig. 2
     (Müller plots), Fig. 3 (clonal trajectories), Extended Data Figs. 3-9 (clonal trajectories) and Supplementary
     Figs. 27-30 (Müller plots)). We have also added Supplementary Note 5 where we have grouped the clonal
     evolutionary histories (Müller plots) for the relevant pre-AML cases, according to diagnosis of MDS, CMML
     or MPN, or receipt of prior chemotherapy.



26                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                        August 2024
Response to reviewers’ comments                                                                                   Watson et al




    Concerning the frequency of specific mutations, our panel did detect FLT3 and NPM1 mutations, although
    less frequently than their prevalence in diagnosed AML. Our findings are consistent with FLT3 mutations
    often being later, potentially disease-initiating events, as they were typically detected only very close to,
    or inferred to arise just before, AML diagnosis (e.g. Figure 2i). Similarly, NPM1 mutations appeared to be
    late events in most instances where they were detected (e.g. Figure 2i, C92_010 in Extended Data Fig 3d).
    However, we note one case (C92_018, Figure 2b) where an NPM1 W288Cfs*12 mutation was present 2.5
    years prior to diagnosis at a stable low VAF, suggesting it was not the immediate transforming event in that
    specific instance. We have expanded on these observations in the discussion.


        addition to Discussion...
        A limitation of our study is that we do not have direct access to diagnostic material, meaning we cannot say
        with certainty that the clones detectable in the pre-AML samples actually gave rise to the AML. Furthermore,
        in some cases it is likely that the final transforming event occurs after our last blood sample. Indeed, there
        are interesting differences between the spectrum of mutations we find in pre-AML and those that we know
        are typically seen at AML diagnosis, e.g. in patients aged ≥60 years, NPM1 and FLT3 mutations are found
        in 25-35% of cases53 , and the chromosomal rearrangements targeted in our study are found in ∼10%54 , yet
        both are rare in our cohort. Their absence in our samples strongly suggests they are late-occurring events in
        AML development. Approximately 20% of pre-AML cases in our cohort display either no clonal expansions
        or only rapid expansions that are limited to the 1-2 years prior to diagnosis. It is hard in these cases to know
        for certain whether there may be pre-existing CH clones harbouring uncommon AML driver mutations that
        lie beyond our targeted sequencing panel. It is interesting to note, however, that evidence from deep targeted
        methylation sequencing55 strongly suggests 50% (5/10) of these cases do indeed contain a pre-existing clonal
        expansion (e.g. C92_055, C92_063), whilst others do not (e.g. C92_052, C92_072).




“Given that there is inevitable uncertainty of clone size estimation, either from inaccuracy of the VAF for technical
reasons, or imperfect reflection of clone size in the VAF if measured in serum samples, I would appreciate the
quantification of uncertainties of the derived quantities. I.e., it would be informative if the authors could provide a
measure of uncertainty for the estimated birth dates of clones and the resulting fitness estimates. While for clones
with strong dynamics during sampling time, it is credible that these estimations are relatively accurate, they are
potentially not very accurate for mutations that have completed a sweep, e.g. like those in Extended Data Fig. 6 a-c
or the ASXL1 mutation in Extended Data Fig. 8b.”




    response 6
    This is an excellent suggestion, and we apologise for this oversight in the initial submission. We have now
    systematically quantified the uncertainty in our estimates of clone occurrence times and fitness effects.
    For each clone, we performed our maximum likelihood inference process 25 times with different random
    seeds to generate a distribution of estimates, from which we derived the ‘95% optimizer range’ and the
    standard deviation. These are now provided in Supplementary Table 9. The methodology for this uncer-
    tainty estimation has been added to the Methods and Supplementary Note 4 (see below). Furthermore, the
    figure legends for Figures 3, 4 and relevant Extended Data Figures now clarify that the displayed clone trees
    and plots show the maximum likelihood estimates (see example figure legend below), and direct readers to
    Supplementary Table 9 for the associated errors.


        addition to Methods: Inferring mutation acquisition age and fitness effects.
        To quantify the uncertainty around our fitness and establishment time estimates, we considered all ‘seeds’
        whose likelihood scores were within +/-20% of the best (lowest) log-likelihood. From these we calculated two
        measures of uncertainty: (1) the 95% range of the parameter estimates across these seeds (‘95% optimizer
        range’), defined as the central interval containing 95% of the values, and (2) the standard deviation of these
        parameter values (see Supplementary Note 4, Supplementary Fig. 34 and Supplementary Table 9).

        C. Quantification of uncertainty for fitness and establishment time estimates.

        culated two measures of uncertainty: (1) the 95% range of the parameter estimates across these seeds (‘95%
        optimizer range’), defined as the central interval containing 95% of the values, and (2) the standard deviation
        of these parameter values.

        Supplementary Fig. 34: Example distributions of seed-wise optimisation results used to quantify confidence in inferred
        clonal parameters. Example results are shown from maximum likelihood optimisation of two pre-AML cases (a. C92_002, b.
        C92_054). Each point represents the inferred fitness (y-axis) and establishment time (x-axis) of a single clone from one optimisation
        run (‘seed’), with colours distinguishing different clones. All ‘seeds’ with likelihood scores within +/-20% of the best (lowest) log-
        likelihood are shown. Horizontal and vertical bars denote the ‘95% optimizer range’, defined as the interval containing 95% of the
        parameter estimates across these selected seeds.

“While the classification into early, branching and late evolution in the main manuscript looks convincing, it is
not immediately clear to me what exactly led to the classification for some of the cases in the Supplements. For
example, it is not clear to me, why Suppl Fig. 16a, e or g would be categorized as linear, while 17e or i would be
called ‘clonal interference' as they look very similar. The same for some of the controls, why is Suppl Fig 21a called
‘clonal interference' when there is only one clone, and the variation in clone size is likely due to incorrect clone size
estimation for indels or variations in the composition of DNA obtained from serum samples. Is this classification
meaningful at all in controls? Why would the occurrence of a KRAS mutation at age of 71 in Suppl Fig 21d be called
late evolution, while the occurrence of a TET2 mutation at age 72 in Suppl Fig 20d is called linear? Should there be
a category ‘unclassified' for samples without sufficient information / long delay between last sampling and AML
diagnosis?’




28                                                                                                                                                                                                           August 2024
Response to reviewers’ comments                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                              Watson et al



    response 7
    We thank the reviewer for their careful examination of the supplementary figures and for raising this point
    about classification. We agree that the spectrum of observed clonal dynamics is continuous, and estab-
    lishing rigid boundaries can be challenging. Our aim in classifying cases into ‘linear evolution’, ‘clonal
    interference’, or ‘late evolution’ was to provide a pragmatic framework to highlight the distinct qualitative
    patterns of pre-leukaemic evolution that can emerge. We have elaborated on our classification criteria in
    the Methods section (see below):

         addition to Methods:
         Evolution pattern classification. The evolution pattern for each pre-AML case and control was classified
         as either ‘clonal interference’, ‘late evolution’ or ‘linear evolution’. ‘Clonal interference’ was defined as the pres-
         ence of multiple driver clones on parallel evolutionary branches, with at least one of these clones expanding
         to a substantial proportion of the haematopoietic pool (operationally defined as >20% cell fraction), thereby
         influencing the trajectories of other co-existing clones. ‘Late evolution’ was defined as cases in which the pu-
         tative pre-leukaemic clone was detectable only within 1-2 years prior to AML diagnosis (or, for controls, prior
         to the matched case’s age at AML diagnosis), or where no pathogenic driver mutations (covered by our panel)
         were detected at all. ‘Linear evolution’ encompassed cases not fitting the above two categories, typically show-
         ing sequential acquisition and expansion of driver mutations along a single dominant trunk, with minimal
         evidence of high-frequency side-branching or competition from parallel high-frequency lineages.


    Regarding the specific examples:
       • Supplementary Fig 17e (now 29e) (C92_050): This was classified as clonal interference because the
         GNB1 p.K57E clone reaches >20% cell fraction.
       • Supplementary Fig 17i (now 29i) (C92_090): The decline of the DNMT3A p.L373R variant leads our
         model to infer the presence of a competing ‘missing driver’ clone (dashed line, Extended Data Fig.
         9e) that surpasses 20% cell fraction, hence the classification.
       • Supplementary Fig 21a (now 31a) (CNTRL_005): The model infers a competing high cell fraction
         ‘missing driver’ that interferes with the detected DNMT3A p.L738Cfs*41 variant (dashed line, Supple-
         mentary Fig. 40a).

    For ease of review, the Müller plots and variant trajectories (including missing drivers) for C92_090 and
    CNTRL_005 are shown below (Fig. 2).

Response to reviewers’ comments                                                                             Watson et al



“For the inference of clonal structure, the authors use faster growth rates as an argument that mutations are more
likely not single mutants. On the other hand, the authors conclude that with each additional mutation, clonal
fitness increases by a factor of 3. Does the former assumption not bias the latter conclusion?”

     response 8
     The reviewer raises a valid point regarding the potential bias in using growth rates to inform multi-mutant
     status. We acknowledge that if growth rate were the sole determinant for classifying a clone as multi-
     mutant, it could indeed bias the subsequent observation that multi-mutant clones have higher fitness.

     However, clonal architecture (and thus the number of mutations per clone) was primarily inferred based
     on the co-occurrence patterns of mutations and the principles of phylogenetic reconstruction (as detailed
     in Methods and illustrated by the Müller plots in Figure 2). Specifically, we used the pigeon-hole principle
     (sum of VAFs >50%), crossing trajectories (indicating separate clones), and correlated VAF dynamics
     (suggesting mutations in the same clone). While exceptionally high growth rates, particularly close to AML
     diagnosis, were a secondary consideration for some low VAF variants where other information was sparse,
     this applied to a minority of classifications.

     Furthermore, our estimates for the fitness of single-mutant clones (median 20% per year) are consistent
     with numerous previous studies of clonal haematopoiesis, which also generally report modest fitness ad-
     vantages for such early drivers. The substantially higher fitness values we observe are predominantly in
     clones robustly identified as carrying multiple mutations the principles outlined in the previous paragraph.


“It seems the authors would be in a good position to determine whether certain mutation combinations are ad-
ditive or synergistic for fitness effects. For example, if TET2 and SRSF2 have particular fitness effects when oc-
curring as initiating mutations, does the combination yield a fitness estimate that is greater than just adding the
two?”

     response 9
     This is an excellent suggestion. Indeed, our data provide examples suggestive of synergistic fitness effects.
     For instance, in case C92_007 (Figure 3e), the SRSF2/IDH2 double-mutant clone expands with a fitness
     of approximately 115% per year. This is substantially greater than the typical fitness effects reported for
     individual SRSF2 or IDH2 single-mutant clones in CH, which are generally in the range of 10-30% per year.
     As discussed in the main text, the overall distribution of fitness effects for double-, triple- and quadruple-
     mutant clones compared to single-mutant clones does suggest a greater-than-additive, or synergistic, trend
     on average. However, while our data allow us to observe these general trends and specific striking examples,
     definitively quantifying the precise synergistic interaction (i.e. deviation from additivity) for specific gene-
     pair combinations would require a larger dataset to achieve statistical power for each pair.


“The occurrence of ‘sweeps', where a mutation fixes and a clone takes over the complete production of hematopoi-
etic cells is interesting. These clones could indeed be missed, if variants were filtered on VAF to exclude potential
germline variants. However, for some of the non-hotspot variants presented here that have already completed the
sweep before the first sampling timepoint, how do the authors know that these are not rare germline mutations?
ASXL1 A709T is almost certainly a germline variant; only truncating mutations are recurrently seen in ASXL1 clonal
expansions. There may be other examples like this.”

     response 10
     The reviewer raises an important point here. Distinguishing a complete somatic sweep that occurred early
     in life from a rare germline variant can be challenging with bulk sequencing data alone, especially for
     non-hotspot variants observed at 50% VAF across all timepoints.

     Since the initial submission, we have employed an orthogonal approach using DNA methylation patterns
     to infer the germline or somatic origin of variants where ambiguity exists (as described in Fonseca et al.
     bioRxiv 2025). This analysis has been applied to ambiguous cases in our cohort. While in the majority
     of instances our initial somatic inferences were supported, for the specific example of ASXL1 p.A709T in
     control CNTRL_179, the methylation data suggests that this is likely a germline variant in this individual
     (see Fig. 3 below), and we have amended our interpretation accordingly.




30                                                                                                            August 2024
Response to reviewers’ comments                                                                                                                       Watson et al



    For CNTRL_192, the methylation data similarly suggest that the TET2 p.Q1542_Q1548del variant, observed
    at ∼30% VAF across all timepoints, is likely germline (see Fig. 3 below). While the sub-heterozygous VAF
    might initially suggest somatic origin, we hypothesise that this reflects underrepresentation of the deleted
    allele due to reduced capture efficiency at the affected locus during library preparation. Indeed, this inter-
    pretation is supported by evidence from our custom panel probe manufacturer (TWIST Biosciences), who
    report a ∼25-40% reduction in probe hybridisation efficiency for templates with continuous mismatches
    of 20-30bp, which is consistent with our observation of the 22bp TET2 deletion at 30% VAF. Together, the
    methylation and capture-efficiency data support the reclassification of this variant as likely germline.

Response to reviewers’ comments                                                                                     Watson et al



Reviewer 2
“Watson et al. conducted sequencing of a target panel of variants for 50 pre-AML women (2-10 time points each)
and a matched sample of healthy women. All samples were drawn from blood between the ages of 50 and 74
years. They identified several expected patterns, including a lower proportion of clonal hematopoiesis (CH) in
healthy women (78%) compared to those with AML (90%). Additionally, the AML patients exhibited larger variant
allele frequencies (VAFs) than the healthy individuals and typically had multiple clonal hematopoiesis (CH) events.
These findings provide valuable confirmations, and it is noteworthy that they observed the first driver hits often
occur decades before CH, with progression to AML requiring sequential driver hits, all derived from the assembly
of a large longitudinal dataset.”


“The area where the authors present a novel claim is in their conclusion that the observed CH dynamics can be
fully explained by classic ecological competition, excluding the roles of extrinsic factors like spatial dynamics in the
bone marrow, which have been previously considered. They arrive at this conclusion by generating a model that
tracks SNV frequencies (VAFs) over time as a proxy for the prevalence of phylogenetic lineages, analogous to com-
petition between species. However, I am not convinced that such a conclusion can be confidently made without
considering the absolute counts of hematopoietic stem cells (HSCs) present in an individual over time. If the num-
ber of HSCs fluctuates due to spatial or environmental constraints, then some of the changes in allele frequency
could be attributed to the depletion of existing stem cell populations, which may or may not be compensated by
the increase in novel HSC lineages..”

    response 11
    Thank you for these thoughtful comments. The reviewer raises an important point regarding the inter-
    play between intrinsic clonal fitness, extrinsic factors, and potential fluctuations in absolute HSC numbers.

    Our study suggests that a model based on intrinsic fitness differences between clones and the resulting
    competition for a shared resource (effectively, the ability to contribute to haematopoiesis) can quantita-
    tively explain many of the complex dynamics observed, such as varying trajectories and clonal interference,
    without needing to invoke more complex, spatially explicit, or fluctuating extrinsic factors as the primary
    drivers. The good agreement between our simple model’s predictions and the observed data (e.g. Fig. 3
    and Fig. 6) supports this.

    Regarding absolute HSC counts, our framework models relative fitness – that is, how much faster one clone
    expands compared to others, including wild-type HSCs. For example, a fitness of 10% per year means
    the mutant clone’s representation in the HSC pool increases by approximately 10% annually relative to
    wild-type HSCs. This does not preclude changes in the absolute total number of HSCs. It is conceivable
    that some observed VAF changes could partly reflect depletion of wild-type or less fit clones rather than
    solely the expansion of fitter ones into a constant pool. However, detailed analyses of HSC population
    dynamics from human phylogenetic studies (e.g. Mitchell et al., Nature 2022) generally suggest that the
    total effective size of the HSC pool remains broadly stable throughout adult life.

    We have now clarified in the main text (Results, section ‘Quantitative dynamics of driver mutations’) that
    our fitness estimates refer to relative growth advantages, and our model focuses on these competitive
    dynamics within the HSC compartment.


        addition to Results: Quantitative dynamics of driver mutations
        After surviving an initial stochastic drift phase, a single clone carrying a driver mutation expands exponentially
        at a rate determine by its fitness (where ‘fitness’ is the growth rate relative to wild-type HSCs).




August 2024                                                                                                                  33
Response to reviewers’ comments                                                                               Watson et al



Reviewer 3
“This study from Caroline Watson, Jamie Blundell and colleagues reports an analysis of serial blood samples in
the years preceding eventual development of AML in 50 patients and controls. Using error corrected sequencing
the authors quantify disease latency and fitness advantage of clones and infer phylogenetic trees. The key findings
they report are (1) in most patients the initiating driver mutation is likely acquired in early life, (2) serial acquisition
of mutations accelerates clonal fitness and (3) strong clonal competition occurs in some patients in the pre-AML
phase. The authors use these data to develop a model of evolutionary dynamics in blood which recapitulates many
of the experimental observations and known AML epidemiology, concluding that they find clonal fitness to be pre-
dictable and find little evidence of extrinsic factors playing a major role in this process.

I found the manuscript to be very well written and data clearly presented. The findings are interesting and the
sample set analysed is powerful. The questions outlined in the introduction are important and their ultimate
model does help to address these questions.”

     response 12
     We thank the reviewer for these kind comments and address their questions below.

“However, many of the conclusions and parameters used for their model are already known from the current liter-
ature. For example, it is well known from a number of previous studies that initiating driver mutations for myeloid
malignancies are acquired early in life. It is also known from ‘snapshot' CH risk models that pre-AML samples
typically have “more mutations per sample, higher variant allele frequencies, indicating greater clonal expansion,
and showed enrichment of mutations in specific genes” (DOI: 10.1038/s41586-018-0317-6). Previous studies have
shown increasing fitness advantage conferred by multiple mutations within a clone e.g. DOI: 10.1038/s41586-021-
04312-6. The observed clonal competition is interesting (although this is also a well-recognised phenomenon) but
mechanistic insights into this process are lacking in the current study. It would be useful if the authors could more
clearly articulate what it is about their ‘unique' sample set that has provided the crucial information on which their
model is based that would otherwise not be known based on current literature.”

     response 13
     We thank the reviewer for this comment. While we acknowledge that several general concepts regarding
     clonal haematopoiesis and AML risk are established, our study offers crucial new insights due to the
     unique nature of our dataset and analysis.

     The key distinction of our work lies in the high temporal resolution of serial samples collected over
     many years prior to AML diagnosis. This has enabled us, for the first time at this scale, to move beyond
     static snapshots and directly observe and quantify the in vivo growth patterns and fitness effects of
     pre-leukaemic clones, including those harbouring multiple driver mutations. These direct, quantitative
     estimates of fitness for successive mutational steps (Figure 4, Figure 5) are a critical empirical foundation
     that was previously lacking.

     It is these quantitative data, particularly for multi-mutant clones, that are instrumental in parameterising
     and validating a realistic evolutionary model (Figure 6). This model, grounded in our empirical measure-
     ments, in turn allows us to simulate and understand the pre-leukaemic evolutionary process with greater
     fidelity. For example, our simulations highlight that full somatic sweeps by pre-leukaemic clones are a sur-
     prisingly common feature preceding AML development — an insight with potential implications for risk
     prediction that emerges from modelling based on these dynamic quantitative data.

“The abstract implies samples collected annually for up to 15 years, but in reality sample availability was much
more patchy with the large majority of samples collected in the 8 years prior to AML diagnosis over a period of
5-6 years. This should be more clearly described with median number of samples over what period of time and
median time before AML provided.”

     response 14
     We thank the reviewer for this comment. We agree it is hard to convey all the exact details of the sampling
     in a short abstract. The statement in the abstract is technically correct, however we are open to other
     suggestions and defer to the editor on this issue. The results section does indeed clarify more precisely the
     nature of the blood sampling.

Response to reviewers’ comments                                                                                   Watson et al




        excerpt from Results
        ...We identified all the women who were cancer-free at enrolment but subsequently developed AML during
        the >12 years follow-up, of whom 50 had sufficient blood sample available from ≥2 timepoints prior to AML
        diagnosis. These 50 women were diagnosed with AML at an average age of 72 years (range 53-83 years) and
        had up to 10 serial blood samples (median 5) available prior to diagnosis (Fig. 1a, Extended Data Fig. 1,
        Supplementary Note 1).



“The study does not provide detailed information with regards to clinical, laboratory and genetic features of the
ultimate AML that developed. This is absolutely crucial in order to validate that the identified clones/variants
drive the development of AML and to explore the possibility of additional clonal evolution and ‘unknown drivers',
important as a substantial number of patients (12%) lacked any known driver mutations using their panel (this is
unusual in AML). Relating to this, the authors describe their method for identifying fusion transcripts but is it really
correct that they find no patients with chromosomal rearrangements? This again would be unusual for AML and
raises technical concerns.”

    response 15
    We agree that having genetic data from the AML diagnosis samples would be ideal for definitively linking
    the pre-leukaemic clones to the overt leukaemia. Unfortunately, diagnostic samples were not collected as
    part of the UKCTOCS study protocol, and we do not have access to them. This is a limitation, and we have
    ensured this is now clearly stated in the Discussion (see below). It is indeed possible that additional driver
    mutations, including those not covered by our panel or late-occurring events, were acquired between our
    last sampling point and AML diagnosis.

    Regarding the absence of detected chromosomal translocations in our pre-AML samples: our panel was
    designed to detect 7 AML-associated chromosomal rearrangements. Their absence in the pre-leukaemic
    samples is consistent with many of these specific rearrangements being disease-defining events that occur
    relatively late in leukaemogenesis, potentially close to the time of transformation, similar to some NPM1
    and FLT3 mutations. We have now expanded the Discussion to address the interpretation of ‘late evolution’
    cases where drivers might be missed or acquired later (see below).

    To confirm our technical ability to call chromosomal rearrangements, we have significantly improved and
    validated our chromosomal rearrangement calling method. In earlier versions of the pipeline, we used
    Manta, but were concerned about its performance, particularly its sensitivity at low VAF and its ability
    to call intra-chromosomal rearrangements (see Gong et al., 2020). We therefore developed a custom rear-
    rangement caller, written in Python, and validated it using both in silico chromosomal rearrangement data,
    as well as serial dilutions of DNA samples from individuals with known chromosomal rearrangements. We
    have added a summary of the approach to the Methods section (shown below) and full details of the caller’s
    development, logic and validation are provided in the new Supplementary Note 2J. Reviewer 1 raised a sim-
    ilar point regarding validation of our chromosomal rearrangement calls and so, to maintain readability and
    avoid duplicating lenthy pages of identical text in this Response to Reviewers document, please see Re-
    sponse to Reviewer 1, Response 3 pages 9-21) for our new Supplementary Note 2J.

        addition to Discussion
        A limitation of our study is that we do not have direct access to diagnostic material, meaning we cannot say
        with certainty that the clones detectable in the pre-AML samples actually gave rise to the AML. Furthermore,
        in some cases it is likely that the final transforming event occurs after our last blood sample. Indeed, there
        are interesting differences between the spectrum of mutations we find in pre-AML and those that we know
        are typically seen at AML diagnosis, e.g. in patients aged ≥60 years, NPM1 and FLT3 mutations are found
        in 25-35% of cases53 , and the chromosomal rearrangements targeted in our study are found in ∼10%54 , yet
        both are rare in our cohort. Their absence in our samples strongly suggests they are late-occurring events in
        AML development. Approximately 20% of pre-AML cases in our cohort display either no clonal expansions
        or only rapid expansions that are limited to the 1-2 years prior to diagnosis. It is hard in these cases to know
        for certain whether there may be pre-existing CH clones harbouring uncommon AML driver mutations that
        lie beyond our targeted sequencing panel. It is interesting to note, however, that evidence from deep targeted
        methylation sequencing55 strongly suggests 50% (5/10) of these cases do indeed contain a pre-existing clonal
        expansion (e.g. C92_055, C92_063), whilst others do not (e.g. C92_052, C92_072).




August 2024                                                                                                                35
Response to reviewers’ comments                                                                                   Watson et al




        addition to Discussion
        One aspect of the simple evolutionary model that does not agree with the observed data is the 20% of cases
        exhibiting late evolution. Our multi-stage model generally predicts such cases to be very rare. We believe there
        are two possible explanations for this. First is that the model does not account for situations in which the
        AML is driven by a single ultra-rare event, e.g. an AML-defining translocation such as t(15;17); PML::RARA.
        Second it is possible that a pre-leukaemic clonal expansion has occurred outside of our sequencing panel.
        Whilst we do target the vast majority of known AML drivers, it is likely that there remain rare AML drivers to
        be discovered, as suggested by clonal expansions of unknown genetic origin seen in clonal haematopoiesis40 .
        This likely explains some of the cases seen in our study, such as C92_052, where our analysis of methylation53
        strongly suggests the presence of a fully swept clone.


        addition to Methods:
        TETRIS-seq indel, FLT3-ITD, chromosomal rearrangements.
        ... A custom chromosomal rearrangement caller was written in Python and validated using serial dilutions
        of samples with known chromosomal rearrangements (see Supplementary Note J). The caller used a stepwise
        algorithm to identify four classes of chromosomal rearrangement. Calling was restricted to the common break-
        point regions of AML-associated rearrangements that were specifically targeted by our sequencing panel (Sup-
        plementary Fig. 6). Discordant read pairs and their supplementary alignments were first extracted from these
        targeted regions to identify candidate breakpoints. Depending on the read configuration, one or both break-
        point partners could be inferred. For discordant reads where only one partner was identified, soft-clipped
        regions ≥10bp were ‘remapped’ by comparing them to breakpoint partner sequences already identified from
        ≥3 read pairs; a match was assigned if there was >95% sequence match. Concordant soft-clipped reads with-
        out discordant alignments were similarly ‘remapped’, by comparing them to breakpoint partner sequences
        that had been identified by ≥5 read pairs. VAFs were calculated for each breakpoint as the ratio of supporting
        reads to local read depth, and the higher of the two breakpoint VAFs was reported. Breakpoint coordinates
        were annotated using ANNOVAR77 and all calls were subsequently post-processed to refine breakpoint co-
        ordinates, remove artifacts, and collapse multiple read-based events corresponding to the same underlying
        rearrangement (see Supplementary Note J). Chromosomal rearrangement calling was initially performed on
        the pre-consensus BAM files, but any rearrangements that remained after post-processing were recalled on
        the SSCS BAM file to enable more accurate VAF quantification.




“Many patients already had high clonality across all samples studied and multiple mutations already present at
the first timepoint. Did these patients have normal blood parameters? It is quite possible some of these patients
had established MDS, MPN or overlap syndromes and that, at least in some patients, they are actually studying
secondary AML. This is crucial as the argument for early detection is very different in MDS versus persons with
normal blood parameters. Many of the identified mutations are common in MDS and would define AML with
MDS related genetic abnormalities.”



     response 16
     We thank the reviewer for this question, which was also raised by Reviewer 1. While detailed blood count
     data were not routinely collected as part of the UKCTOCS protocol, we have obtained and incorporated
     information on any diagnoses of myelodysplastic syndrome (MDS), chronic myelomonocytic leukaemia
     (CMML) or myeloproliferative neoplasm (MPN) for all participants. We have added details to the Methods
     section on how these data were obtained (see below). This information, including the timing of such diag-
     noses relative to AML, is now presented in Supplementary Table 10, summarised as an addition to Extended
     Data Fig. 2 (see below) and annotated on the relevant individual case figures (Fig. 2 (Müller plots), Fig. 3
     (clonal trajectories), Extended Data Figs. 3-9 (clonal trajectories) and Supplementary Figs. 27-30 (Müller
     plots)). We have also added Supplementary Note 5 (shown below) where we have grouped the clonal evo-
     lutionary histories (Müller plots) for the relevant pre-AML cases, according to diagnosis of MDS, CMML or
     MPN, or receipt of prior chemotherapy (see below). While full blood counts were unfortunately not part
     of UKCTOCS data collection, these additions now make it possible to assess the relationship between high
     conality and prior haematological conditions. Our data show instances of individuals with extensive clonal
     expansions who did not have a recorded MDS, CMML or MPN diagnosis prior to AML, and conversely,
     individuals with MDS, CMML or MPN where such large expansions were not detected decades prior.

        addition to main text: Methods

        Information on any diagnosis of myelodysplastic syndrome (MDS; ICD-10 code D46 or C94.6), chronic
        myelomonocytic leukaemia (CMML; ICD-10 code C93.1) or myeloproliferative neoplasm (MPN; ICD-10 codes
        D45, D47.1, D47.3, D47.4, C92.1, C92.2, C94.4) was obtained for all pre-AML cases and controls via record link-
        age to the National Cancer Registry (England/ Wales), Hospital Episodes Statistics (HES) databases or Patient
        Episode Database for Wales (PEDW), the Office for National Statistics (ONS) mortality records and, for par-
        ticipants in Northern Ireland, the Central Services Agency and Northern Ireland Cancer Registry. Where the
        diagnosis appeared in more than one of these sources, the earliest recorded entry was taken as the date of di-
        agnosis. Information on receipt of chemotherapy (ICD-10 codes Z51.1, Z51.2, Z92.6) or radiotherapy (ICD-10
        codes Z51.0, Z92.3) prior to AML diagnosis (or, for controls, prior to the matched case’s age at AML diagnosis)
        was obtained from the HES databases and PEDW. No pre-AML case or control was recorded as having received
        prior radiotherapy. Smoking information, where available, was obtained from UKCTOCS participant ques-
        tionnaires and HES databases (ICD-10 code Z72.0). Supplementary Table 10 summarises the timing of any
        recorded diagnosis of MDS, CMML or MPN (including MPN subtype), as well as prior chemotherapy expo-
        sure and smoking history, where available. The last updates from all linkage sources were received in 2020. As
        HES and PEDW only cover NHS-funded care in England and Wales it is possible that diagnoses or treatments
        delivered privately, abroad, or elsewhere in the UK may not have been captured.

Response to reviewers’ comments                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                    Watson et al




      addition to supplement: Supplementary Note 5

      5. pre-AML cases also diagnosed with MDS, CMML or MPN, or received prior chemotherapy
      Clonal evolutionary histories for pre-AML cases who were also diagnosed with MDS, CMML, or MPN, as well
      as those with prior chemotherapy exposure, are grouped accordingly and shown in Supplementary Figs. 46, 47,
      48, and 49, respectively. Our analysis did not identify a consistent association between specific evolutionary
      patterns and a preceding MDS, CMML or MPN diagnosis. Although selective sweeps might be anticipated
      in many such cases, we observed individuals who developed MDS, CMML, or MPN without evidence of a
      detectable antecedent sweep.
