“The reported VAF detection limit of 0.1%, impressive but almost all the validation is in silico. For SNVs, rearrange-
ments and mCA this proposed 0.1% sensitivity, and precision of quantification, needs experimental validation
using different levels of spike-in samples from cell lines or samples with known genetic changes; particularly im-
portant for chromosome level changes and to validate the quantification of clonal burden upon which many of the
analyses depend e.g. sFig12 looks very marginal for gains and losses even with 10% level. Without this validation, I
would question whether they are really achieving the sensitivity for detection and precision for quantification that
is claimed.”

     response 17
     We agree with the reviewer that rigorous assay validation is crucial, a point also raised by Reviewer 1.
     To address this, we have performed extensive validation of our TETRIS-seq platform. For validation of
     SNV/ indel detection, we used Horizon Myeloid Reference Standard (containing known mutations at
     defined variant allele frequencies (VAFs)) run in serial dilutions (100%, 25%, 1%, 0.1%) and analysed
     2 replicates per dilution. We assessed concordance of inferred VAFs and determined assay sensitivity,
     specificity and precision. With typical duplex depth of ∼2000, our practical limit of detection (LoD) for
     SNVs and indels was ∼0.1% VAF. Across instances with expected VAF ≥ 0.1%, strict sensitivity was 88.8%,
     rising to 95% if considering the ability to ‘rescue’ variants (and recall them as ‘real’) if they were present at
     another longitudinal timepoint. Quantitative agreement between expected and observed VAFs was strong
     (Pearson r 0.975, with mean absolute error (MAE) of 0.97%). Specificity calculated at the position-specific
     substitution level (REF → ALT) was 0.9999992 (false positive rate of 7.69 ×10−7 ). We have added details
     of this validation work to Supplementary Note 2E and 2H. To maintain readability and avoid duplicating



40                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                        August 2024
Response to reviewers’ comments                                                                                                                                                                                                             Watson et al



    length pages of identical text in this Response to Reviewers document, please see Response to Reviewer 1,
    Response 3) for our new Supplementary Note 2E and 2H.

    We have also significantly improved and validated our chromosomal rearrangement calling method, as
    described in Response 15) (and Response to Reviewer 1, Response 3), using both in silico chromosomal
    rearrangement data, as well as serial dilutions of DNA samples from individuals with known chromosomal
    rearrangements.

    Regarding mosaic chromosomal alterations (mCAs), the principles illustrated in Supplementary Figure 16
    (shown below) show expected BAF shifts for different clonal fractions. Our ability to detect low-fraction
    mCAs is enhanced by longitudinal data allowing phasing (Supplementary Fig. 17, shown below), and we
    confidently call mCAs down to 0.1% cell fraction where supported by clear BAF deviations across multiple
    phased SNPs.

        Supplementary Fig. 16: LRR and BAF deviations for mCA detection. Normal regions (black plot) contain SNPs with B-allele
        frequencies (BAFs) of 0 (homozygous AA alleles), 0.5 (heterozygous AB alleles) and 1.0 (homozygous BB alleles) and no change in
        read depth (log2 ratio of 0). Regions with gain events (red plots) show deviations in BAF, up to a maximum of ±0.17 if a duplication
        event is in 100% of cells. Gain events result in an increase in read depth (log2 ratio > 0), due to the extra genetic material. Regions with
        loss events (blue plots) or CN-LOH events (orange plots) also show deviations in BAF and can both have full loss of heterozygosity if
        the loss or CN-LOH affects 100% of cells. Loss and CN-LOH events can be distinguished by the read depths, which are decreased
        in loss events (log2 ratio < 0) due to loss of genetic material, but are unchanged in CN-LOH events (log2 ratio = 0). Data generated for
        schematic using simulated samples (100 SNPs per region with mean read depth across ‘panel’ of 1000 reads).

        Supplementary Fig. 17: Phasing SNPs for detection of low cell fraction mCAs. mCAs detected at high enough cell fraction to
        allow differentiation of the heterozygous SNPs that have deviated above and below 0.5 BAF enable SNPs to be ‘phased’ (e.g. in
        the final timepoint ‘sample’ pre-AML diagnosis shown on the left). This phasing information can then be applied to earlier timepoint
        samples (shown on the right) and provides higher sensitivity for detection of smaller BAF deviations and therefore higher sensitivity
        for detection of mCAs at lower cell fraction. Data generated for schematic using a simulated sample (100 SNPs per region with mean
        read depth across ‘panel’ of 1000 reads).

Response to reviewers’ comments                                                                                  Watson et al



“There is extensive experimental data supporting that extrinsic factors can influence clonal fitness. I find the claim
that there is limited evidence of extrinsic factors influencing clonal fitness highly speculative and I would recom-
mend this is toned down and perhaps they could expand on how their model might be adjusted to incorporate
environmental factors as suggested in the discussion. The stalling/reversal of clones shown in Fig 1c would be
consistent with extrinsic effects. How do clones interfere with each others growth without doing this cell extrin-
sically noting that this occurs in some cases before high level clonality is established (excluding competition for
space within the niche as a factor)?”

     response 18
     We appreciate the reviewer’s perspective on the role of extrinsic factors, and we have revised the abstract
     and discussion to ensure our position is more nuanced (see below).

     Our primary conclusion is that a relatively simple evolutionary model, incorporating intrinsic clonal
     fitness and direct competition between lineages for a common resource (i.e., representation in the
     haematopoietic output), can quantitatively recapitulate a remarkable range of the observed pre-leukaemic
     dynamics, including trajectories showing stalling or reversals (e.g. Figure 1c, Figure 3f,g). Such dynamics,
     including clonal interference, emerge naturally in this framework when, for example, a new, fitter clone
     arises and outcompetes established ones, or when multiple clones with differing fitness values expand
     concurrently, altering the ‘mean fitness’ of the population against which each clone competes.

     Our model demonstrates that these complex behaviours can arise without needing to invoke additional
     specific extrinsic factors such as niche interactions or changing environmental pressures as the predominant
     drivers for the patterns we see. This is not to claim that extrinsic factors play no role in haematopoiesis or
     leukaemogenesis – indeed, they are known to be important as the reviewer states. Rather, our findings sug-
     gest that the fundamental principles of Darwinian competition based on cell-intrinsic fitness advantages
     are powerful enough to explain the first-order features of pre-AML clonal evolution observed in our cohort.

        change to Abstract
        Somatic evolution in ageing tissues underlies many cancers. However, our quantitive understanding of the
        rules governing this pre-cancerous evolution remains incomplete. Here we exploit a unique collection of se-
        rial blood samples collected annually up to 15 years prior to diagnosis of acute myeloid leukaemia (AML) to
        provide a quantitative description of pre-cancerous evolutionary dynamics. Using deep duplex sequencing
        and evolutionary theory, we quantify the acquisition ages and fitness effects of the key driver events in AML
        development. The first driver mutations are typically acquired in the first few decades of life when the blood
        remains highly polyclonal. These early slow-growing clones subsequently acquire multiple further driver mu-
        tations which confer selective advantages up to 100-fold larger than the early drivers. These faster-growing
        clones harbouring multiple driver mutations can cause complete somatic sweeps of the blood decades before
        diagnosis, a feature strongly associated with future AML. Once established in the blood, the dynamics of driver
        mutations are highly predictable. Trajectories are shaped by strong clonal competition between lineages with
        limited evidence of other extrinsic factors playing a major role. Our data show that the clonal dynamics of
        blood are consistent with a set of remarkably simple evolutionary rules which strike a balance between chance
        and determinism.



“The ‘late evolution' cases are puzzling. This is a good example of why details of the AML cases is crucial. It
seems unlikely that 20% of AML will have ‘uncommon driver mutations' when genomic landscape of AML is so
well characterised. Why not carry out WGS of AMLs to identify all of the drivers? This is crucial for the purposes of
this study to distinguish between very short pre-leukaemic phase vs uncommon driver mutations.”

     response 19
     We thank the reviewer for this comment and agree that the ‘late evolution’ cases warrant careful consider-
     ation. We have expanded on this in the discussion, as shown in Response 15).

     These cases, where driver mutations are detected only very close to AML diagnosis or not at all with our
     panel, are indeed intriguing. While our targeted panel comprehensively covers genes recurrently mutated
     in CH and AML (Methods), it is possible that in some instances:
         • The key pre-leukaemic evolution occurred via driver mutations not included in our panel or in non-
           coding regions.
         • The pre-leukaemic clone remained below our detection limit (VAF 0.1% ) for most of the observation



42                                                                                                                  August 2024
Response to reviewers’ comments                                                                                                                   Watson et al



          period, only expanding rapidly very late.
        • The crucial transforming events (e.g. certain translocations, or specific mutations like FLT3-ITD
          or NPM1 which are often late events) occurred shortly before diagnosis, after our last sampling point.

    Even with WGS, distinguishing functional drivers from passengers among many rare variants would remain
    challenging and so even broader sequencing would not conclusively resolve this issue. We believe the ‘late
    evolution’ pattern highlights a subset of AML that may have a genuinely shorter measurable pre-leukaemic
    phase involving currently recognised driver genes, or involves less common initiating mechanisms. As
    mentioned in Response 15, diagnostic samples are unavailable to definitively explore the genetics of the
    eventual AML in these specific cases. As outlined in our accompanying manuscript characterising methy-
    lation dynamics in these samples, there is clear evidence from the behaviour of fluctuating CpGs that some
    of the late evolution cases do indeed lack an expanded clone in the pre-AML samples.

“When quantifying dynamics, the assumption is that the population frequencies of all clones sum to one. Could
the authors more clearly explain how this works when only low level clone(s) are identified e.g. in controls. How
are population frequencies of ‘all clones' measured?”



     response 21
     This is a helpful point for clarification. In our framework, the ‘population’ consists of all haematopoietic
     stem cells. When we state that the frequencies of all clones sum to one, this explicitly includes the wild-
     type (non-mutated) HSCs as the background population. Therefore, even if only low-level mutant clones
     are identified (as is common in controls), their frequencies are considered relative to the entire HSC pool,
     the majority of which would be wild-type. We have ensured this is explicitly stated in the Methods section
     describing the quantitative framework (see below).

        Inferring mutation acquisition age and fitness effect. We developed a mathematical framework for inferring
        the acquisition age and fitness effects of clones. In this framework each clone (defined by its most recently
        acquired mutation) has an ‘establishment time’ (t 0 ) and fitness effect (s), which determine the clone’s size
        over time, such that at a particular time (t 1 ), the clone size is given by the equation:

“I have a number of questions relating to the final model of evolutionary dynamics in blood:

Why does their model not use the actual inferred median fitness advantage of single mutant clones (20%), dou-
ble (38%) etc?”

    response 22
    This is an excellent question. The median fitness values input into our simulation model (e.g., s 0 = 10% for
    the first driver, with subsequent drivers conferring an r=3-fold larger median effect) represent an estimate
    of the underlying distribution of fitness effects available upon acquiring a new driver mutation. However,
    the median fitness effects we observe in our pre-AML cohort (e.g., 20% for single mutants, 38% for double
    mutants from Figure 5) are estimates from clones that are, by definition, on a trajectory towards AML.
    There is a subtle but important distinction: the evolutionary process leading to AML will inherently select
    for clones that happen to acquire mutations with higher-than-average fitness effects from the available
    spectrum, especially for earlier mutations (i.e. single-mutants or double-mutants).

    Our simulations reflect this: while the median input fitness for a single mutation (s 0 ) is 10% per year, the
    median fitness of those single-mutant clones that actually progress to virtual AML in the simulations is
    indeed higher (approximately 20%, see code for clonal dynamic simulations). Likewise, while the median
    fitness for double mutants is ∼ 30% the median fitness of those double-mutant clones that actually progress
    to virtual AML in the simulations is again higher (approximately 40%, see code for clonal dynamic simula-
    tions). Thus, the parameters s 0 = 10% and r = 3 were chosen because simulations using these values best
    recapitulated the median fitness of mutant clones that actually progress to AML as well as key macroscopic
    observables, including the real-world age-incidence of AML (Figure 6c) and the relative proportions of lin-
    ear versus branched evolutionary patterns seen in our empirical data (Figure 6b). This is further detailed
    in the Methods.



“How does this fit with known changes in HSC number and rate of division with time”

    response 23
    This is a great point. Our model operates with the product N τ (number of cycling HSCs multiplied by their
    symmetric division time), which is estimated from human data (both single-cell phylogenies [e.g. Lee-Six
    et al., Nature 2018 and Mitchell et al., Nature 2022] and VAF spectra [e.g. Watson et al., Science 2020.]) to be
    approximately N τ = 105 years. Both approaches show that this value remains broadly constant throughout
    adulthood. Observable quantities relevant to clonal evolution, such as the structure of phylogenetic trees
    and the strength of genetic drift, are determined by this product N τ rather than N or τ independently.
    Therefore, our model is consistent with current understanding of HSC population dynamics by using this
    established N τ value, without needing to deconvolve τ and τ, which themselves could in principle vary
    with age in ways that keep their product relatively stable.




August 2024                                                                                                              45
Response to reviewers’ comments                                                                                 Watson et al



“How does HSC number change with time in their model?”

     response 24
     As outlined in the previous response, published data indicate that the product N τ remains approximately
     constant throughout adult life. In our model, this is implemented by keeping N τ fixed at 105 years. For sim-
     plicity in the model, this constant N τ is achieved by holding both N and τ constant (at N = 100, 000 HSCs
     and τ = 1 year, respectively), reflecting the stable effective population size relevant for these evolutionary
     dynamics over the timescales considered



“Why does the model assume symmetrical cell division? A model where HSCs divide symmetrically is not compat-
ible with hematopoiesis”

     response 25
     Thank you for this comment; it highlights a point we ought to have clarified in the original submission.
     Our model does not assume that HSCs only undergo symmetrical divisions; indeed, a system reliant solely
     on symmetric divisions would not be compatible with sustained haematopoiesis as the reviewer states.
     Haematopoiesis requires self-renewal alongside differentiation (often via asymmetric division).

     However, the evolutionary dynamics of HSC clonal sizes — that is, the expansion or contraction of a par-
     ticular genetically distinct HSC lineage — are driven by net changes in the number of HSCs within that lin-
     eage. Such changes arise from an imbalance between symmetric self-renewing divisions (births within the
     lineage) and symmetric divisions leading to differentiation or death of both daughter cells (deaths within
     the lineage). Asymmetric divisions, while crucial for maintaining the overall stem cell pool and producing
     differentiated progeny, do not, in themselves, alter the size of the parent HSC clone. Therefore, models of
     HSC clonal evolution, including ours, typically focus on the rates of symmetric “birth” and “death” events
     as these govern changes in clone size. This is a standard and well-established approach in modelling so-
     matic evolution in stem cell populations (see e.g. Lee-Six et al., Nature 2018, Watson et al., Science 2020,
     Williams et al., Nature 2022, Kapadia et al., Nature 2025). We have ensured this distinction is clear in our
     Methods section (shown highlighted below).

        addition to Methods
        A simulated model of evolutionary dynamics in blood. In this model, blood is maintained by a pop-
        ulation if N cycling HSCs that divide symmetrically every τ years, modelled as a continuous time birth death
        process38 . Asymmetric divisions can occur in this framework, but they do not affect clone sizes and therefore
        are not explicitly modelled.




“How does the model and assumptions with regards to driver mutation accumulation fit the known mutation rate
in HSC?”

     response 26
     This is an important question. The somatic mutation rate in human HSCs is well-characterised, at approx-
     imately 15-20 SNVs per cell per year across the genome (Lee-Six et al., Nature 2018; Abascal et al., Nature
     2021). This equates to a rate of ∼ 6 × 10−9 per cell per bp per year. Our model uses a parameter U , the rate
     of acquiring driver mutations per HSC per year, set at U ∼ 10−5 . This is not the genome-wide SNV rate,
     but rather the aggregate rate at which mutations arise that confer a significant fitness advantage (i.e. >10%
     per year which is the typical scale of fitness effect required to become detectable over a human lifespan).
     This value is derived from, and consistent with, estimates from previous studies of clonal haematopoiesis
     (see Watson et al., Science 2020, Poon et al., Nature Genetics 2021, MacGregor et al., bioRxiv 2025), which
     suggest that a mutation rate of this order across a number of genomic sites is required to explain the ob-
     served prevalence and dynamics of CH. It implies that there are multiple thousands of specific positions
     that can act as initial drivers of clonal expansion, a concept also supported by the diversity of known CH
     driver genes and mutations (i.e. hundreds of targets across tens of common CH genes).




46                                                                                                                 August 2024
Response to reviewers’ comments                                                                          Watson et al



“How does the model fit the previously reported variation in clonal advantages that vary throughout life and for
different mutations? E.g. DNMT3A-R882 does not confer a stronger clonal advantage but does increase the risk
of AML substantially more than other DNMT3A mutations. TET2 and DNMT3A mutations are thought to exert
different clonal fitness advantage with age. Spliceosome mutations exert a much stronger clonal advantage than
other CH mutations.”

    response 27
    The reviewer makes an excellent point about the known heterogeneity in clonal advantages conferred by
    different mutations and in different contexts. Our model is, by design, a simplified framework aiming to
    identify fundamental principles.

    It does not explicitly encode gene-specific fitness effects (e.g. for DNMT3A R882 vs. other DNMT3A
    mutations) or age-dependent fitness variations for specific genes. Instead, it assumes that at each step of
    acquiring a new driver mutation (first, second, etc.), the fitness advantage conferred is drawn from a prob-
    ability distribution (Equation 3, Methods), with the median of this distribution increasing geometrically
    with each additional driver (the ‘r ’ factor). This implicitly accommodates a range of fitness effects at each
    stage.

    The primary purpose of this simplified model is to demonstrate that a few core evolutionary
    rules—stochastic acquisition of driver mutations, a general increase in fitness with mutation burden (ge-
    ometric jumps), and clonal competition—are sufficient to generate many of the complex dynamics we
    observe empirically, as well as macroscopic patterns like AML age-incidence and the relative proportions
    of different evolutionary trajectories (linear vs. branched). By showing that these basic elements can repro-
    duce key features, we highlight their fundamental importance. Incorporating the known detailed complex-
    ities, while valuable for other types of predictive models, would obscure the goal of identifying these core
    general principles here. A more parameter-rich model would, by definition, likely fit the data but might not
    offer the same parsimonious insight into the underlying general rules.



“All controls who do not develop AML should nevertheless develop oligoclonal haematopoiesis with age – how
does this fit their model”

    response 28
    This is an excellent point, and our model is indeed consistent with the expectation that controls will de-
    velop age-related oligoclonal haematopoiesis. Extended Data Figure 10 (a-d) illustrates simulated trajecto-
    ries for control individuals (age-matched to virtual AML cases but not themselves developing AML within
    the simulation timeframe). These simulations clearly show the emergence of clonal expansions, often in-
    volving single or occasionally double-mutant clones, reflecting the oligoclonal state typical of CH in ageing
    healthy individuals. This demonstrates that the same underlying evolutionary framework and parameters
    can capture both the dynamics of CH in individuals who remain healthy and the rarer progression to AML,
    which in our model is defined by the acquisition of a sufficient number of co-operating driver mutations
    (e.g. 4 drivers) leading to frank AML.

Evolutionary dynamics in the decades preceding acute myeloid leukaemia:
Response to reviewers’ comments on revised manuscript
We thank the editor and all 3 reviewers for their constructive comments and questions. We address each of the
reviewers’ comments below.


Reviewer 1
“I will here focus on my major two technical concerns, which relate to the accuracy of using cell-free DNA for quan-
tifying hematopoietic cell VAF, and the use of a novel sequencing assay. My other concerns have been satisfactorily
addressed.

I previously recommended that the authors compare VAF from serum and granulocyte (or HSC) DNA in the same
samples. This remains my suggestion and would be the best way to remove all doubts about the results. It should be
fairly trivial to obtain fresh blood samples from a number of people with CH/AML and split the sample into sorted
granulocytes and serum from blood that has been stored for 22 hours as in UKCTOCS and show the concordance
in VAF.

The authors have elected not to do this but instead present an analysis of cell-type deconvolution of serum based
on DNA methylation patters in the cell-free DNA of 88 samples. Since this is an unconventional approach and does
not directly address the concern, there must be no doubt about the methods used here. The results of this analysis
show 90% granulocyte and 10% monocyte origin of this DNA, with negligible contribution from other lineages. I
have several questions about the analysis as presented. First, the authors have used a method (EpiDISH) designed
to be used for genomic DNA analysis, not cell-free DNA. This might be problematic because the fragment sizes of
cell-free/leaked DNA are smaller and more irregular due to nucleases in blood that digest non-nucleosomal DNA,
which could confound cell type inferences. Please provide a size trace of the DNA obtained from UKCTOCS serum
samples prior to library preparation and compare to genomic DNA. Are there differences between the AML cases
and controls? How was DNA was prepared for WGBS? Second, the authors approach used a reference dataset gen-
erated from methylation array data and applied it to WGBS data; this might be problematic for known or unknown
reasons. For both these reasons, I recommend the authors use a reference panel and method designed for cell-free
DNA and derived from WGBS data (Celfie, PMID 33976150). Third, the authors perform relatively shallow ( 7X)
sequencing, which could influence the reliability of the results. Please show the coverage at each informative site
across the samples and the number of sites with adequate coverage for each cell type.

   response 1
    We thank the reviewer for these important questions regarding the cellular origin of the DNA in the UKC-
    TOCS samples. We appreciate the reviewer’s suggestion to compare paired serum and granulocytes from
    fresh blood. However, we reasoned that characterising the actual UKCTOCS samples used in the study via
    methylation deconvolution would provide more direct and relevant evidence of their specific composition
    than a proxy experiment using fresh blood from different individuals.
    To address the reviewer's specific technical concerns regarding the validity of this deconvolution:
       1. The nature of UKCTOCS serum DNA (Genomic vs. Cell-Free): The reviewer correctly notes that
          EpiDISH is designed for genomic DNA. However, this was a deliberate choice based on the specific
          nature of these samples. Due to the delayed processing protocol of the UKCTOCS trial (overnight
          storage before centrifugation), we hypothesised that the DNA in the serum is not standard apop-
          totic cell-free DNA (cfDNA), but rather predominantly genomic DNA released from neutrophils that
          lysed during the clotting process. This interpretation is consistent with prior studies (e.g., Wong et
          al. Clin Biochem 49 (18) 2016) which demonstrated that the increased DNA concentration in serum
          compared to plasma is driven by the release of genomic DNA predominantly from neutrophils.
          As requested, we have provided TapeStation traces for 4 representative samples (2 pre-AML, 2 con-
          trols) in Figure 1. These traces confirm our hypothesis: They show a high-molecular-weight compo-
          nent (>1 kb) and a nucleosome ladder consistent with partially degraded genomic DNA. The DNA
          Integrity Number (DIN) is ∼3, and concentrations are ∼10 ng/uL (two orders of magnitude higher
          than standard plasma cfDNA). There is no consistent difference in fragmentation between cases and
          controls.
          Because the DNA behaves physicochemically like degraded genomic DNA, the use of EpiDISH was
          methodologically sound.
       2. Validation with CelFie (Method designed for cfDNA): To remove any doubt, we followed the re-
          viewer's recommendation and repeated the deconvolution analysis using CelFie, a method specif-

2                                                                                                                                                                                              March 2026
Response to reviewers’ comments on revised manuscript                                                     Watson et al



My second concern related to the validation of TETRIS-seq. They have now performed such validation and report
a high sensitivity for detecting variants ( 90% at 0.1% VAF) and a low mean absolute error in VAF replicates (0.97%).
I am confused why 0.1% is still used as the lower limit of detection in the paper given this statement: “The density
of variants we detect starts to decline below 0.3% VAF, suggesting that 0.3% VAF is our reliable limit of detection for
the longitudinal final timepoint samples.” Please explain.

   response 2
    Thank you for this comment. This distinction arises from our use of a hierarchical detection strategy,
    which uses the longitudinal nature of the UKCTOCS cohort. We distinguish between the limit of de novo
    discovery and the limit of longitudinal tracking.

    Limit of De Novo Discovery (∼0.3% VAF): To identify a new variant for the first time without prior knowl-
    edge, we must correct for multiple hypothesis testing across the entire sequencing panel. We therefore
    apply a stringent significance threshold (p < 6 × 10−6 ), which empirically corresponds to a VAF of ∼ 0.3%.
    This explains the "density decline" below 0.3% for new calls.

    Limit of Longitudinal Tracking (∼0.1% VAF): Once a variant is definitively identified (meeting the strict
    p < 6 × 10−6 discovery threshold) in at least one timepoint, the statistical burden for detecting that same
    variant in other timepoints from the same individual is substantially reduced. We are no longer scanning
    the whole sequencing panel, but testing a specific hypothesis at a specific genomic position. We can there-
    fore apply a more sensitive threshold (p < 0.1), which corresponds to a VAF of ∼ 0.1%. Consequently, while
    we conservatively require a clone to reach ≥ 0.3% VAF to be discovered, our ability to trace its dynam-
    ics (growth and regression) extends down to 0.1% VAF. Since the paper focuses on evolutionary dynamics
    (which utilizes the full longitudinal trajectory), 0.1% is the relevant limit of quantification for our conclu-
    sions.

Another, perhaps more important, issue is the reproducibility of the VAF measurements, especially for low VAF
clones. This is important because the growth rates for small clones are generally a large relative change in VAF
calculated from very small absolute changes, so the VAFs need to be very accurate and precise to have reliable
inferences for growth rate. Based on the data in Supplementary Figure 8A, it appears that the analytical precision
of VAF measurements may not very good for clones with VAF below 0.5-1%. I believe the MAE value the authors
report is misleading, as this metric will be heavily weighted by clone size. Please show the MAE and also the mean
relative error based on different windows of VAF. If the reproducibility of VAF measurements for smaller clones is
poor, then I think these would have to be removed from the analysis.”

   response 3
    We thank the reviewer for this suggestion. As requested, we have re-analysed the validation data to
    calculate both the Mean Absolute Error (MAE) and Mean Relative Error (MRE), stratified by VAF range (see
    Supplementary Fig. 14 and inset table below).

    There are three important points to make.
      1. Measurement Precision vs. Stochastic Noise: Our analysis confirms that while the Mean Absolute
         Error remains small (≤ 0.3%) down to low frequencies, the Mean Relative Error (MRE) does indeed
         increase below 0.1% VAF. We wish to emphasize that this increase in relative error is not a technical
         failure of the assay, but a fundamental consequence of binomial sampling noise (counting statis-
         tics) at 2000x depth. At 0.1% VAF, the expected number of variant reads is small (∼2), meaning that
         stochastic sampling naturally results in high relative variance.
      2. Robustness of Growth Rate Inference: The reviewer expresses concern that this noise may impact
         the reliability of our fitness inferences. It is important to clarify that our fitness estimation frame-
         work uses a maximum-likelihood model based on binomial count probabilities, not simple linear
         regression. The model explicitly calculates the likelihood of observing a specific read count (k) given
         the depth (N ) and predicted VAF (p) using the binomial distribution B (N , p). Therefore, the model
         expects high relative noise at low VAFs and weighs the data points accordingly. The stochastic vari-
         ation leads to wider confidence intervals for our fitness estimates (correctly reflecting uncertainty)
         but does not introduce systematic bias or false growth signals.
      3. Data Cutoff: To strictly address the reviewer’s concern about reliability, we have adopted 0.1% VAF
         as our limit of reliable quantification. We confirm that no variants below 0.1% VAF were included in
         the UKCTOCS evolutionary analysis.

       Supplementary Fig, 14: Observed vs expected VAF across Horizon Myeloid Reference Standard dilutions with duplex error
       correction. Datapoints are coloured according to decision category: blue - detected (p -value < 10−6 ); green - borderline, eligible for
       longitudinal rescue (p -value < 0.1 with ≥2 variant reads); orange - borderline, single variant read (p -value <0.1, 1 variant read (or <4
       for indels), removed by post-processing filters); red - not above position error-rate (p -value≥0.1); grey - not detected (0 variant reads).
       Shaded area shows the 95% confidence interval for VAF expected from binomial counting noise at sequencing depth ∼2000X. Inset
       table summarises mean absolute errors (MAE) and mean relative errors (MRE) across expected VAF ranges.



       Quantitative accuracy. To reflect how the pipeline is used in practice, quantitative accuracy was assessed
       using both ‘detected’ variants and ‘borderline, eligible for rescue’ variants. On a log-log scale of observed
       vs expected VAF (Supplementary Fig. 14) and restricting to variants with expected VAF ≥0.1% VAF, observed
       VAFs correlated strongly with expected values (Pearson r = 0.975), with a mean absolute error (MAE) of 0.97%,
       meaning that, on average, observed VAFs were typically within approximately +/- 1% of the expected value.
       The regression slope was 1.014, indicating only a slight over-estimation in VAF and minimal systematic bias in
       VAF estimation. When stratified by expected VAF (Supplementary Fig. 14, inset table), MAE remained small
       (≤0.3% VAF), while mean relative error (MRE) increased below 0.1% VAF, consistent with stochastic variation
                                                                                                                    p
       in molecule counts at ∼2000X duplex depth. The precision of VAF estimates scales approximately with 1/ np,
       where n is the number of unique duplex molecules and p the true VAF, meaning that reproducibility is con-
       strained by molecular counting noise at low VAFs. At this depth, the expected relative variability is ∼0.7 at 0.1%
       VAF and >2 at 0.01% VAF, however the absolute deviations remain small (MAE ≤ 0.3% VAF). On this basis, 0.1%
       VAF represents our lower limit of reliable quantification for this assay, with any variants below this threshold
       considered detectable but not quantitatively reliable.

Reviewer 2
“My previous reservation about this manuscript was that the population dynamics could be better modeled if we
knew the actual population size of HSCs in patients, as the manuscript assumed they remained constant across
ages and individuals. The authors have acknowledged that "It is conceivable that some observed VAF changes
could partly reflect depletion of wild-type or less fit clones rather than solely the expansion of fitter ones into a
constant pool" but they suggest that the HSC pool may be roughly the same size throughout a persons (adult) life,
and cite the Mitchell paper to support this (though the Osorio and Lee-Six 2018 papers make such suggestions
as well). This does not necessarily answer the questions; no one seems to have adequately addressed the issue
of population size constancy. However, to address the comment I made, authors further emphasize the relative
nature of their inferred fitness scores for AML-associated lineages in comparison to unaffected/healthy lineages in
the respective figure legend: "...(where ’fitness’ is the growth rate relative to wild-type HSCs)."
In my opinion, this is likely the best possible answer given the available constraints, as a recapture-style experiment
to quantify population size per lineage is unlikely to be feasible at this stage. I suggest that they mention this fact
in the text to ensure complete transparency.

   response 4
    We agree with the reviewer that this is an important point that should be prominent in the main text, not
    just the figure legends. We have added the following explicit statement to the Results section (under “Quan-
    titative dynamics of driver mutations”) where the fitness estimates are first introduced:

       addition to Results section (under “Quantitative dynamics of driver mutations”)
        After surviving an initial stochastic drift phase, a single clone carrying a driver mutation expands
        exponentially at a rate determined by its fitness (where ‘fitness’ is the growth rate relative to wild-
        type HSCs under the assumption of a constant effective population size)


I do, however, have another comment. The authors have not made available (or at least I could not find them)
alignments or results from variant calling, which others could use for reproducing the finding and for downstream
analyses. Previous articles published by Nature (Mitchell and Williams) had made FastA files available as an R data
file (.rda) on their GitHub linked in the Data Availability section. Since the current authors did not build FastA files,
the VCFs could be sufficient for reproducibility studies.”

   response 5
    We agree that reproducibility is important and will make the VCFs publicly available via Zenodo upon pub-
    lication. FASTA files were not generated for the duplex consensus workflow, but the VCFs contain all infor-
    mation required for independent verification of findings.

Reviewer 3
“The authors have done a very thorough job responding to my comments. It is a vast amount of work with huge
amounts of supplemental material with 49 supplemental figures and a 50 page rebuttal. The authors should be
commended for presenting it all so clearly.

I appreciate the arguments made about novelty which are reasonable and are essentially a matter of opinion. My
opinion remains that the current study is very interesting, and the sample set is unique, but the findings support
what is already known, albeit using a powerful new set of samples. Pre-AML samples have previously described
analysis of serial samples and early high VAF mutations e.g. Desai et al Nat Med 2018 but these did not have as
many cases with serial samples. The most striking finding, that extrinsic factors are unlikely to play a role in AML
development, is in my view highly speculative and unsubstantiated. I also have doubts about the early clonal
sweep given the number of cases now uncovered with antecedent myeloid malignancy.


    response 6
    We respectfully emphasise that the novelty of this study lies in the unprecedented temporal resolution of
    the data. To our knowledge, no previous study has reconstructed the quantitative evolutionary history of
    AML-initiating clones across annual timepoints for up to a decade. This longitudinal density allows us to
    move beyond comparing static "snapshots" of clonal burden to explicitly measuring the rates of expansion
    and interaction.

    Regarding the drivers of these dynamics, we do not claim that extrinsic factors (such as inflammation or
    environmental exposures) are irrelevant to clonal fitness. We fully appreciate the role of gene-environment
    interactions, as elegantly demonstrated by studies such as Bolton et al. (Nat. Genet., 2020). However, our
    data and modelling indicates that these extrinsic factors appear to be “second-order” effects in the nat-
    ural history of these patients. Instead, the complex trajectories observed in the years leading up to AML
    are sufficiently explained by three "first-order" intrinsic elements: (i) stochasticity in the precise timing of
    driver mutation acquisition, (ii) fitness differences inherent to specific variants and combinatorial geno-
    types and (iii) clonal competition between co-existing lineages. Our data and modelling demonstrates that
    these three intrinsic forces alone can recapitulate the major features of pre-leukaemic evolution without
    needing to invoke complex time-varying environmental selection pressures.


Specific points that in my view do require additional actions

1. My key original concern is somewhat amplified after reading the new material. This relates to the lack of samples
and clinical information relating to AML diagnosis. This is a major problem exemplified in extended Table 10 which
outlines some very unusual clinical scenarios. As I thought was likely, the authors have now uncovered multiple
cases with a pre-diagnosis of a myeloid malignancy. Some of these are quite atypical presentations. For example
MDS diagnosed 10 weeks after AML (048), CML diagnosed 4 years after AML (063), MDS diagnosed 1 week before
AML (047), a case of MF diagnosed 3 months before AML (022) and others. All of these would be very unusual
clinical scenarios. This seems to rely on information from national cancer, death and administrative registries –
there is a very real chance that diagnoses will be incorrectly coded or missed altogether. The absence of blood
counts mean other patients may have had myeloid malignancy either not diagnosed or not recorded in the registry
information, a frequent situation in UKBB for example. A number of cases had AML and MDS diagnosis made
simultaneously – again this is not a clinical scenario I recognise e.g. case 043 with AML and MDS diagnosed at
the same time with no mutations a year before diagnosis. A number of patients received chemotherapy with no
samples between chemotherapy and AML diagnosis – an entirely different clone could have been selected. Some
cases here again seem very odd e.g. 3 cases with chemotherapy given within weeks of AML diagnosis. This is again
a very unlikely clinical scenario. I have major doubt with regards to the veracity of clinical information here. Can
the authors explain why it is not possible to go back to medical records for these patients; was consent for this
not provided? On a brief look, the patients on this study did consent to this but I do not have access to the full
details so an explanation would be helpful here. It remains a distinct possibility that poorly coded diagnoses might
have led to incorrect diagnosis of AML being entered, or MDS/MPN missed. It is crucial to independently back
up the diagnosis, and timing of this, by accessing medical records and genetic information available at diagnosis
to ascertain which mutations and/or cytogenetic abnormalities were missed (see also below). If information is
from death registries, timing of death should be included. Does the death registry include information on timing
of diagnosis?




6                                                                                                             March 2026
Response to reviewers’ comments on revised manuscript                                                  Watson et al



   response 7
    We thank the reviewer for highlighting this issue. We have now undertaken a detailed case-by-case re-
    evaluation of all relevant diagnoses and treatment coding. This has clarified diagnostic classifications and
    treatment context, resolved instances of miscoding, and improved the precision of clinical annotations
    across the cohort.

    Re-evaluation of diagnostic coding. For every case we re-reviewed National Cancer Registry records, Of-
    fice for National Statistics (ONS) mortality records and Hospital Episode Statistics (HES/PEDW) entries. We
    examined the number of entries, the specialty of the admitting team, concordance across data sources and
    temporal patterns of coding. Potential miscoding was assessed by triangulating across sources. Diagnos-
    tic entries that appeared only once in HES/ PEDW and were discordant with other HES/PEDW episodes,
    cancer registry data, or death certificate information were treated as likely miscodes and excluded. This
    process identified that:
         • All apparent CML diagnoses (C92_045, C92_063) represented miscoding (a single HES entry, e.g.
           when admitted under a non-haematology inpatient team) and have been removed.
         • Several chemotherapy codes originally assumed to be pre-AML were in fact AML-directed treatment
           and have been reclassified. We now report only chemotherapy clearly associated with non-AML ma-
           lignancies prior to AML diagnosis, relevant to therapy-related AML context.

    Correction of AML case status. Importantly, this review identified three cases that were not AML (C92_029
    and C92_051 = acute lymphoblastic leukaemia (ALL); C92_088 = lymphoma). These cases and their
    matched controls have been removed, and all grouped analyses have been updated accordingly. We are
    grateful to the reviewer for prompting this correction.

    MDS/ CMML/ MPN diagnoses near AML. We recognise the reviewer’s concern regarding MDS, CMML or
    MPN diagnoses occurring close to, or after, AML diagnosis. After review:
      • Some cases show clear longitudinal coding of MDS/ MPN over multiple admissions, as well as
        National Cancer Registry confirmation, consistent with genuine antecedent myeloid disease (e.g.
        C92_022 had repeated MPN entires in HES starting 3 years prior to AML diagnosis, followed by
        splenomegaly and multiple myelofibrosis (MF) entries in the 3 months prior to AML diagnosis, sug-
        gesting progressive transformation of their MPN/ MF to AML).
      • In one case (C92_047), the death certificate specifically records ‘transforming MDS AML’, supporting
        biological continuity.
    More generally, registry dates reflect first recorded diagnosis, not biological onset. We therefore state in
    the revised manuscript that registry timing is not used to infer precise biological sequence but rather to
    indicate the first appearance of a diagnosis in national records.

    Ontogeny classification and interpretation. We acknowledge that registry-based ascertainment may not
    capture all antecedent diagnoses or treatments and, in the absence of contemporaneous blood count
    data, some individuals may have harboured biologically evolving but clinically unrecognised myeloid
    disease prior to AML diagnosis. These factors naturally limit the completeness and certainty of ontogeny
    classification (e.g. de novo versus secondary AML), although a subset of cases had clearly documented
    antecedent myeloid disease or prior cytotoxic therapy allowing confident assignment. Importantly
    registry-derived diagnoses were intended to provide clinical context rather than to define biological AML
    ontogeny, and were not used to inform the clonal or evolutionary analyses, which were based directly on
    serial genomic data. Incomplete ontogeny classification also reflects the fact that early myeloid disease
    may remain clinically silent, particularly in older adults in whom age-related clonal haematopoiesis is
    common. Accordingly, we now make it clear in the Discussion that the cohort studied reflects the spectrum
    of biological and clinical backgrounds typical of AML arising in older adults, and that the evolutionary
    dynamics identified represent processes operating across this real-world population rather than a single
    ontogeny-defined subgroup.

    Concurrent cancer diagnoses. All additional non-haematological malignancies that were noted around
    the time of AML diagnosis are recorded in the National Cancer Registry and listed as primary or con-
    tributing causes on death certification, supporting that these represent genuine diagnoses rather than
    miscoding. Their apparent temporal proximity to AML diagnosis likely reflects the timing of clinical
    detection during intensive evaluation rather than implying simultaneous biological onset.




March 2026                                                                                                         7
Response to reviewers’ comments on revised manuscript                                                             Watson et al



    Chemotherapy timing and sampling. We agree that we do not have samples between chemotherapy
    and AML diagnosis for these relevant individuals. We now explicitly state that additional clonal evolution
    following chemotherapy cannot be assessed and that our observations relate only to clonal architecture at
    the sampled pre-AML timepoints.

    Access to clinical records. We explored the possibility of direct medical record review with the UKC-
    TOCS governance team. This is not permitted under the trial’s ethical and governance framework (REC
    05/MRE02/17; Section 251 approval). Follow-up is conducted exclusively via linkage with national health
    registries, and acquisition of new data from NHS digital has formally closed. In addition, national SACT
    and CORE pathology datasets are only reliably complete from ∼2021 onwards, and so would not cover
    most cases in this cohort.

    Death certificate information. We now include cause of death and survival interval from AML diagnosis
    in Supplementary Table 10, as requested. Death registry data do not include date of cancer diagnosis.

    In response to the reviewer’s concerns, we have expanded the Methods and Discussion sections, substan-
    tially revised and restructured Supplementary Note 5 and added clinical annotations to Extended Data Fig.
    2. We show these changes below (text changes in Methods highlighted in blue). We have also updated Sup-
    plementary Table 10, and incorporated the cancer and chemotherapy annotations into the relevant clonal
    trajectory and Muller plots throughout the manuscript.


       updated text in Methods
       Information on any diagnosis of myelodysplastic syndrome (MDS; ICD-10 code D46 or C94.6), chronic
       myelomonocytic leukaemia (CMML; ICD-10 code C93.1) or myeloproliferative neoplasm (MPN; ICD-10 codes
       D45, D47.1, D47.3, D47.4, C92.1, C92.2, C94.4), or other haematological malignancy (for example, lymphoma
       or myeloma) was obtained for all pre-AML cases and controls via record linkage to the National Cancer Registry
       (England/ Wales), Hospital Episodes Statistics (HES) databases or Patient Episode Database for Wales (PEDW),
       the Office for National Statistics (ONS) mortality records and, for participants in Northern Ireland, the Central
       Services Agency and Northern Ireland Cancer Registry. Information on diagnoses of non-haematological ma-
       lignancies was also obtained via linkage to national cancer registries and, HES/PEDW databases and ONS
       mortality records. These clinical diagnoses were extracted to characterise antecedent myeloid disease, prior
       malignancies, and treatment exposures that provide clinical context relevant to secondary and therapy-related
       AML frameworks (see Supplementary Note 5 for detailed case annotations). Where a diagnosis appeared in
       more than one data source, the earliest recorded entry was taken as the date of diagnosis. Recorded diagnosis
       dates therefore reflect the first appearance of a diagnosis in national records and may not correspond to bio-
       logical disease onset. Potential miscoding was assessed by triangulating across available sources: diagnostic
       entries that appeared only once in HES/PEDW and were discordant with other HES/PEDW episodes, cancer-
       registry data, or death certificate information were treated as likely miscoded and excluded. Information on
       receipt of chemotherapy (ICD-10 codes Z51.1, Z51.2, Z92.6) or radiotherapy (ICD-10 codes Z51.0, Z92.3) prior
       to AML diagnosis (or, for controls, prior to the matched case’s age at AML diagnosis) was obtained from the
       HES databases and PEDW. For participants with a record of chemotherapy, the indication was determined by
       cross-refereing diagnostic codes in HES/PEDW and/or cancer-registry data. Chemotherapy codes temporally
       associated with AML diagnosis and lacking evidence of a prior malignancy were considered AML-directed
       and were not classified as pre-AML exposure. No pre-AML case or control was recorded as having received
       prior radiotherapy. Smoking information, where available, was obtained from UKCTOCS participant ques-
       tionnaires and HES databases (ICD-10 code Z72.0). The last updates from all linkage sources were received
       in 2020. As HES and PEDW only cover NHS-funded care in England and Wales it is possible that diagnoses
       or treatments delivered privately, abroad, or elsewhere in the UK may not have been captured. Supplemen-
       tary Table 12 summarises the timing of any recorded diagnosis of MDS, CMML, MPN, other haematological or
       non-haematological malignancy, as well as prior chemotherapy exposure and smoking history for all pre-AML
       cases and controls, with mortality outcomes (cause of death and survival interval) summarised for pre-AML
       cases only.

       New text in Discussion
        A clinical limitation of this study is that information on antecedent myeloid disease and prior chemotherapy
        was derived from registry linkage rather than direct clinical record review. Registry-based ascertainment may
        not capture all diagnoses or treatments and some individuals may have harboured evolving but unrecognised
        myeloid disease prior to AML diagnosis. These factors limit the completeness and certainty of ontogeny clas-
        sification (e.g. de novo versus secondary AML), although a subset of cases had clearly documented antecedent
        myeloid disease or prior cytotoxic therapy allowing confident classification in some cases (Supplementary
        Note 5). Importantly, incomplete ontogeny classification reflects not only limitations of clinical data capture
        but also the fact that early myeloid disease may remain clinically silent. This is well recognised in AML de-
        velopment, particularly in older adults in whom age-related clonal haematopoiesis is common. Accordingly,
        the cohort studied here reflects the spectrum of biological and clinical backgrounds typical of AML arising in
        older adults, and the evolutionary dynamics identified represent processes operating across this real-world
        population rather than a single ontogeny-defined subgroup.

Response to reviewers’ comments on revised manuscript                                                                                                                                                                                                                                            Watson et al




      Updated Supplementary Note 5


       5. Clinical background and treatment context of selected pre-AML cases
       A subset of pre-AML cases had additional clinically relevant history, including antecedent myeloid neoplasms,
       or other malignancies with or without documented chemotherapy exposure before AML diagnosis. These
       annotations capture settings in which AML can arise as secondary AML (arising after a prior myeloid disor-
       der), or as therapy-related AML (arising following cytotoxic therapy) and therefore provide relevant context
       for interpreting pre-AML clonal evolution. Clonal evolutionary trajectories for these individuals are shown
       in Supplementary Figs 58, 59 and 60). We also show here individuals who were diagnosed with non-myeloid
       malignancies around the time of AML diagnosis, or afterwards. Although these diagnoses do not inform the
       pre-AML evolutionary period, they are included for completeness as they may reflect broader cancer suscepti-
       bility. Clonal evolutionary trajectories for these individuals are shown in Supplementary Fig. 61. The numbers
       within each category are small and so this section is descriptive rather than intended to support formal com-
       parisons.


       Antecedent myeloid neoplasm (secondary AML context)
       Some individuals had a documented diagnosis of a myeloid neoplasm prior to AML, including myelodysplas-
       tic syndrome (MDS), chronic myelomonocytic leukaemia (CMML) and myeloproliferative neoplasms (MPN),
       such as essential thrombocytosis (ET) or myelofibrosis (MF). These cases are consistent with secondary AML,
       in which AML arises in the setting of a pre-existing clonal myeloid disorder. Our analysis did not identify a con-
       sistent association between specific evolutionary patterns and a preceding diagnosis of MDS, CMML or MPN.
       Although clonal sweeps might be anticipated in many such cases, we observed individuals who developed
       MDS, CMML or MPN without evidence of a detectable antecedent sweep. Several MDS and CMML diagnoses
       occurred close to, at the time of, or after AML diagnosis. In routine clinical practice, MDS/ CMML may be
       recognised only once AML has emerged or may be coded retrospectively, and registry timing does not always
       reflect the biological onset of disease. We therefore present these cases in the context of myeloid disease con-
       tinuity but do not infer precise temporal sequence from coding dates alone.

        Prior confirmed or possible chemotherapy exposure (therapy-related AML context)
        A number of individuals had documented exposure to chemotherapy prior to AML diagnosis, or had a prior
        malignancy for which treatment information was not recorded (i.e. they may have received chemotherapy)
        (Supplementary Fig 60). These cases align with clinical scenarios often described as therapy-related AML,
        where prior chemotherapy exposure may influence clonal selection. TP53 mutations are found in ∼30-40%
        of therapy-related myeloid neoplasms and are often detectable at very low frequency prior to chemotherapy,
        with chemotherapy thought to provide a selective pressure favouring their expansion103,104 . Consistent with
        this, two of the four individuals with documented prior chemotherapy exposure had TP53-mutant clones
        detectable prior to chemotherapy, compatible with selection of pre-existing clones occurring prior to AML
        diagnosis (Supplementary Fig 60a & c).




March 2026                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                           11
Response to reviewers’ comments on revised manuscript                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                   Watson et al




      Updated Supplementary Note 5 cont..
       Whilst the number of such cases is small, pre-AML clonal landscapes in individuals who had received
       prior chemotherapy (Supplementary Fig 60) appear relatively simple at the sampled timepoints (all prior to
       chemotherapy), with a small number of detectable clones and limited evidence of complex clonal architec-
       tures. Compared with many individuals who developed AML without chemotherapy exposure, these trajec-
       tories appeared less heterogeneous and less branched. These observations are consistent with the possibil-
       ity that AML arising after chemotherapy may, in some cases, arise from relatively modest pre-existing clonal
       haematopoiesis, in which chemotherapy may act as a selective pressure on pre-existing clones103 .

       Prior non-myeloid malignancy without recorded chemotherapy
       Several individuals had a prior diagnosis of non-myeloid malignancy (including both solid tumours and lym-
       phoid malignancies) for which treatment was limited to surgery or local management, with no chemotherapy
       recorded in the available datasets (Supplementary Fig 61a-f). These cases did not show an obvious clonal
       evolutionary pattern distinct from the broader pre-AML cohort.

       Updated Supplementary Note 5 cont..
        Non-myeloid malignancies diagnosed at or after AML diagnosis
        A small number of individuals were diagnosed with non-myeloid malignancies at or after the time of AML
        diagnosis (Supplementary Fig 61g-j). Although these events do not inform the pre-AML evolutionary period,
        they are noted here as they may reflect broader cancer susceptibility in certain individuals. No consistent evo-
        lutionary pattern was apparent in these cases. All of these malignancies were recorded on death certification
        as a primary or contributing cause, supporting the validity of diagnosis. Their apparent temporal proximity to
        AML likely reflects the timing of clinical detection during intensive evaluation rather than implying simulta-
        neous biological onset.

2. A final point here is that ext Table 10 also identifies two cases of CML, one simultaneous with AML diagnosis (045)
yet in this case no BCR::ABL was detected and there was a missing driver. The other case (063) had CML diagnosed
4 years after AML (very unusual) and no BCR::ABL or any other mutation detected. This raises major doubts with
regards to their pipeline for detecting chromosomal rearrangements. Again, medical record adjudication or some
other explanation for absence of BCR::ABL is required


March 2026                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                          13
Response to reviewers’ comments on revised manuscript                                                                                                           Watson et al



     response 8
     We thank the reviewer for raising this important point. We have specifically re-evaluated the two cases
     listed as having CML (C92_045 and C92_063) using National Cancer Registry data, ONS mortality records,
     and HEA entries, as described in response 7. This review revealed that both instances represented
     miscoding rather than true CML. In each case, the CML code appeared as a single HES entry and was
     not supported by concordant National Cancer Registry data, repeated specialist haematology coding or
     death certificate information. C92_045 had a single non-haematology HES entry around the time of AML
     diagnosis, whereas all other coding related to AML. C92_063 similarly had an isolated CML code recorded
     during admission under a non-haematology team, with multiple surrounding admissions coded as AML
     and no National Cancer Registry confirmation of CML.

     These entries therefore met our criteria for likely miscoding (isolated, discordant HES codes without reg-
     istry or mortality support) and have now been excluded. Supplementary Table 10 and the associated an-
     notations have been updated accordingly. As these were not genuine CML cases, the absence of BCR::ABL
     is consistent with the clinical data and does not indicate a failure of the rearrangement detection pipeline.



Other points:


The Horizon Myeloid Reference Standard is helpful for mutation detection sensitivity. The validation does show
that VAF quantification is quite noisy and inaccurate at this level. Does this not affect the model?



     response 9
     We thank the reviewer for this comment and we agree that accurate quantification of low VAFs is important
     for reliable growth-rate inferences. As shown in the updated Supplementary Fig. 14 (inset table) (shown
     below), absolute deviations in VAF remain small (MAE ≤ 0.3% VAF) down to ∼0.1% VAF, which we define as
     the lower limit of reliable quantification. Variability below this level reflects stochastic counting noise and
     was not relevant to our analyses, as no variants in the UKCTOCS samples were called <0.1% VAF. Impor-
     tantly, our maximum-likelihood model explicitly incorporates binomial sampling noise when comparing
     predicted and observed VAF trajectories (as shown below in highlighted excerpt from methods), so that
     measurement variability is captured within the likelihood function rather than treated as systematic error.
     Consequently, the observed level of technical noise does not bias the inferred fitness or establishment-time
     estimates.

       excerpt from Methods: Inferring mutation acquisition age and fitness effect.
        ...we developed a maximum likelihood approach to infer fitness and establishment time estimates for each
        clone in the population by comparing the predicted VAF trajectories to the observed VAF trajectories in all pre-
        AML cases and controls. In order to assign a likelihood to the observed data, given the predicted trajectories,
        we developed a likelihood function as follows: for each variant at each timepoint, we assigned a likelihood to
        the observed number of variant reads using a binomial probability with mean given by the predicted frequency
        and sample size given by the total depth at that position in the sample. The log(likelihoods) were summed
        across all the variant timepoints for all trajectories and our maximum likelihood approach minimised the sum
        of all the total log(likelihoods)




This does not address the issue relating to mCA calling and I do not find the simulations convincing, particularly
in light of the point above that apparently the BCR-ABL fusion gene was not detected in two patients with CML
in the study. This is not fully validated and forms an important part of their modelling. Please provide empirical
evidence or in-silico mixing (or both) for CN-LOH, gains, losses at 0.1–0.5% with sensitivity/precision and VAF
accuracy.

   response 10
    We thank the reviewer for this important point. As described in response 8, the two apparent CML cases
    (C92_045 and C92_063) have now been identified as miscoding in national health registries and so neither
    represents a true CML diagnosis. The absence of BCR::ABL in these individuals is therefore entirely
    expected. We also note that BCR::ABL, as a reciprocal chromosomal translocation rather than a mosaic
    chromosomal alterations (mCA), would not be detected by the mCA calling pipeline and so its absence
    does not speak to mCA caller performance in either case.

    We agree, however, that the mCA calling required more thorough validation, and, in response to the
    reviewer’s comments, we have substantially strengthened this aspect of the paper. The approach we use
    is unchanged in principle: unphased detection of mCAs at higher cell fractions, followed by longitudinal
    phasing of SNPs to enable detection of the same events at lower cell fractions at other timepoints. However,
    we have now implemented this as a substantially more robust and sophisticated multi-detector Python-
    based pipeline. First, five complementary detectors targeting different signatures of allelic imbalance and
    copy-number change are applied to identify mCAs at higher cell fractions (‘unphased mCA caller’), with
    candidate events subject to extensive quality control filtering before a final call set is produced. Haplotype
    phase information, derived from the BAF signal within the regions identified by the unphased caller, is
    then used by a second longitudinal ‘phased caller’ to detect the same events at lower cell fractions at
    other available timepoints. We have extensively validated the performance of both callers using in-silico
    mixing experiments to empirically characterise sensitivity, precision and cell fraction accuracy across all
    three mCA types (gain, loss, CN-LOH) at a range of cell fractions. This process has led us to adopt more
    conservative, empirically justified detection thresholds than those stated previously, as described below.

    In-silico simulation framework. Synthetic mCA events were spiked into real TETRIS-seq data from
    UKCTOCS control samples, encompassing a factorial design of 14 cell fractions (0.1% to 100%), three
    mCA types (gain, loss, CN-LOH), and 11 genomic geometries (interstitial and telomeric events at 2, 3,
    5, 10, and 20Mb, plus whole-arm). The mCA caller parameters were tuned exclusively on a training set,
    and all performance metrics were derived from an independent test set generated from different control
    individuals, ensuring that sensitivity and precision estimates were not inflated by overfitting.

    Unphased caller performance. For events ≥ 5Mb, overall sensitivity was 96.4% at 100% cell fraction, falling
    to 57.9% at 25% cell fraction, with the 50% sensitivity threshold at ∼20% cell fraction for CN-LOH and
    ∼25% for gains and losses. Precision exceeded 94% at cell fractions ≥50% and remained ≥79% wherever
    detection occurred, with a peak false-positive rate of only 0.13 per sample. Event type misclassification
    was rare: 6.2% of detected CN-LOH events were miscalled as loss (n = 38) or gain (n = 19), and all misclas-
    sifications were confined to cell fractions ≤25% cell fraction, reflecting the limited LRR signal available to
    distinguish event type at low cell fraction. No gain events were misclassified as loss or vice versa.

    Phased caller performance. When phasing was derived from a 75% cell fraction index sample, the 50%
    sensitivity threshold was ∼1% cell fraction for CN-LOH and 1.5% for gain and loss events: a 15-20 fold



March 2026                                                                                                                 15
Response to reviewers’ comments on revised manuscript                                                           Watson et al



     improvement over the unphased caller. Overall sensitivity reached 99% at ≥5% cell fraction, 89% at 2.5%
     cell fraction, 80% at 2% cell fraction, and 52% at 1% cell fraction.

     Sensitivity at 0.1-0.5% cell fraction and revised minimum detectable cell fraction. The reviewer specifi-
     cally requests performance data at 0.1-0.5% cell fraction. Our in-silico experiments show that at these very
     low cell fractions, sensitivity of the phased caller falls well below 10% for all three mCA types, reflecting the
     fundamental limit imposed by sampling noise at the available sequencing depth. At these very low cell
     fractions, BAF deviations are simply too small to consistently exceed the significance threshold of the one-
     sided t -test (P<0.05). We therefore adopt ∼1% cell fraction as a conservative, empirically justified lower
     limit for the phased caller. The previously stated 0.1% figure represented a theoretical minimum based on
     read-count considerations and is now superseded by this more rigorous empirically grounded estimate,
     albeit a more conservative one. No mCA calls below 1% cell fraction were included in our evolutionary
     models (e.g. for inferring establishment time and fitness of each clone). However, estimated cell fractions
     at timepoints that did not reach significance were used as upper bounds on cell fraction (using the upper
     95% confidence interval) ensuring that non-detections were treated as informative censored observations
     rather than simply excluded.

     Cell fraction accuracy. At cell fractions ≥5% , estimated cell fractions correlated strongly with true values
     across all three mCA types with no systematic bias. At cell fractions approaching the detection limit (∼1%
     cell fraction), cell fraction estimates are subject to upward bias, as only those samples in which noise
     inflates the BAF signal above the significance threshold will be called. In practice, however, only 2 of the 8
     somatic mCAs detected in our cohort had their earliest significant detection in the 1-2% cell fraction range
     (both in C92_022), and in this individual three further timepoints at substantially higher cell fraction were
     available to anchor the trajectory. In the remaining 6 individuals, the earliest significant detection of their
     mCA was at ≥4% cell fraction. Fitness and establishment times are estimated from the full longitudinal
     trajectory across multiple timepoints simultaneously, so any bias in individual low cell-fraction estimates
     has negligible influence on the final inferences.

     We have updated the Results and Methods section and relevant Supplementary sections to describe the
     revised mCA caller and its validation. These changes are shown below.


        updated text in Results: Deep sequencing of longitudinal blood preceding AML
         Using TETRIS-seq we reliably achieved a duplex consensus depth of >1500x, enabling robust detection of sin-
         gle nucleotide variants (SNVs) and indels at variant allele frequencies (VAFs) ≥0.1% (Supplementary note 2F).
         Using the longitudinal nature of our samples we were able to phase single nucleotide polymorphisms (SNPs)
         on sections of chromosomes affected by mCAs enabling detection of chromosomal gains, losses and copy-
         neutral loss of heterozygosity (CN-LOH) events ≥5Mb down to ∼1% cell fraction (Supplementary note 2K and
         2L). Together this approach enabled us to sensitively and precisely track the dynamics of a broad range of
         AML-associated genomic alterations in the decade preceding AML diagnosis.
