Response to reviewers’ comments on revised manuscript                                                                                                                                    Watson et al




       updated text in Methods: TETRIS-seq mCA and KMT2A-PTD calling
        mCAs were detected from SSCS reads using a custom Python-based caller, developed to exploit both
        deviations in heterozygous SNP ‘B-allele frequencies’ (BAF) and read depths (log2 R ratios) in the mCA/
        chromosomal rearrangement panel, as well as longitudinal haplotype phasing information derived from
        timepoints at which the same mCA was present at higher cell fraction (see Supplementary Note 2K). Loss
        and gain events result in deviations in both LRR and BAF whereas CN-LOH events result in BAF deviations
        without a change in LRR (because there is no change in the amount of genetic material) (Supplementary
        Fig. 25). LRR values were computed from read depths normalised to a panel of normals (PON) constructed
        from 36 control samples, using a leave-one-out strategy for samples within the PON. Five complementary
        detectors were run independently per chromosome, targeting different signatures of allelic imbalance and
        copy-number change: a BAF sliding-window detector (run at three window sizes to capture events across
        a range of sizes), an LRR-first detector, a homozygous-region detector for high cell fraction CN-LOH and
        loss events, a small-gains detector targeting trisomy-pattern BAF splits, and an arm-level detector for subtle
        whole-arm events. Candidate events were classified as gain, loss or CN-LOH by selecting the event type whose
        expected LRR best matched the observed LRR given the BAF-derived cell fraction estimate. Cell fraction
        was estimated from the heterozygous BAF peak separation using the formulae in Table S5. Calls from all
        five detectors were merged and deduplicated, and CN-LOH events detected at 100% cell fraction at both the
        earliest and latest available timepoints were classified as likely germline and excluded. Based on evaluation
        against an independent simulated test set, which demonstrated substantially reduced sensitivity for events
        <5 Mb, only events ≥5 Mb were retained for all downstream analyses (Supplementary Figs. 26-29).

        For individuals in whom a somatic mCA was detected, all available longitudinal samples were sequenced and a
        custom phased longitudinal caller was applied. Haplotype phase was derived from the ‘index sample’, defined
        as the timepoint closest to AML diagnosis for cases (or the matched timepoint for controls), with the exception
        of one case (C92_012) in whom the mCA was below the detection of the unphased caller at this timepoint, and
        so their preceding timepoint was used as the ‘index sample’ instead. Phase was assigned in the ‘index sample’
        by comparing each heterozygous SNP’s BAF relative to 0.5. These phase assignments were then applied to all
        other available timepoints from the same individual, and a one-sided one-sample t -test was used to assess
        whether the mean phased BAF deviation significantly exceeded zero (P < 0.05). Cell fraction was estimated
        from the mean phased deviation using event-type specific formulae (Table S5). On simulated test data, the
        phased caller achieved a 50% sensitivity detection threshold of approximately 1% cell fraction, compared with
        20-25% cell fraction for the unphased caller (Supplementary Figs. 26-29). Somatic mCA calls detected in the
        UKCTOCS pre-AML cases and controls can be found in Supplementary Table 8.


       updated text in Supplementary Note 2K: Detecting mosaic chromosomal alterations (mCAs):

        K. Detecting mosaic chromosomal alterations (mCAs)
        Mosaic chromosomal alterations (mCAs) each produce characteristic deviations in heterozygous SNP B-allele
        frequencies (BAF) and/ or read depths (log2 R ratios; LRR), which can be exploited for mCA detection (Sup-
        plementary Fig. 25). The expected BAF and LRR values for each event type, and the formulae used to derive
        cell fraction from the heterozygous BAF peak separation, are given in Table S5. mCA calling was performed on
        SSCS rather than DCS reads, as the higher depth afforded by SSCS improves power for detecting subtle allelic
        imbalance across many SNPs, while the per-base accuracy of duplex error correction is not required when
        signal is aggregated across tens to hundreds of SNPs per event.

       mCA detection was performed in two stages. First, an unphased caller was applied independently to each
       sample to identify mCAs in the samples closest to AML diagnosis for cases, or the matched timepoint for con-
       trols. Second, for individuals in whom a somatic mCA was detected, haplotype phasing was derived from the
       index sample and applied retrospectively to the other available timepoints from the same individual, enabling
       detection of the same mCA at much lower cell fractions. The unphased and phased callers are described in
       turn below.

       Calculating log2 R ratios (LRR) and B-allele frequencies (BAF). For each sample, the average read
       depth across each 120 bp targeted region (SNP region) was calculated and normalised to the average read
       depth across all targeted regions in the sample, producing ‘sample normalised read depths’ for each SNP
       region. To account for inter-region variation in read depth coverage, due to variability in capture efficiency,
       a panel of normals (PON) was constructed from 43 control samples sequenced across three sequencing
       libraries. To prevent samples harbouring significant copy-number alterations from contaminating the
       reference, the coefficient of variation (CV) of sample-normalised read depths was calculated per chromosome
       for each control and controls were excluded from the ‘panel of normals’ if their read depth CV for ≥ 1
       chromosome was more than 1.5 standard deviations above the mean CV. After filtering, 36 of 43 control
       samples were retained in the final PON reference matrix. For each sample to be analysed (including controls),
       PON-normalised read-depth ratios were calculated using a leave-one-out strategy: if the sample itself was a
       member of the PON, it was excluded from the reference set for that normalisation, thereby avoiding circular
       normalisation. The PON-normalised depth for each targeted region was calculated as the sample’s read depth
       divided by the mean depth across all remaining PON controls for that region. LRR values were then computed
       as log2 (PON-normalised depth).

       The variant allele frequency (VAF) of all targeted SNPs, as well as any SNPs with > 1% minor allele frequency in
       1000 Genomes, was calculated from the read counts (alternative reads / total depth) and used as the B-allele
       frequency (BAF). For each sample, per-SNP BAF and per-bin LRR values were merged by genomic coordinate
       and SNPs (with BAF 0.02-0.98) were used to compute allelic imbalance (AI = 0.5 - min(BAF, 1-BAF)) at each lo-
       cus, yielding 0 for balanced heterozygotes and approaching 0.5 for complete allelic imbalance. The per-probe
       coefficient of variation (CV), computed across PON controls, was used to identify probes with unreliable depth
       estimates. Probes whose CV exceeded three scaled median absolution deviations (MAD) above the median CV
       were considered outliers; their LRR values were set to NaN to prevent noisy depth estimates from influencing
       copy-number detection while the SNP was retained for BAF-based allelic imbalance analysis. This is justified
       because BAF is a within-sample ratio (alternative reads / total reads) unaffected by the between-sample cap-
       ture variability that drives high probe_CV, whereas LRR depends directly on comparison to the PON mean and
       is therefore unreliable when that mean is poorly estimated.


       TETRIS-seq mCA caller: unphased single-timepoint detection
       The TETRIS-seq mCA caller incorporates five complementary detectors run independently per chromosome,
       each designed to be sensitive to a different signature of allelic imbalance or copy-number change that might
       be missed by any single approach alone (Table S6).

        Detector 1: BAF alllelic-imbalance (AI) sliding-window detector. For each chromosome, heterozy-
        gous SNPs were split into p-arm and q-arm subsets. Within each arm, a rolling window of 15 heterozygous
        SNPs (stepping every 3rd SNP) computed the mean AI and the fraction of SNPs supporting allelic imbalance.
        The window AI track was scanned sequentially to identify contiguous intervals of allelic imbalance: a candi-
        date event was opened when the window mean AI exceeded an ‘entry threshold’ (0.03), with at least 25% of
        SNPs in the window supporting allelic imbalance, and was closed when AI fell below an ‘exit threshold’ (0.03)
        for 3 consecutive windows.

        The rough boundaries of the possible mCA were then refined in three stages. First, the PELT (Pruned Exact
        Linear Time) changepoint algorithm (ruptures library in Python) segmented per-SNP AI values in the region
        surrounding each candidate event and segments whose median AI exceeded local background were classified
        as part of the event region. Second, for events with strong LRR signal (|corrected LRR| > 0.20), boundaries were
        moved to the position of the steepest LRR transition. Third, a two-stage edge-trimming procedure removed
        flanking regions where consecutive SNPs fell below an adaptive AI cutoff, with safeguards to prevent excessive
        trimming of large events.

        Because low cell fraction events spread across many SNPs can be missed by a 15-SNP window, the BAF AI
        detector was re-run was run at two additional larger window sizes: 40 heterozygous SNPs and 80 heterozygous
        SNPs, to capture events spanning ≥8Mb and ≥15Mb respectively.

        Detector 2: LRR-first detector with BAF confirmation. While Detector 1 starts from BAF and uses LRR
        to refine, Detector 2 first identifies regions of abnornal read depth and then checks whether BAF confirms
        allele imbalance. LRR values were smoothes across neighbouring SNPs and compared to a per-chromosome
        baseline. Because a large mCA can itself distort the chromosome baseline, this was estimated iteratively after
        excluding candidate mCA regions. When the baseline was contaminated by a large event (extreme baseline
        LRR or fewer than 30% of bins neutral), a genome-wide baseline was used instead. Contiguous stretches of
        elevated or depressed LRR spanning ≥ 3Mb were identified as candidate events. Each candidate event was
        then required to have concordant BAF evidence: at least 70% of heterozygous SNPs in the region had to show
        allelic imbalance, and the cell fraction estimates derived independently from LRR and BAF had to agree within
        0.20.

        Detector 3: Homozygous-region detectors. At high cell fractions, CN-LOH and loss events can elimi-
        nate nearly all heterozygosity across a region, making these events identifiable even without measuring the
        degree of BAF deviation, as the absence of heterozygous SNPs is itself the signal. Two variants of this detector
        scanned for such regions at different resolutions: a large-event version targeted events ≥10Mb and a small-
        event version capturing events down to 3Mb. Both used sliding windows to identify stretches with very low
        heterozygous SNP density, then extended boundaries outward until normal heterozygous SNPs reappeared.
        Regions with heterogenous LRR were split, as these likely represent adjacent but distinct events. The small-
        event version included an additional check to prevent false calls in probe-sparse centromeric regions, where
        low heterozygous SNP density reflects poor coverage rather than a true event. In both versions, classification
        was based on LRR, with regions with substantially depressed LRR classified as losses and those with LRR near
        zero classified as CN-LOH.




March 2026                                                                                                                            19
Response to reviewers’ comments on revised manuscript                                                             Watson et al




      updated text in Supplementary Note 2K: Detecting mosaic chromosomal alterations (mCAs) cont..
       Detector 4: Small-gains detector. This detector targeted trisomy-pattern gains at ≳30% cell fraction,
       where BAF splits into a characteristic pattern of approximately 0.33 and 0.67 rather than the standard
       heterozygous deviation around 0.5 that is seen in lower-cell-fraction gains detected by Detector 1. Sliding
       windows of 2Mb (stepping every 1Mb) identified regions where SNPs shifted into this trisomy BAF range
       coincided with elevated LRR (median > 0.15). An adaptive noise floor derived from unaffected regions of the
       same chromosome was used to distinguish true signal from background variation. Final calls required that
       cell fraction estimates derived independently from LRR and BAF agreed within 0.20.

       Detector 5: Arm-level detector. Detectors 1-4 all use sliding windows, which can miss events that span
       an entire chromosome arm, because there is no contrast between adjacent windows to trigger detection.
       This detector instead compared each arm as a whole against two references: the opposite arm of the same
       chromosome and a genome-wide background derived from all other chromosomes. Arms with statistically
       elevated allelic imbalance relative to both references were flagged as candidate events. As with the other
       detectors, classification used LRR contrast to distinguish CN-LOG (no change in LRR) from gains (elevated
       LRR) and losses (depressed LRR). Boundaries were refined by walking inward from arm ends to identify where
       the signal began. To avoid double-counting, this detector was retained only for regions not already captured
       by the windowed detector.

       mCA event classification. Once a candidate mCA event was identified, it needed to be classified as gain,
       loss or CN-LOH. For each candidate event type, the cell fraction was first estimated from the BAF peak sep-
       aration, using the median of the lower (µ1 ) and upper (µ2 ) BAF peaks among heterozygous SNPs (Table ??).
       The expected LRR, given the cell fraction calculated from the BAF, was then compared to the observed LRR.
       Before comparison, the observed LRR was corrected for per-chromosome drift by subtracting the median LRR
       of SNPs outside the event region, or a genome-wide baseline when the event spanned a large fraction of the
       chromosome. The event type that minimised the squared standardised residual between the observed and
       expected LRR was selected:
                                                        LRRobs − LRRexp (e) 2
                                                      µ                    ¶
                                               S(e) =
                                                              σLRR
       where σLRR was estimated from the MAD of LRR values on the same chromosome outside called intervals.

       Quality-control filters. After classification, each candidate mCA event from the BAF AI detector (the pri-
       mary detector and source of the majority of calls), was subjected to seven quality-control filters and events had
       to pass all filters to be retained. The other detectors applied their own internal quality checks, as described
       above. The BAF AI quality-control filters targeted distinct failure modes:
           • Filter A (minimum BAF peak separation): the observed ∆ was required to exceed a dynamic thresh-
              old that decreased with the square root of the number of supporting heterozygous SNPs, preventing
              noise fluctuations in small regions from producing spurious calls. The threshold was relaxed when LRR
              independently confirmed the event direction.
           • Filter B (minimum cell fraction): events with BAF-derived cell fraction < 0.12 were rejected, corre-
             sponding to the approximate limit of reliable allelic imbalance detection.
           • Filter C (LRR contrast): for gain and loss events, the median LRR of the event region was required to
             differ from the chromosome background by a minimum amount, preventing CN-LOH events with mild
             LRR drift from being misclassified as gains or losses.
           • Filter D (chaos filter): events with low mean AI but high AI variance were rejected, eliminating regions
             where a few outlier SNPs drove the signal without a consistent underlying event.
           • Filter E (peak-to-median ratio): events with very low median AI but high 95th-percentile AI were re-
             jected, targeting spike-driven false positives.
           • Filter F (AI contrast vs. background): the AI within the event region was required to be statistically and
             substantively elevated relative to the same-chromosome background. For events occupying >25% of
             the chromosome, a genome-wide comparison was additionally applied.
           • Filter G (BAF–LRR agreement): events with poor agreement between observed and expected LRR (clas-
             sification fit score > 4.0) were rejected.
       CN-LOH events were subjected to additional filters including an adaptive minimum heterozygous SNP count
       that scaled with event span and inversely with BAF signal strength, a minimum signal-to-noise ratio, a sym-
       metry check requiring both upper and lower BAF peaks to be populated, and a minimum number of valid LRR
       probes within the region.

       updated text in Supplementary Note 2K: Detecting mosaic chromosomal alterations (mCAs) cont..
        Algorithmic parameters. Key algorithmic parameters and their default values are shown in Table S7. All
        parameters were optimised on a training set of simulated samples (Box 4) and held constant for all analyses
        reported in this study.

                        Table S7: Key algorithmic parameters used within the TETRIS-seq mCA caller.

        Parameter                   Value                         Description

        het_lo, het_hi              0.02, 0.98                    BAF range for heterozygous SNP classification
        window_hets                 15                            Number of het SNPs per sliding window (primary pass)
        step_hets                   3                             Step size in het SNPs
        θ_enter, θ_exit             0.03                          AI entry/exit thresholds for state machine
        min_span_mb                 3                             Minimum event span (Mb)
        min_consecutive_hets        5                             Minimum consecutive supporting hets
        min_spanning_hets           3.0                           Minimum distance (Mb) between first and last supporting het
        gap_mb                      8                             Maximum gap between consecutive supporting hets (Mb)
        min_supporting_fraction     0.25                          Minimum fraction of hets supporting the event
        loss_lrr                    -0.12                         LRR threshold for loss classification
        gain_lrr                    0.12                          LRR threshold for gain classification
        neutral_lrr                 0.04                          Maximum |LRR| for CN-LOH classification
        min_cf_baf                  0.12                          Minimum BAF-derived cell fraction
        EXIT_PERSISTENCE            3                             Number of below-threshold windows to exit a call
        CPD pen_multiplier          3.0                           PELT changepoint penalty multiplier
        Multi-scale windows         40, 80                        Additional window sizes for large-event sensitivity
        Genome baseline fallback    |LRR| >0.20 or <30% neutral   Triggers genome-wide LRR baseline
        Extension signal check      AI ≥0.04, ≥5 hets             Required to adopt wider boundary in dedup


        Merging, deduplication, and final mCA call set. Because multiple detectors can identify the same
        event, calls from all five detectors were combined and deduplicated. Before merging, calls near chromoso-
        mal ends were evaluated and if there were few normal heterozygous SNPs between a call boundary and the
        last probe on the arm, the boundary was extended to the arm end, reflecting the likelihood that the event ex-
        tended to the telomere. Adjacent calls of the same mCA event type separated by a short gap (<2Mb, or <5Mb
        with consistent LRR across the gap) were merged. When overlapping calls from different detectors agreed on
        event type, the boundary was set using a signal-aware strategy: each extension zone was tested for AI signal,
        and the wider boundary was adopted only where signal was present. When overlapping calls disagreed on
        event type, the event type was arbitrated by median LRR evidence (the most direct indicator of copy number
        change) and the intersection of boundaries was taken. Only events with MEDIUM or HIGH confidence were
        retained.


        Sensitivity and specificity of TETRIS-seq unphased mCA caller
        The evaluate the performance of the TETRIS-seq mCA caller, a set of simulated samples with known ground-
        truth mCA events was generated (Box 4). Algorithmic parameters (Table S7) were developed and optimised
        using a training set of simulated samples, but a test set of independently simulated samples, generated from
        different control individuals, was used for performance evaluation. This separation ensured that sensitivity
        and specificity estimates were not inflated by overfitting to the training data.

        A detected mCA was considered a true positive if it matched the ground-truth event on chromosome, event
        type, and genomic location with 50% overlap. Detected events that partially overlapped the truth region
        (≥20% reciprocal overlap) but differed in event type or were substantially mis-sized were classified as partial
        matches. Detected events on chromosomes with no simulated event were classified as false positives.
        Simulated events with no detected call were classified as false negatives.

        Sensitivity was strongly dependent on event size: events of 2-3Mb were largely below the detection limit, with
        sensitivity only 3.8% for 2Mb events and 45.7% for 3Mb events even at 100% cell fraction (Supplementary
        Fig 26a.). Subsequent performance evaluation was therefore restricted to events ≥5Mb (n = 561), within the
        effective resolution of the targeted panel. Overall sensitivity was 96.4% at 100% cell fraction, falling to 57.9% at
        25% cell fraction (Supplementary Fig. 26b). CN-LOH events were detectable at lower cell fractions than gains
        or losses, consistent with their larger BAF deviation at equivalent cell fraction: the 50% sensitivity threshold
        was reached at ∼20% cell fraction for CN-LOH versus ∼25% for both gain and loss (Supplementary Fig. 26c).
        Event geometry also influenced detectability, with whole-arm events achieving 58% sensitivity at 15% cell
        fraction compared with 5Mb interstitial and telomeric events reaching equivalent performance at ≥ 25% cell
        fraction (Supplementary Fig. 26d).

       Supplementary Fig. 26: Performance of TETRIS-seq unphased mCA caller on simulated test data. Sensitivity
       and precision were evaluated on a test set of simulated samples with known ground-truth mCA events (Box 4). a.
       Sensitivity as a function of cell fraction stratified by event size, demonstrating that events ≥5Mb fall within the effective
       detection range of the panel. b. Overall sensitivity versus cell fraction for events ≥5Mb (n = 561). c. Sensitivity by
       event type (CN-LOH, gain, loss) for events ≥5Mb d. Sensitivity by event geometry for events ≥5Mb. e. Precision
       (positive predictive value, left axis) and false positive rate per sample (right axis) for events ≥5Mb as a function of
       cell fraction. f. Sensitivity heatmap stratified by chromosome and cell fraction (≥10% cell fraction shown) for events
       ≥5Mb; values indicate percentage of simulated events detected.


       Precision was high, exceeding 94% at cell fractions of 50% and above and remaining ≥79% wherever detection
       occurred, with a peak false-positive rate of 0.13 per sample at 25% cell fraction (the cell fraction at which the
       mCA caller is most active relative to its discriminatory power) (Supplemetary Fig. 26e). Type misclassification
       was rare and directional: 6.2% of detected CN-LOH events were miscalled as loss (n = 38) or gain (n = 19),
       and all misclassifications were confined to cell fractions ≤25%, reflecting the difficulty of resolving event type
       by LRR when the copy-number deviation is small. Crucially, no gain events were misclassified as loss or vice
       versa. Inter-chromosomal variation in sensitivity was modest at cell fractions ≥50% but more pronounced at
       15-25% cell fraction, likely reflecting differences in per-chromosome probe density within the targeted panel
       (Supplementary Fig. 26f).


       TETRIS-seq mCA caller: phased longitudinal detection
       Because mCA calling relies on the ability to detect deviations in heterozygous BAFs, the noisier the BAF mea-
       surements (e.g. due to low sequencing read depth), the harder it will be to detect subtle BAF deviations asso-
       ciated with low cell fraction mCAs. Haplotype phasing, which involves identifying SNPs which lie on the same
       chromosome and therefore have the same direction of BAF deviation, can be used to improve the sensitivity
       of low cell fraction mCA calling. This approach has been used to call mCAs at cell fractions as low as 1% in UK
       Biobank participants97,98 by utilising long-range phase information generated from long identical-by-descent
       (IBD) tracts shared among distantly related individuals99 . Such population-scale phasing is not available for
       the UKCTOCs participants. However, one of the benefits afforded by having longitudinal samples is the ability
       to use large BAF deviations from higher-cell fraction mCAs, detected at timepoints closer to AML diagnosis, to
       identify which SNPs lie on the same chromosome in the affected region of interest. This phasing information
       can then be applied to the same individual’s samples from earlier timepoints, enabling much higher sensitivity
       for detection of the mCA when it is at lower cell fraction (Supplementary Fig. 27).

        Supplementary Fig, 27: Phasing SNPs for detection of low cell fraction mCAs. mCAs detected at high enough cell
        fraction to allow differentiation of the heterozygous SNPs that have deviated above and below 0.5 BAF enable SNPs
        to be ‘phased’ (e.g. in the final timepoint ‘sample’ shown on the left). This phasing information can then be applied
        to earlier timepoints (shown on the right) and provides higher sensitivity for detection of smaller BAF deviations
        and therefore higher sensitivity for detection of mCAs at lower cell fraction. Data generated for schematic using a
        simulated sample (100 SNPs per region with mean read depth across ‘panel’ of 1000 reads).


        The unphased mCA calling framework above was applied to the sample closest to the time of AML diagnosis
        for each case, or the matched timepoint for controls, plus typically at least one earlier timepoint. For indi-
        viduals in whom a somatic mCA was detected at any timepoint (and not suspected to be of germline origin),
        all remaining available longitudinal samples from that individual were sequenced and SNP variant data gen-
        erated, enabling application of the phased caller across the full longitudinal series. Haplotype phasing was
        derived from whichever timepoint showed the highest cell fraction estimate (the ‘index’ sample), and applied
        to all other timepoints from that individual.

        Deriving haplotype phase from the index sample. Within each mCA region, haplotype phase was as-
        signed to every SNP in the index sample by comparing its BAF to 0.5. SNPs with BAF above 0.5 were assigned
        phase = 1 (ALT allele on the amplified or retained haplotype), whilst those below were assigned phase = 0. A
        minimum median allelic imbalance (AI) of 0.01 in the index region was required to accept the phasing.

        Phased cell fraction estimation at earlier timepoints. The phase assignments derived from the index
        sample were then applied to all other available timepoints from the same individual. At each timepoint, SNPs
        within the mCA region that had been assigned a phase in the index sample were identified and filtered for
        residual allelic signal. For CN-LOH and loss events a three-pass adaptive filter was used. First, an initial filter
        of BAF ∈ [0.2, 0.8] was applied. If the density of SNPs with VAFs in this range was <0.5 SNPs/Mb or fewer
        than 3 SNPs were recovered, the filter was widened to BAF ∈ [0.05, 0.95]. If this was still insufficient, it was
        widened further to BAF ∈ [0.001, 0.999], excluding exact germline homozygotes at VAF = 0 or 1. This adaptive
        approach ensures that high-cell-fraction events, where originally heterozygous SNPs may be pushed toward
        homozygosity, are not missed by an overly conservative heterozygosity filter. For gain events, a fixed filter of
        BAF ∈ [0.2, 0.8] was used, as gain events shift BAFs away from 0.5 but do not push SNPs toward homozygosity.
        For each retained SNP, the phased deviation was computed as:

                                                                                     phased deviation = (2 × phase − 1) × (BAF − 0.5)

        where phase ∈ {0, 1} is the haplotype assignment from the index sample.

        This formula produces a positive value when a SNP’s BAF is shifted in the direction predicted by its phase as-
        signment, and a negative value when it shifts the other way. Under a true mCA, the shifts are coherent (i.e.
        most SNPs are pushed in the same direction), so the mean phased deviation is consistently positive. In the
        absence of an mCA, phased deviations are symmetrically distributed around zero, as noise-driven BAF shifts
        are equally likely to align with or oppose the phase assignment. This formula produces a positive value when
        a SNP’s BAF is shifted in the direction predicted by its phase assignment, and a negative value when it shifts
        the other way. Under a true mCA, the shifts are coherent (most SNPs are pushed in the same direction), so
        the mean phased deviation is consistently positive. In the absence of an mCA, phased deviations are sym-
        metrically distributed around zero, as noise-driven BAF shifts are equally likely to align with or oppose the
        phase assignment.A one-sided one-sample t -test was used to assess whether the mean phased deviation sig-
        nificantly exceeded zero (P < 0.05; minimum 3 phased SNPs required). Cell fraction was estimated from the
        mean phased deviation using the event-type-specific formulae in Table S5.

      updated text in Supplementary Note 2K: Detecting mosaic chromosomal alterations (mCAs) cont..
       Performance of the phased longitudinal caller. Performance was evaluated on the same simulated
       test set used for the unphased caller (see Box 4). Each simulated mCA exists as a "family" of samples in which
       the same event was introduced into the same control at cell fraction ranging from 0.1% to 100%. To mimic a
       longitudinal study design, the highest cell-fraction sample served as the index timepoint from which phasing
       was derived, and this phasing was then applied to detect the event in all lower cell fraction samples from the
       same family. Performance was assessed separately for phasing derived from index samples at 100%, 75%, 50%
       and 25% cell fraction.

       The phased caller markedly improved sensitivity at low cell fractions compared to the unphased caller
       (Supplementary Fig. 28). When phasing was derived from a 75% cell fraction index sample (n = 517 events
       ≥5 Mb), overall sensitivity reached 99% at cell fractions ≥5% and remained high at very low cell fractions:
       89% at 2.5%, 80% at 2% and 52% at 1% (Supplementary Fig. 28a). The 50% sensitivity detection was 1% for
       CN-LOH and 1.5% for gain and loss events; an improvement of approximately 15-20-fold compared to the
       unphased mCA caller (Supplementary Fig. 28b). Whole-arm events achieved near-perfect detection even at
       very low cell fractions, while 5 Mb events showed the lowest sensitivity (Supplementary Fig. 28c). Sensitivity
       was similar across mCA geometries (Supplementary Fig. 28d) and chromosomes (Supplementary Fig. 28f),
       though chromosomes 21 and 22 showed somewhat lower sensitivity, likely reflecting their smaller size and
       lower SNP density. Cell fraction estimates were well-calibrated above 1% true cell fraction but showed upward
       bias at very low cell fractions (Supplementary Fig. 28e), consistent with detection at low cell fraction being
       enriched for events with unusually large BAF deviations. The gain in sensitivity attributable to phasing was
       substantial across all event types, particularly below 5% cell fractions (Supplementary Fig. 28g). The quality
       of the index timepoint sample affected phased detection sensitivity primarily at the very lowest target cell
       fractions (Supplementary Fig. 28h). At target cell fractions ≥5%, all index cell fractions tested (25-100%)
       yielded ≥96% sensitivity, indicating that phasing is robust even when derived from moderate cell fraction
       events. At target cell fractions of 1-2.5%, sensitivity was modestly higher when phasing was derived from a 25%
       cell fraction reference than from a higher one (100%). This likely reflects a selection effect: events detected at
       the index timepoint at only 25% cell fraction are necessarily those with the strongest allelic imbalance signal
       (typically larger events with more phased SNPs), which makes them inherently easier to detect at low target
       cell fractions. Lower index sample cell fractions therefore enrich for events with favourable characteristics.

       False positive rate of the phased longitudinal mCA caller. To estimate the false positive rate (FPR), a
       within-family null design was used. For each simulated family in which the mCA was detected in the index
       sample (tested separately at index cell fractions of 100%, 75%, 50% and 25%), phasing was derived from that
       index sample and applied to the original unmodified control SNP file for the same individual (i.e. 0% cell frac-
       tion). Any significant detection in this null sample was recorded as a false positive. This design directly mirrors
       our UKCTOCS longitudinal sample analysis approach, i.e. phasing from a high cell fraction timepoint, then
       testing whether the same event is detectable in an earlier timepoint from the same individual. This approach
       ensures that any false positives arise from phasing noise rather than cross-individual BAF differences. Overall
       FPR across all reference cell fractions was 6.4% (128/2017 null test), modestly above the nominal α = 5% level
       and consistent with the slight non-uniformity of the p-value distribution under the null (Supplementary Fig.
       29a). FPR decreased with increasing index cell fraction: 8.3% at 25%, 6.7% at 50%, 6.2% at 75%, and 5.2% at
       100% (Supplementary Fig. 29b), approaching the nominal α = 5% level when phasing was derived from a high
       cell fraction index sample. FPR was lowest for gain events (4.2%) and highest for loss events (8.5%) (Supple-
       mentary Fig. 29c), consistent with loss events producing a weaker allelic imbalance signal per SNP at a given
       cell fraction, resulting in less confident phase assignments and greater susceptibility to noise-driven false de-
       tection. FPR was also higher for larger events (10% FPR for whole arm events), than for small ones (<2% FPR
       for 5Mb events) (Supplementary Fig. 29d), likely reflecting the greater opportunity for accumulated phasing
       noise across longer regions.

        Supplementary Fig. 28: False positive rate of the TETRIS-seq longitudinal phased mCA caller on simulated
        null data. The false positive rate (FPR) was estimated using a within-family null design: phasing was derived from
        the index sample at a given cell fraction and applied to the original unmodified control sample from that individual
        (0% cell fraction). Any significant detection in this null sample was recorded as a false positive. Significance was
        assessed by one-sided t-test at α = 0.05. Distribution of one-sided p-values across all null tests (n = 2017). Under
        the null hypothesis, p-values should be approximately uniform; the modest excess near zero is consistent with the
        overall FPR slightly exceeding the nominal 5% level. b. FPR stratified by index sample cell fraction (25%, 50%, 75%,
        100%). Numbers indicate false positives per total tests. c. FPR stratified by mCA type (CN-LOH, gain, loss). d. FPR
        stratified by mCA size; 2 Mb and 3 Mb events are excluded as these fall below the effective detection range of the
        unphased caller.

Response to reviewers’ comments on revised manuscript                                                                                                       Watson et al




      updated text in Supplementary Note 2K: Detecting mosaic chromosomal alterations (mCAs) cont..

          Box 4: Generating simulated samples containing mosaic chromosomal rearrangements (mCAs).
          To evaluate the sensitivity of the mCA calling pipeline across a range of biologically relevant pa-
          rameters, a custom python script was written to generate simulated mCAs by computationally
          spiking synthetic events into real sequencing data from UKCTOCS control samples. The simulation
          encompassed a factorial design of 14 cell fractions (0.1%, 0.5%, 1%, 1.5%, 2%, 2.5%, 5%, 10%, 15%,
          20%, 25%, 50%, 75%, and 100%), three mCA types (gain, loss, CN-LOH), and 11 genomic geometries
          (interstitial and telomeric events at 2, 3, 5, 10, and 20Mb, plus whole chromosome arm). These events
          were applied to two independent panels of nine control samples each: a training set for parameter
          tuning and a test set for performance evaluation.

          Control sample selection and training/ test split. Control samples were drawn from a panel of
          33 samples sequenced across three sequencing libraries (SLX_19285, SLX_20125, SLX_20127). For
          each library, two quality metrics were calculated for every candidate sample: (i) mean sequencing
          depth across all panel regions, and (ii) coefficient of variation (CV) of the sample’s PON-normalised
          read-depth ratios. Samples were ranked by their proximity to the library’s median depth and median
          CV. The top three samples per library were assigned to the training set and the next three to the test
          set, yielding nine training and nine test control samples across the three libraries (Supplementary Fig.
          30). The mCA caller was tuned exclusively on the training set, but the sensitivity and specificity of the
          mCA caller was assessed using the test set.

                                              SLX_19285
                                              SLX_20125
                                      0.350   SLX_20127

                                              Test Set

          Supplementary Fig. 30: Selection of control samples for mCA simulation. Scatter plot of mean sequenc-
          ing depth versus coefficient of variation (CV) of PON-normalised read-depth ratios for 33 control samples
          across three sequencing libraries (SLX_19285, SLX_20125, SLX_20127). For each library, samples were
          ranked by their proximity to the library’s median depth and median CV. The three closest samples per library
          were assigned to the training set (black circles) and the next three to the test set (grey circles), yielding 9
          training and 9 test samples.

          Chromosome assignment and mCA placement. For each control sample, three chromosomes were
          selected for simulation from three size-based groups: large (chromosomes 1-7), medium (chromo-
          somes 8-15), and small (chromosomes 16-22, X). One chromosome was drawn at random from each
          chromosome size group, avoiding chromosomes harbouring pre-existing mCA events in that sample.
          Chromosome reuse was minimised across samples where possible, to ensure broad genomic coverage
          of the simulation. Chromosome arms with fewer than 50 probes in the targeted panel were excluded
          from whole-arm simulations; this excluded the p-arms of chromosomes 13, 14, 15, 21 and 22 (which
          are acrocentric and have minimal unique sequence), yielding 41 of 46 arms eligible for simulation.
          For each chromosome, events were placed at 11 genomic geometries: interstitial and telomeric seg-
          ments at five lengths (2, 3, 5, 10 and 20Mb), plus a whole-arm event. Interstitial events were placed
          at a random position within the chromosome arm, with a 500kb buffer from arm boundaries. Whole-
          arm events spanned the first and last probe positions within the arm. All placements were verified to
          containing a minimum number of probes (at least 5, or 2 per Mb, whichever was greater), and events
          failing this criterion were discarded.




26                                                                                                                                                            March 2026
Response to reviewers’ comments on revised manuscript                                                                 Watson et al




       updated text in Supplementary Note 2K: Detecting mosaic chromosomal alterations (mCAs) cont..

             Generation of simulated mCAs. For each SNP within the simulated mCA region, new read counts
             were drawn stochastically to mimic sequencing-associated sampling noise. Heterozygous SNPs
             (defined as those with 0.2 < VAF < 0.8 in the unmodified control sample) were identified, and each was
             randomly assigned with equal probability to one of two haplotypes. This haplotype assignment was
             generated once per unique event (defined by the combination of chromosome, geometry and mCA
             type) and kept constant across all 14 mCA simulated cell-fraction levels, ensuring that the haplotype
             was fixed irrespective of clone size and thus enabling the differing cell fractions to mimic longitudinal
             samples harbouring the same mCA at different stages of clonal expansion.

             The expected total depth (d ) and VAF for each SNP, were determined by the event type and cell frac-
             tion ( f ), as shown in Table S8. Given these expected values, the simulated total depth at each SNP was
             drawn from a Poisson distribution (with a minimum depth of 1), and the simulated variant read count
             from a Binomial distribution parameterised by the simulated total depth and expected VAF. For gain
             and loss events, PON-normalised read-depth values in genomic bins overlapping the event region
             were multiplied by the appropriate copy-number scaling factor (1 + f /2 for gains; 1 − f /2 for losses)
             and a small random factor (normally distributed with mean 1 and standard deviation 0.02) to simu-
             late measurement variability; the LRR was then recomputed as log2 (normalised depth). Homozygous
             SNPs within the simulated mCA region were also rescaled in total depth by these factors, while pre-
             serving their original VAF, to maintain consistency in the read-depth signal. For CN-LOH events, no
             depth or LRR adjustment was applied, as CN-LOH does not alter copy number.


             Table S8: Expected total depth and heterozygous VAFs for simulated mCAs. d denotes the observed
             total read depth at a given SNP in the unmodified control sample; f denotes the simulated cell fraction.
             Haplotype 1 is the amplified (gain), retained (loss), or duplicated (CN-LOH) haplotype; haplotype 0 is the
             other haplotype.

                Somatic mCA     Expected total depth    Expected VAF (haplotype = 1)   Expected VAF (haplotype = 0)

                Gain                 d × (1 + f /2)            (1 + f )/(2 + f )                 1/(2 + f )
                Loss                 d × (1 − f /2)            (1 − f )/(2 − f )                 1/(2 − f )
                CN-LOH                     d                      (1 + f )/2                     (1 − f )/2



             Simulation output. Each simulation produced a modified SNP file and a modified PON-normalised
             read-depth file, retaining the original file format so that simulated samples could be processed
             through the mCA calling pipeline without modification. A truth manifest was generated recording
             the sample, mCA type, cell fraction, chromosome, coordinates, geometry, and random seed for ev-
             ery simulation, enabling systematic benchmarking of caller performance. The complete simulation
             yielded ∼12,000 individual simulated datasets per set (9 × 3 × 11 × 3 × 14, minus events discarded for
             insufficient probe coverage), for a total of ∼24,000 simulated samples across the training and test sets
             combined.




       New Supplementary Note 2L: Somatic mCAs detected in UKCTOCS samples...


        L. Somatic mCAs detected in UKCTOCS samples with TETRIS-seq mCA caller
        Supplementary Figs. 31-38 show the longitudinal mCA detection plots for all cases and controls in whom a
        somatic mCA was detected by the TETRIS-seq mCA caller. For each individual, timepoints are ordered chrono-
        logically from top (closest to AML diagnosis for cases, or matched timepoint for controls) to bottom (earliest
        available sample). The unphased single-timepoint caller was applied to the final available timepoint in all in-
        dividuals, with the exception of one case (C92_012) in whom the mCA cell fraction was below the detection
        threshold of the unphased caller at the final timepoint; in this case the unphased caller was applied to the
        penultimate timepoint instead. The timepoint at which the unphased caller was applied is referred to as the
        ‘index’ sample. Haplotype phase was derived from the index sample and applied to all remaining timepoints
        from the same individual using the longitudinal phased caller.

Why is median fitness in single hit clones in controls (12%) different to behaviour of single hit clones in pre-AML
(20%) the authors argue that single hit clones destined for later AML have a greater clonal advantage. Is this con-
text dependent selection not evidence of a possible role for extrinsic factors influencing clonal behaviour? Just a
comment required here.

   response 11
    On the observation that single-hit clones in pre-AML cases have higher median fitness (20%) than those
    in controls (12%), we believe this is more likely due by statistical conditioning on the outcome rather than
    context-dependent selection. Our logic is as follows:

    AML is a rare event that typically requires the acquisition of multiple driver mutations. The probability of
    a clone acquiring a second driver mutation is directly proportional to its population size (the number of
    target cells available for the second hit). Consequently, clones with higher fitness expand more rapidly,
    create a larger target population, and are therefore statistically much more likely to acquire subsequent
    drivers and progress to AML within a human lifespan.

    When we look retrospectively at patients who did develop AML (the pre-AML group), we are effectively
    selecting for trajectories that successfully “made the cut”. This introduces a survival bias towards higher
    fitness values in the initial driver clone. In contrast, the control group represents the unbiased distribution
    of fitness effects, which includes many lower-fitness clones (e.g., 12%) that never expanded sufficiently to
    acquire the necessary secondary hits for transformation.

    We observe this exact effect in our simulations of leukaemogenesis. Pre-AML single-mutant clones are
    approximately twice as fit as the single-mutant clones in controls, despite there being no context depen-
    dent selection present in these simulations. Therefore, while ruling out context dependent selection is not
    possible, we do know that statistical conditioning leads to higher fitness in pre-AML single-hit clones.



March 2026                                                                                                                                                                                                                                       35
Response to reviewers’ comments on revised manuscript                                                                 Watson et al



There is no evidence that HSCs numbers remain constant in pre-AML pts with multiple clones. This seems like a
major assumption without evidence to back it up. The number of cells with stem cell properties might well increase
many fold in the pre-AML phase. An additional added complication that differentiation block might occur with
multiple mutations, affecting VAF in mature neutrophils. Again, this required additional comment rather than
experimentation.

     response 12
     We thank the reviewer for this important point. We agree that assumptions regarding constant HSC pop-
     ulation size and differentiation are simplifications and it is important to highlight this.We have added the
     following statement to the discussion to explicitly caveat this assumption:

        updated text in Discussion
        We acknowledge that our model assumes a constant effective HSC population size, and that biological factors
        such as pre-leukaemic stem cell expansion or differentiation blocks could influence the relationship between
        variant allele frequency and true clonal burden. Consequently, our fitness estimates should be interpreted as
        the effective competitive advantage relative to the background population.




New data:

With regards to the new data includes, for the cell type deconvolution analysis I am unclear how many samples
were analysed 23 pre-AML and 23 control? Elsewhere in the methods it states 96 and 88. Are these technical
duplicates? Please clarify.

     response 13
     In total, whole-genome bisulphite libraries were prepared for 96 UKCTOCS samples, originating from 23
     pre-AML cases and 23 controls. Some individuals contibuted more than one longitudinal sample, result-
     ing in 96 samples overall. Of these, 88 samples met the minimum coverage threshold for inclusion in the
     cell-type deconvulation analysis (≥ 286 of 333 CpGs covered). We have revised the text in Methods and
     Supplementary Note 1 (shown below) to make the sample numbers clearer.

        updated text in Methods
        Cell type deconvolution. To estimate the proportions of different blood cell types (B-cells, NK-cells, CD4 T-
        cells, CD8 T-cells, monocytes, neutrophils and eosinophils) present in the samples, we used the R package
        EpiDISH with the reference dataset “centDHSbloodDMC.m”61 . Whole genome bisulphite (WGBS) libraries
        were prepared for 96 UKCTOCS samples from 23 pre-AML cases and 23 controls, some of whom contributed
        more than one longitudinal timepoint. Of these 96 samples, 88 met the minimum coverage criterion (≥ 286 of
        333 CpGs covered) and were included in the deconvolution analysis (average depth ∼7X). See Supplementary
        Note 1 and Supplementary Fig. 1.


        updated text in Supplementary Note 1: UKCTOCS cell type deconvolution

Evolutionary dynamics in the years preceding acute myeloid leukaemia:
Response to reviewers’ comments on revised manuscript
We thank the editor and reviewers for their constructive comments and questions. We address reviewer 3’s re-
maining concerns below. Reviewer comments are shown in plain text and our responses in grey boxes. Relevant
manuscript text is shown in outlined boxes, with text added in this revision highlighted in blue.


Reviewer 3
“Apologies once again for the delay with my review.

Thanks to the authors for presenting their thoughts and revisions so clearly.

The revisions substantially improve the validation of the pipeline for CNA and SV noting the significant changes
made to the claimed sensitivity, which seems to have no impact on their model. I think technically the paper is
improved.

My fundamental concerns remains the same:

1. The lack of clinical ground truth at AML diagnosis is a major concern. I understand the explanation that it
is not possible to access the clinical data and/or samples at AML diagnosis directly - but this is a problem and,
in addition, the revision history raises significant concerns. At the outset, cases with a prior diagnosis of haem
malignancy were missed. There are 17/47 cases in the cohort with concurrent haem malignancy. This has not
impacted the model. New cases of ALL or misclassified CML have been identified - again, no impact on the model.
There are many AML cases with no identified driver and/or one mutation e.g. in ASXL1 (ext fig 5e) or DNMT3A
(ext data fig 4e and 6d) which one does not see in clinical practice. Cases remain with concurrent DLBCL and
NHL (pretty unusual), MDS diagnosis very close to AML diagnosis and other odd clinical scenarios. What about
other myeloid malignancies in the cohort without AML? One would expect at least equal numbers of MDS and
MPN. No targeted rearrangements were detected in the assayed pre-diagnostic serum samples. The study cannot
conclude that fusion-defined AML was absent in the eventual leukemias, nor can it confidently infer the timing
of such lesions without paired diagnostic material. In my view, there is a well justified major concern about the
veracity of the clinical data and therefore any conclusions about population dynamics of AML. This should be very
clearly highlighted in the main text.

    response 1
    We agree that the absence of diagnostic material and direct clinical record review is an important limita-
    tion, and, as the reviewer requests, we have made this explicit in the main text. The limitations paragraph
    of the Discussion (reproduced below) states that clinical information was derived from registry linkage
    rather than clinical record review, that registry-based ascertainment may not capture all diagnoses or
    treatments, and that some individuals may have harboured unrecognised myeloid disease before AML
    diagnosis. In this revision we have added that registry-derived diagnoses provided clinical context only
    and did not inform the evolutionary analyses, and that clonal selection following chemotherapy could
    not be assessed. It also states that we cannot be certain the clones detected pre-diagnosis gave rise to
    the AML, and that the final transforming event (including NPM1/FLT3 mutations and chromosomal
    rearrangements) may in some cases occur after our last blood sample. We have also changed the title and
    abstract (see response to point 2 below).

    AML diagnosis. There is no doubt about the AML diagnosis itself in any of the 47 cases. Every case is
    supported by independent national data sources (National Cancer Registry, death certificates and Hospital
    Episode Statistics). All cases except two appear in at least two of these sources, and those two cases have
    multiple AML entries within their hospital records.

    Antecedent and concurrent haematological disease. Of the 47 pre-AML cases, 14 (30%) have a recorded
    antecedent or concurrent myeloid neoplasm (MDS, CMML or MPN); the remaining 3 of the 17 cases noted
    by the reviewer had a lymphoid malignancy (DLBCL, NHL or myeloma). The proportion with antecedent
    myeloid disease is consistent with published rates of secondary AML in older adults: 30% in an unselected
    sequential AML cohort (Lindsley et al., Blood 2015; 125:1367–1376) and 25% of patients aged 70–79 years in
    the population-based Swedish Acute Leukemia Registry (Hulegårdh et al., Am J Hematol 2015; 90:208–214).
    We believe the clinical and mutational spectrum we observe reflects AML as it arises in an unselected
    population of older women, rather than the case mix of a haematology referral series, and that several



October 2026                                                                                                      1
Response to reviewers’ comments on revised manuscript                                                              Watson et al



    of the unusual scenarios highlighted by the reviewer reflect this population-based design. The clinical
    annotations were curated case by case across the cancer registry, hospital coding and death certificate
    data, as described in the Methods and Supplementary Note 5.

    Myeloid malignancies without AML. This is a nested case–control study: controls were age- and
    timepoint-matched UKCTOCS participants who remained free of blood cancer, and none of the 46
    controls has a recorded haematological malignancy (Supplementary Table 10). The study was therefore
    not designed to estimate the incidence of MDS or MPN in the wider UKCTOCS cohort.

    Pre-diagnostic mutation spectrum. Pre-diagnostic samples capture founding events rather than the full
    mutation spectrum present at diagnosis. This is consistent with both the single-mutant clones high-
    lighted by the reviewer (early clones may carry one driver and acquire additional hits later) and the scarcity
    of NPM1/FLT3 mutations and fusions. We agree that the absence of targeted rearrangements in pre-
    diagnostic samples does not show that fusion-defined AML was absent at diagnosis, and the Discussion
    states that these events may occur after our last blood sample. The dynamics of the pre-AML clones are
    clearly distinguishable from those in age-matched controls, even if further drivers were acquired after our
    last sample, and the paper’s conclusions rest on these pre-AML evolutionary dynamics rather than on the
    mutational composition of the diagnostic AML.

       Text in Discussion
        A limitation of our study is that we do not have direct access to diagnostic material, meaning we cannot be
        certain that the clones detectable in the pre-AML samples actually gave rise to the AML. Furthermore, in some
        cases it is likely that the final transforming event occurs after our last blood sample. Indeed, there are inter-
        esting differences between the spectrum of mutations we find in pre-AML and those typically seen at AML
        diagnosis, e.g. in patients aged ≥60 years, NPM1 and FLT3 mutations are found in 25-35% of cases52 , and the
        chromosomal rearrangements targeted in our study are found in ∼10%53 , yet both are rare in our cohort. Their
        absence in our samples strongly suggests they are late-occurring events. Approximately 20% of pre-AML cases
        in our cohort display either no clonal expansions or only rapid expansions limited to the 1-2 years prior to di-
        agnosis. Evidence from deep targeted methylation sequencing54 strongly suggests 50% (5/10) of these cases do
        contain a pre-existing clonal expansion (e.g. C92_048, C92_052, C92_072), whilst others do not (e.g. C92_055,
        C92_063). Two of these expansion-positive cases (C92_048 and C92_052) carried germline DDX41 variants.
        A clinical limitation of this study is that information on antecedent myeloid disease and prior chemother-
        apy was derived from registry linkage rather than direct clinical record review. Registry-based ascertainment
        may not capture all diagnoses or treatments and some individuals may have harboured unrecognised myeloid
        disease before AML diagnosis. These factors limit the completeness and certainty of ontogeny classification
        (e.g. de novo versus secondary AML), although a subset of cases had clearly documented antecedent myeloid
        disease or prior cytotoxic therapy allowing confident classification (Supplementary Note 5). Registry-derived
        diagnoses provided clinical context only and did not inform the evolutionary analyses. Clonal selection fol-
        lowing chemotherapy could not be assessed, as no samples were available between chemotherapy and AML
        diagnosis. Importantly, incomplete ontogeny classification reflects not only limitations of clinical data capture
        but also the fact that early myeloid disease may remain clinically silent, particularly in older adults in whom
        age-related clonal haematopoiesis is common. Accordingly, our cohort reflects the spectrum of biological and
        clinical backgrounds typical of AML arising in older adults, and the evolutionary dynamics identified represent
        processes operating across this real-world population rather than a single ontogeny-defined subgroup.



2. The degree to which the modelling conclusions are actually constrained by the (revised) data is unclear. Very
significant revisions have not had any material impact on the model. A number of examples show fitness is in-
ferred from extremely limited data e.g. ext Fig 9d (with many other examples). The abstract still implies annual
sampling as early as 15 years but the reality is the sampling is quite sparse and usually over a much shorter period.
I have carefully read the arguments made by the authors but I still do not think the paper justifies the stronger
conclusion that extrinsic factors are unimportant or only second-order. At most, they have shown that a simple
intrinsic-fitness model can fit many pre-AML trajectories reasonably well. That is not a surpise and could have
been modelled without these data. It is not the same as demonstrating that models incorporating strong extrinsic
selection factors might also be consistent with equally well-fitting models. I believe this aspect of the discussion
needs to be toned down much more as could be quite misleading for the field. The different fitness of single hit
clones in pre-AML versus controls speaks to this point. The authors argue that "statistical conditioning on the out-
come" is the explanation for increased fitness of single-hit pre-AML clones but it seems entirely plausible, or even
likely, that extrinsic pressures in certain individuals alter the clonal fitness of single hit clones, making progression
to AML more likely due to increase population size



2                                                                                                                    October 2026
Response to reviewers’ comments on revised manuscript                                                            Watson et al



    response 2
    Sampling and the abstract. We agree, and have changed the title and abstract to give a more accurate
    picture of the sampling. “Decades” has been replaced with “years” in the title, and the abstract now de-
    scribes samples “collected annually for up to a decade and beginning as early as 15 years prior to diagnosis”.

    Constraint of the model by the data. We respectfully suggest that the stability of the model across
    revisions reflects the stability of the data it is fitted to, rather than a lack of constraint. The revisions
    improved variant calling, the robustness of mCA and structural variant calls, and case classification, but
    none of these changes materially altered the observed longitudinal trajectories to which the model is
    fitted. Where fitness is inferred from few timepoints, the uncertainty is quantified: for every clone we
    report the 95% optimiser range and standard deviation of the fitness and establishment time estimates
    (Methods, Supplementary Note 4 and Supplementary Table 9), so that estimates based on limited data can
    be identified.

    Extrinsic factors. We agree that our data cannot exclude a role for extrinsic factors, and we do not claim
    that they are unimportant. Our claim is narrower: a model in which clones have an intrinsic fitness is
    sufficient to explain the observed trajectories. We agree that this does not rule out models with strong
    extrinsic selection that might fit equally well. Likewise, our simulations show that conditioning on a rare
    outcome is sufficient to produce the higher apparent fitness of single-hit clones in pre-AML cases than in
    controls (Extended Data Fig. 10a–d), but this does not exclude a contribution from extrinsic pressures in
    some individuals.

        Text in Abstract
        Here we exploit a unique collection of serial blood samples, collected annually for up to a decade and be-
        ginning as early as 15 years prior to diagnosis of acute myeloid leukaemia (AML), to provide a quantitative
        description of precancerous evolutionary dynamics.


        Text in Results: Quantitative driver mutation dynamics
        Overall these findings demonstrate that, once established in the haematopoietic stem cell (HSC) pool, the
        dynamics of pre-leukaemic clones are surprisingly predictable. Whilst we cannot exclude extrinsic effects al-
        tering some clones, one does not need to invoke more complex scenarios to explain the data: one can achieve
        remarkably close fits to variant trajectories by assuming clones have an intrinsic fitness and compete globally
        for one resource with minimal spatial structure.



Overall, the paper's real novelty is narrower than the manuscript sometimes suggests. It is already well established
that somatic mutations can precede AML by years, that CH clones show gene- and combination-specific longitudi-
nal fitness differences, and that aging hematopoiesis becomes progressively oligoclonal under positive selection.
What this paper adds is powerful serial pre-diagnostic sampling and the attempt to fit explicit clone trajectories
over time. That is meaningful, but many of the conclusions drawn already had substantial support before this
work. The conclusion that extrinsic factors are second order cannot be reliably concluded from these data.
I believe there is not a great deal more experimentation the authors can do, they have done a lot of work to address
my comments as best they can.

    response 3
    We thank the reviewer for recognising the value of the serial pre-diagnostic sampling and the clone
    trajectories. We respectfully suggest that the advance is broader than this. Existing longitudinal CH studies
    characterise clonal dynamics in unselected ageing populations. None has resolved how these dynamics
    unfold in individuals destined to develop AML, at the depth we present: serial samples spanning up to a
    decade, individual-clone fitness inference, and quantitative reconstruction of clonal architecture across
    the pre-AML trajectory. To our knowledge, no comparable picture exists for any human cancer, and we
    believe this represents a substantial advance.

    Together with the technical improvements Reviewer 3 acknowledges, we believe the paper’s central claims
    are well supported.
